GP & Hematology Question Sets

General Pathology Question Bank + Item Sets 21–30 | Answers from Arif's Pathology & Haematology (15th ed., 2025)

GENERAL PATHOLOGY — Cell Adaptation · Cell Injury · Cell Death
CELL ADAPTATION
1 Define cellular adaptation. What are the types of adaptation? Give examples of each type.

Definition: Adaptations are reversible changes in the size, number, phenotype, metabolic activity or function of cells in response to changes in their environment (physiologic states such as pregnancy, or some pathologic stimuli), during which new but altered steady states are achieved, allowing the cell to survive and continue to function.

Types and examples:

TypeChangeExamples
HypertrophyIncrease in size of cellsLeft ventricle in hypertension; uterus in pregnancy; skeletal muscle in exercise
HyperplasiaIncrease in number of cellsBreast at puberty and in pregnancy; endometrium; benign prostatic hyperplasia
AtrophyDecrease in size and metabolic activity of cellsSenile brain atrophy; disuse atrophy of immobilized limb; uterus after parturition
MetaplasiaChange in phenotype of cellsSquamous metaplasia of bronchus in smokers; Barrett esophagus
High yield: All four adaptations are REVERSIBLE — remember them as Bigger (hypertrophy), More (hyperplasia), Smaller (atrophy), Different (metaplasia). Malignant transformation can arise only in hyperplasia and metaplasia.

Ref: V1-ch02

2 Define hyperplasia. Describe the mechanism of hyperplasia. How can hyperplasia act as fertile soil of malignancy?

Definition: Hyperplasia is an increase in the number of cells in an organ or tissue in response to a stimulus. It occurs in cells capable of mitotic division (labile or stable cells), e.g. glandular epithelium of endometrium, breast and prostate.

Mechanism: Hyperplasia results from growth factor-driven proliferation of mature cells and, in some cases, from increased output of new cells from tissue stem cells:

  1. Increased local production of growth factors or hormonal stimulation.
  2. Production of transcription factors.
  3. Transcription of various cellular genes.
  4. Increase in the number of cells, then increase in tissue mass.

Hyperplasia as fertile soil of malignancy: Pathologic hyperplasia is a non-neoplastic but abnormally sustained proliferation driven by excess hormonal or growth stimuli. If additional oncogenic mutations accumulate in these actively dividing cells, the hyperplastic epithelium may progress through dysplasia to carcinoma — e.g. patients with endometrial hyperplasia are at increased risk of endometrial cancer.

Ref: V1-ch02

3 What are the types of hyperplasia? How can hyperplasia be a fertile soil of neoplasia?

Types of hyperplasia:

  1. Physiologic hyperplasia — (a) Hormonal: glandular proliferation of female breast at puberty and during pregnancy; hyperplasia of the pregnant uterus. (b) Compensatory: growth of remaining tissue after partial loss, e.g. after partial hepatectomy or unilateral nephrectomy (liver regeneration).
  2. Pathologic hyperplasia — endometrial hyperplasia (abnormal menstrual bleeding), benign prostatic hyperplasia, skin warts due to papillomaviruses.

How it becomes neoplasia: Pathologic hyperplasia is a fertile soil for cancerous proliferation because the cells are already in a sustained proliferative state under hormonal or viral drive; acquisition of further genetic damage converts it to dysplasia and then to cancer (e.g. endometrial hyperplasia to endometrial carcinoma).

FeatureHyperplasiaNeoplasia
NatureAdaptation, coordinatedUncoordinated, abnormal proliferation
CauseExcess of normal physiologic stimulus (hormone)Spontaneous or pathologic stimulus (carcinogen)
CoursesUsually regress (reversible)Proceeds, not reversible
Pleomorphism / atypical mitosesAbsentPresent (bizarre mitoses)
EffectNot fatalFatal in cancer

Ref: V1-ch02

4 Define hypertrophy. Describe the mechanism of hypertrophy.

Definition: Hypertrophy is an increase in the size of cells that results in an increase in the size of the affected organ. The hypertrophied organ has no new cells, just larger cells, so it occurs mainly in non-dividing (permanent) cells such as cardiac and skeletal muscle.

Stimuli: Increased functional demand and specific hormonal stimulation.

Mechanism:

  1. Mechanical work (pressure or volume overload) or trophic signals (e.g. activation of beta-adrenergic receptors) stimulate the myocytes.
  2. Increased cellular protein synthesis enables assembly of additional sarcomeres, so the cell enlarges.
  3. Pattern reflects the stimulus: pressure overload adds sarcomeres in parallel (concentric increase in wall thickness), while volume overload adds them in series (ventricular dilatation).

Ref: V1-ch02

5 What are the types of hypertrophy? How does left ventricular hypertrophy occur in hypertension?

Types of hypertrophy:

  1. Physiologic — skeletal muscle hypertrophy due to exercise (body builders); hypertrophy of uterus during pregnancy; hypertrophy of breast during lactation due to prolactin and estrogen.
  2. Pathologic — hypertrophy of cardiac muscle due to increased workload (e.g. hypertension, aortic stenosis).

How left ventricular hypertrophy (LVH) occurs in hypertension:

  1. Chronic systemic hypertension causes pressure overload of the left ventricle, increasing cardiac work, wall stress and stretch of myocytes.
  2. Cardiac muscle cells are permanent (non-dividing), so hyperplasia is impossible; the myocytes respond by increasing protein synthesis and assembling additional sarcomeres in parallel.
  3. This produces concentric increase in wall thickness — LVH. If untreated, complications are ischemic heart disease, heart failure, arrhythmia, stroke and sudden cardiac death.

Ref: V1-ch02

6 Define atrophy. Describe the mechanism of atrophy.

Definition: Atrophy is a reduction in the size of an organ or tissue due to a decrease in cell size and number. The cells shrink and the organ becomes smaller (e.g. senile atrophy of brain and heart).

Mechanism: Atrophy results from decreased protein synthesis and increased protein degradation.

  1. Decreased protein synthesis: reduced trophic signals (e.g. from growth receptors) decrease nutrient uptake and mRNA translation.
  2. Increased protein degradation — (a) lysosomal (autophagic) pathway: autophagic vacuoles containing mitochondria, ER etc. fuse with lysosomes whose acid hydrolases (cathepsins) digest them; (b) ubiquitin-proteasome pathway, e.g. in cancer cachexia.
  3. Undigested residue persists as lipofuscin — brown atrophy of the heart in chronic starvation.

Ref: V1-ch02

7 What are the causes of atrophy? How does cell number decrease in atrophy?

Causes of atrophy:

  1. Decreased workload (disuse atrophy) — muscle wasting in bed rest or plaster cast immobilization.
  2. Loss of innervation (denervation atrophy) — damage to supplying nerves.
  3. Diminished blood supply — senile atrophy of brain and heart from cerebral atherosclerosis/ischemia.
  4. Inadequate nutrition — marasmus, chronic inflammation, cancer cachexia.
  5. Loss of endocrine stimulation — post-menopausal atrophy of endometrium, vaginal epithelium and breast.
  6. Pressure atrophy — compression by an enlarging benign tumor.

Types: Physiologic (fetal notochord/thyroglossal duct atrophy; uterus after parturition) and pathologic (localized: denervation, disuse, pressure; generalized: starvation, senile).

How cell number decreases: The dominant change is shrinkage of individual cells; the number falls when chronically atrophied cells are lost — reduced renewal by division of stable cells together with increased cell removal by apoptosis — so the organ loses both cell size and cell mass.

Ref: V1-ch02

8 Define metaplasia. Describe the mechanism of metaplasia.

Definition: Metaplasia is a reversible change in which one differentiated cell type (epithelial or mesenchymal) is replaced by another cell type. (Note: the correct word is "replaced" — not converted or transformed.) Like all adaptations it is reversible.

Mechanism:

  1. Metaplasia is the result of either reprogramming of stem cells present in the local tissue, or colonization by differentiated cell populations from adjacent sites.
  2. The process is mediated by signals generated by cytokines, growth factors and extracellular matrix components in the cells' environment.
  3. These signals induce specific transcription factors that bring about differentiation of stem cells into a particular lineage (e.g. squamous instead of columnar epithelium).

Ref: V1-ch02

9 Classify metaplasia with examples. How can metaplasia be harmful?

Classification with examples:

  1. Epithelial metaplasia — (a) Squamous metaplasia (columnar to squamous — the commonest): respiratory tract ciliated epithelium in chronic smokers; salivary gland, pancreatic and bile ducts due to stones; vitamin A deficiency. (b) Columnar metaplasia (squamous to columnar): Barrett esophagus, where refluxed gastric acid replaces squamous epithelium by intestinal-like columnar cells.
  2. Connective tissue (mesenchymal) metaplasia — formation of cartilage, bone or adipose in tissues that do not normally contain them, e.g. bone formation in muscle (myositis ossificans) after fracture, fibroblast converted to osteoblast.

How it is harmful:

  • Alteration of normal physiological function — in respiratory squamous metaplasia the protective mucus secretion is lost.
  • Malignant transformation — the same influences may induce carcinoma in the metaplastic epithelium (e.g. squamous cell carcinoma of the lung); metaplasia may also progress to dysplasia and cancer (Barrett esophagus to adenocarcinoma).

Ref: V1-ch02

10 How does metaplasia occur in the respiratory tract of a chronic smoker? What may be the consequence?

Mechanism in the respiratory tract:

  1. Smoking (also chronic bronchitis and vitamin A deficiency) causes chronic irritation of the bronchial epithelium.
  2. This activates key regulatory proteins and transcription factors driven by cytokine, growth factor and matrix signals.
  3. The normal ciliated columnar (pseudostratified) epithelium is replaced by tougher stratified squamous epithelium — squamous metaplasia occurs.

Consequences:

  • Loss of a protective mechanism — mucus secretion and mucociliary clearance are lost, so infections are more frequent.
  • The metaplastic squamous epithelium is exposed to the same carcinogenic influence and may undergo malignant transformation — hence squamous cell carcinoma is the common form of respiratory tract cancer.

Ref: V1-ch02

11 Metaplasia is a two-edged sword; justify.

Justification: Metaplasia produces a benefit and a harm at the same time, hence it is called a double-edged sword.

Advantage (merit): It allows survival of cells in an adverse environment. In the commonest epithelial metaplasia (columnar to squamous), the more rugged stratified squamous epithelium survives circumstances in which the fragile specialized columnar epithelium would have succumbed — e.g. chronic smoking, ductal stones, vitamin A deficiency. Its purpose is protective.

Disadvantage (demerit):

  1. Alteration of normal physiological function — respiratory squamous metaplasia loses mucus secretion, a key protective mechanism.
  2. Malignant transformation — persisting causative influences may induce cancer in the metaplastic epithelium, e.g. squamous cell carcinoma of the respiratory tract, or adenocarcinoma in Barrett esophagus.

Ref: V1-ch02

12 Define autophagy. Shortly describe the mechanism. How does it help the body in survival?

Definition: Autophagy ("self-eating") is the lysosomal digestion of the cell's own components. It is a subcellular response to injury involved in the removal of damaged organelles.

Mechanism:

  1. Fragments of the cell's own organelles (mitochondria, ER) destined for destruction are enclosed in membrane-bound autophagic vacuoles.
  2. Lysosomes discharge their hydrolytic contents (acid hydrolases such as cathepsins) into these vacuoles.
  3. The sequestered cytoplasmic material is digested and the usable products (amino acids, fatty acids) are recycled.
  4. Undigested residue persists as residual bodies — e.g. lipofuscin granules producing brown atrophy of the heart.

Role in survival: It clears damaged organelles and misfolded protein aggregates that would otherwise trigger apoptosis or necrosis, maintains organelle quality control and energy homeostasis during starvation by recycling raw materials, and so lets the cell continue functioning during stress.

Ref: V1-ch01, V1-ch02

13 How is the cell cycle maintained? How can growth factors stimulate cell proliferation?

Maintenance of the cell cycle: The cycle proceeds G1 to S to G2 to M through the sequential action of cyclins bound to cyclin-dependent kinases (CDKs); periodic synthesis and degradation of cyclins drives each transition.

  1. Restriction point in late G1: mitogen-activated cyclin D-CDK4/6 phosphorylates Rb, releasing E2F which switches on S-phase genes.
  2. DNA damage checkpoints (G1/S, intra-S, G2/M) and spindle checkpoint halt the cycle for repair, mediated by p53 and CDK inhibitors (p21, p27, p16).
  3. Normal cells also obey density-dependent inhibition, anchorage dependence and growth factor requirement, which keep proliferation in check.

Growth factor stimulation: Growth factors bind receptor tyrosine kinases and activate RAS-MAPK and PI3K-AKT signalling, increasing cyclin D synthesis so CDK4/6-driven Rb phosphorylation and E2F release push the cell into S phase. In tissues this appears as hyperplasia, which is the result of growth factor-driven proliferation of mature cells and increased output from tissue stem cells.

Ref: V1-ch02

14 Define stem cell. Mention its types and functions. What are the peculiarities of stem cells?

Definition: Stem cells are undifferentiated primitive cells that retain the capacity both to self-renew and to differentiate into specialized cell lineages.

Types:

  1. Embryonic stem cells — totipotent/pluripotent (inner cell mass), can form all body tissues.
  2. Adult (somatic) stem cells — multipotent or unipotent, e.g. haematopoietic stem cells, mesenchymal stem cells, intestinal crypt and skin basal cells.
  3. Induced pluripotent stem (iPS) cells — somatic cells reprogrammed to a pluripotent state.

Functions: Maintain tissue homeostasis and turnover, regenerate tissue after injury, act as the reserve pool from which new cells are produced (including the stem cells that are reprogrammed in metaplasia and the increased stem-cell output in hyperplasia), and serve as targets for cell therapy.

Peculiarities: long-lived and often quiescent; undergo asymmetric division (one daughter remains a stem cell, the other differentiates); high telomerase activity and sustained self-renewal; reside in protective niches; retain an immature phenotype with broad developmental potential.

Ref: V1-ch02

15 What are the mechanisms of cellular aging? Why is malignancy common in older age?

Mechanisms of cellular aging: Cellular aging is a progressive decline in cellular function and viability caused by genetic abnormalities and accumulation of cellular and molecular damage. It results from a combination of:

  1. Accumulation of DNA damage (e.g. by free radicals, carcinogen exposure, sporadic errors).
  2. Reduced capacity of the cell to divide (replicative senescence).
  3. Defective protein homeostasis (accumulation of abnormally folded proteins).
  4. Dysregulated nutrient sensing.

Morphology of aged cells: accumulation of lipofuscin and advanced glycation end products (AGEs), pleomorphic vacuolated mitochondria, distorted Golgi and reduced endoplasmic reticulum.

Why malignancy is common in old age: Decades of accumulated, incompletely repaired DNA mutations and telomere shortening increase genomic instability; immunosenescence reduces surveillance of transformed cells; chronic low-grade inflammation and defective protein and organelle quality control allow damaged clones to escape apoptosis and expand — so age is one of the strongest independent risk factors for cancer.

Ref: V1-ch02

CELL INJURY
16 Define cell injury. Mention the causes of cell injury with examples.

Definition: If the limits of adaptive responses are exceeded, or if cells are exposed to damaging insults, deprived of critical nutrients, or compromised by mutations affecting essential cellular functions, a sequence of events follows that is termed cell injury. It occurs when cells are stressed so severely that they can no longer adapt.

Causes with examples:

CauseExamples
Oxygen deprivationHypoxia — cardiorespiratory failure, anemia, CO poisoning, severe blood loss; ischemia — impeded arterial flow or reduced venous drainage
Physical agentsMechanical trauma, burns, deep cold, radiation, electric shock, sudden pressure changes
Chemicals and drugsArsenic, cyanide, mercury, alcohol, CCl4, pesticides, asbestos, therapeutic drug side effects
Infectious agentsVirus, rickettsiae, bacteria, fungi, parasites
Immunologic reactionsAutoimmune disease, reactions to viruses and environmental substances
Genetic abnormalitiesDown syndrome, sickle cell anemia
Nutritional imbalancesProtein-calorie deficiency, vitamin deficiency, excess (obesity, atherosclerosis)

Ref: V1-ch01

17 List the cellular responses to various types of injury. What are the consequences of irreversible cell injury?

Cellular responses to stress and noxious stimuli:

  1. Cellular adaptations — hyperplasia, hypertrophy, atrophy, metaplasia.
  2. Reversible (mild) cell injury — cellular swelling and fatty change.
  3. Irreversible (severe) cell injury — cell death by necrosis and apoptosis.
  4. Subcellular alterations — lysosomal catabolism/autophagy, induction (hypertrophy) of smooth ER, mitochondrial alterations, cytoskeletal abnormalities.
  5. Intracellular accumulations.
  6. Pathological calcification.
  7. Cellular aging.

Consequences of irreversible cell injury: Increasing swelling of the cell, swelling and disruption of lysosomes, vacuolization of mitochondria with reduced capacity to generate ATP, disruption of cellular membranes and profound nuclear changes — so the cell dies. Membrane leakage releases intracellular enzymes (creatine kinase, LDH) into blood (basis of biomarker tests); dead cells provoke inflammation, are removed by phagocytosis, and are followed by organization, fibrous repair, or dystrophic calcification if not removed.

Ref: V1-ch01

18 When does a reversible cell injury become irreversible? Describe the mechanism of fatty change in liver due to reversible injury.

Point of no return: Although it is still largely undefined, two phenomena consistently characterize irreversibility:

  1. Inability to reverse mitochondrial dysfunction (lack of oxidative phosphorylation and ATP generation) even after the original injury has resolved.
  2. Profound disturbances in membrane function of the cell and its organelles — especially lysosomal membrane injury, which causes enzymatic dissolution of the cell characteristic of necrosis.

Mechanism of fatty change (steatosis): It is the abnormal accumulation of triglycerides in parenchymal cells; excess triglyceride results from a defect at any step from fatty acid entry to lipoprotein exit:

  1. Excess entry of free fatty acids (starvation, diabetes mellitus).
  2. Increased fatty acid synthesis from acetate.
  3. Decreased oxidation of fatty acids due to anoxia.
  4. Increased esterification to triglyceride (alcoholism, via increased alpha-glycerophosphate).
  5. Decreased apoprotein synthesis (protein-energy malnutrition, CCl4 poisoning) and impaired lipoprotein secretion, so triglycerides cannot be exported.

Ref: V1-ch01, V1-ch02

19 How does ischemia cause cell injury? What is ischemia-reperfusion injury?

Ischemic injury:

  1. Ischemia decreases oxidative phosphorylation in mitochondria and lowers ATP. Loss of ATP first causes reversible injury: Na+-K+-ATPase pump failure leads to influx of Ca2+, water and Na+ with loss of K+, causing cell and organelle swelling, loss of microvilli and bleb formation; increased anaerobic glycolysis produces lactic acid, lowering pH and clumping nuclear chromatin; ribosomes detach from rough ER so protein synthesis falls and lipid is deposited.
  2. With persistent ischemia, membrane injury causes leakage of enzymes (CK, LDH), Ca2+ influx, release and activation of lysosomal enzymes — irreversible injury and death by necrosis. Ischemia is more dangerous than hypoxia because substrate delivery for glycolysis also stops and metabolites accumulate.

Ischemia-reperfusion injury: Restoration of blood flow can paradoxically exacerbate injury and cause death by necrosis and apoptosis. Mechanisms: oxidative stress from reactive oxygen and nitrogen species (damaged mitochondria, leukocyte oxidases), intracellular calcium overload, inflammation (cytokines, adhesion molecules, recruited neutrophils) and complement activation by deposited IgM. It matters in myocardial and cerebral infarction and after reperfusion therapy.

Ref: V1-ch01

20 Describe the mechanism of cell injury in ATP depletion/hypoxia.

Mechanism: Hypoxia/ischemia (also mitochondrial damage and toxins such as cyanide) reduce oxidative phosphorylation and deplete ATP. ATP depletion to 5-10% of normal has widespread effects:

  1. Pump failure: decreased activity of the energy-dependent Na+-K+-ATPase pump causes influx of Ca2+, water and Na+ and loss of K+, producing ER and cellular swelling, loss of microvilli and appearance of blebs (reversible injury).
  2. Increased anaerobic glycolysis: more lactic acid is produced, intracellular pH falls and nuclear chromatin clumps.
  3. Detachment of ribosomes from rough ER: protein synthesis decreases, leading to lipid deposition (fatty change).
  4. Membrane and calcium effects: impaired phospholipid turnover and failure of Ca2+ pumps raise cytosolic Ca2+, activating phospholipases, proteases, endonucleases and ATPases that damage membranes and DNA.
  5. Eventually irreversible damage to mitochondrial and lysosomal membranes occurs and the cell undergoes necrosis.
High yield: ATP depletion triad to memorize — pump failure (swelling), anaerobic glycolysis (lactic acidosis, chromatin clumping), ribosome detachment (low protein synthesis, fat deposition).

Ref: V1-ch01

21 Define free radical. Classify it. How are free radicals generated within the cells?

Definition: Free radicals are chemical species that have a single unpaired electron in an outer orbit.

Classification:

  1. Oxygen-derived free radicals — superoxide anion (O2-), hydrogen peroxide (H2O2), hydroxyl ion (OH).
  2. Carbon-derived free radicals — CCl3 radical.
  3. Nitrogen-derived free radicals — NO, ONOO-, NO2-, NO3-.

Generation within cells:

  1. Reduction-oxidation reactions of normal metabolism — during respiration oxygen is partially reduced stepwise (1 electron: superoxide; 2: H2O2; 3: hydroxyl).
  2. Absorption of radiant energy (UV light, X-rays) — ionizing radiation splits water to OH + H.
  3. Rapid bursts of ROS in activated leukocytes during inflammation; intracellular oxidases such as xanthine oxidase generate superoxide.
  4. Enzymatic metabolism of chemicals or drugs (CCl4 generates CCl3 radical).
  5. Transition metals (Fe, Cu) catalyse radical formation, e.g. Fenton reaction: H2O2 + Fe2+ gives Fe3+ + OH + OH-.
  6. Nitric oxide from endothelium, macrophages and neurons, converted to highly reactive peroxynitrite.

Ref: V1-ch01

22 How can free radicals damage the cells? How are these eliminated/removed from the body?

Damage caused by free radicals:

  1. Lipid peroxidation of membranes: radicals attack double bonds of unsaturated fatty acids in plasma and organellar membranes; peroxides formed act on other fatty acids, producing propagation and extensive membrane damage.
  2. Oxidative modification of proteins: oxidation of amino acid side chains, formation of covalent protein-protein cross-links (disulfide bonds), and oxidation of the backbone causing fragmentation.
  3. Lesions in DNA: breakdown of DNA strands, cross-linking of strands and formation of adducts.

Free radical injury contributes to chemical and radiation injury, ischemia-reperfusion injury, cellular aging and microbial killing by phagocytes.

Removal/elimination:

  1. Spontaneous destruction — radicals are unstable and decay (superoxide to O2 and H2O2).
  2. Antioxidants that block initiation or scavenge radicals — vitamins E and A, vitamin C (ascorbic acid) and glutathione.
  3. Binding of Fe and Cu to storage/transport proteins (transferrin, ferritin, lactoferrin, ceruloplasmin) limiting ROS formation.
  4. Enzymes — superoxide dismutase (superoxide to H2O2), catalase (H2O2 to water and O2), glutathione peroxidase (peroxides reduced using GSH).

Ref: V1-ch01

23 What is the effect of membrane damage and mitochondrial damage on the cell? Mention the effects of DNA damage of cells.

Effect of mitochondrial damage: opening of the mitochondrial permeability transition pore reduces membrane potential and ATP generation, so oxidative phosphorylation fails and the cell dies by necrosis; release of mitochondrial proteins into cytosol triggers death by apoptosis; incomplete oxidative phosphorylation generates reactive oxygen species that cause further injury.

Effect of membrane damage:

  • Plasma membrane — loss of osmotic balance, influx of fluid and ions, leakage of cellular contents and of glycolytic intermediates needed for ATP reconstitution; leakage of enzymes (CK, LDH) is the basis of biomarker tests; leaked contents provoke inflammation.
  • Lysosomal membranes — acid hydrolases (RNases, DNases, proteases, phosphatases, glucosidases) escape and digest the cell, pushing it into necrosis.

Effects of DNA damage: if repairable, damage-checkpoint mechanisms arrest the cycle for repair; if too severe (radiation, anticancer drugs, ROS, aging), a p53-triggered suicide program kills the cell by apoptosis; misrepaired or unrepaired damage causes mutations, aberrant gene expression and malignant transformation, and accumulated damage contributes to cellular aging.

Ref: V1-ch01

24 Describe the intracellular accumulations with examples. Mention common sites of lipid accumulation.

Definition: Intracellular accumulation is a manifestation of metabolic derangement characterized by intracellular accumulation of abnormal amounts of various substances.

Types with examples:

  1. Normal cellular constituents in excess — water, lipids, protein, carbohydrates (e.g. fatty change).
  2. Abnormal exogenous substances — carbon particles (coal miners), silica (silica workers).
  3. Abnormal endogenous substances from abnormal synthesis/metabolism — glycogen storage disease, haemochromatosis (iron), Wilson disease (copper), mutated alpha-1-antitrypsin.
  4. Pigments — exogenous: carbon/coal dust (anthracosis); endogenous: lipofuscin, melanin, hemosiderin, bilirubin.

Mechanisms: inadequate removal of a normal substance (defective packaging/transport, e.g. fatty change); accumulation of an abnormal endogenous substance (defective folding, packaging, transport or secretion); failure to degrade a metabolite (inherited enzyme deficiency, storage diseases); deposition of an abnormal exogenous substance the cell cannot degrade or export (carbon, silica).

Common sites of lipid accumulation: liver (most common, as it is the major organ of fat metabolism), heart, skeletal muscle and kidney; triglycerides accumulate in hepatocytes as fatty change.

Ref: V1-ch01, V1-ch02

25 Define and classify pathological calcification. Give the mechanisms of pathological calcification.

Definition: Pathologic calcification is the abnormal tissue deposition of calcium salts, together with smaller amounts of iron, magnesium and other mineral salts.

Classification:

FeatureDystrophic calcificationMetastatic calcification
DefinitionDeposition of calcium locally in dead or dying tissue despite normal serum calciumDeposition in normal tissue due to hypercalcemia
Serum calciumNormalIncreased
SitesAtheromas, aged/damaged heart valves, tuberculous lymph node, infarct, fat necrosis, papillary thyroid carcinoma (psammoma bodies)Gastric mucosa, kidneys, lungs, systemic arteries, pulmonary veins
Psammoma bodiesMay be foundNot found

Mechanism: The final common pathway is formation of crystalline calcium phosphate in the form of apatite, in two phases:

  1. Initiation (nucleation) — formation of a microcrystal that propagates and perforates membranes; intracellularly it occurs in mitochondria of dead or dying cells, extracellularly in phospholipids of membrane-bound vesicles, cartilage and bone matrix vesicles.
  2. Propagation — depends on the concentration of Ca2+ and phosphate and on inhibitors and proteins in the extracellular space.

Ref: V1-ch02

26 What are the causes of metastatic calcification? List the common sites of metastatic calcification.

Definition: Metastatic calcification is deposition of calcium salts in normal tissue due to hypercalcemia.

Causes (four principal causes of hypercalcemia):

  1. Increased secretion of parathyroid hormone (PTH) or PTH-related protein with bone resorption — e.g. parathyroid adenoma.
  2. Resorption of bone tissue — primary bone marrow tumors (multiple myeloma, leukemia), diffuse skeletal metastases (breast cancer), accelerated bone turnover (Paget disease), immobilization.
  3. Vitamin D-related disorders — vitamin D intoxication, sarcoidosis, idiopathic hypercalcemia of infancy (Williams syndrome).
  4. Renal failure (chronic kidney disease) — phosphate retention causing secondary hyperparathyroidism.

Common sites: gastric mucosa, kidneys, lungs, systemic arteries and pulmonary veins (it never occurs in bone and enamel).

Consequences: calcium in kidney causes nephrocalcinosis and renal damage; in lungs, respiratory deficits; in blood vessels, obstruction to blood flow (ischemia).

Ref: V1-ch02

27 Describe various types of abnormal pigmentations.

Classification: Abnormal pigments are classified as exogenous (introduced from outside) and endogenous (produced within the body).

Exogenous pigments:

  • Carbon/coal dust — anthracosis, accumulation of carbon particles in the lungs of coal miners.
  • Other inhaled or injected deposits — silica, and metallic pigments such as silver (argyria) or tattoo pigment.

Endogenous pigments:

PigmentNature and features
LipofuscinYellow-brown "wear-and-tear" pigment of lipid peroxidation of membranes; undigested residual material of autophagy; accumulates in aged cells and in brown atrophy of the heart
MelaninDark pigment from tyrosine metabolism; increased in nevi, Addison disease, and pigmented skin lesions
HemosiderinGolden-brown iron-containing pigment derived from hemoglobin; seen in hemolysis, hemorrhage, infarcts (hemosiderosis); demonstrated by Perls Prussian blue stain
BilirubinProduct of hemoglobin breakdown; accumulates in jaundice and in bile staining of tissues

Ref: V1-ch02

CELL DEATH
28 What are the types of cell death? Describe the basic changes in necrotic cells.

Types of cell death:

  1. Two principal types — necrosis and apoptosis (irreversible cell injury equals cell death).
  2. Other mechanisms of cell death — necroptosis, pyroptosis and ferroptosis.

Basic changes in necrotic cells:

Nuclear changes (from non-specific DNA breakdown), in sequence:

  1. Pyknosis — nuclear shrinkage with increased basophilia as chromatin condenses into a solid mass.
  2. Karyorrhexis — fragmentation of the pyknotic nucleus.
  3. Karyolysis — nuclear fading as chromatin is dissolved by DNases and RNases; the nucleus disappears within 1-2 days.

Cytoplasmic changes: increased eosinophilia (loss of RNA and denatured proteins), glassy homogeneous appearance (glycogen loss), vacuolated moth-eaten cytoplasm (organelle loss), and myelin figures from damaged membranes. The whole cell swells, membranes rupture and contents leak, provoking inflammation.

Ref: V1-ch01

29 Define necrosis. Shortly describe the mechanism of necrosis.

Definition: Necrosis is a spectrum of morphologic changes that follow cell death in living tissue, largely resulting from the progressive degradative action of enzymes on the lethally injured cell. It is a pathologic process consequent to severe injury, characterized by denaturation of cellular proteins, leakage of cellular contents through damaged membranes, local inflammation and enzymatic digestion of the lethally injured cell.

Mechanism:

  1. Severe or persistent injury causes loss of membrane integrity (driven by ATP depletion, membrane damage and free radical injury).
  2. Cellular contents leak through the damaged plasma membrane into the extracellular space (e.g. ATP, uric acid, other molecules).
  3. These molecules are recognized by macrophage and other receptors, triggering phagocytosis of debris and cytokine production, which induces inflammation.
  4. Enzymes from the dying cells themselves (autolysis by own lysosomes) and from immigrant leukocytes (heterolysis) digest the cell.

Ref: V1-ch01

30 What are the types of necrosis? Give the morphology of coagulative necrosis.

Types of necrosis:

  1. Basic types — coagulative necrosis (e.g. myocardial infarction, kidney, liver, adrenal infarcts) and liquefactive/colliquative necrosis (e.g. abscess, boil, ischemic necrosis of brain).
  2. Special types — gangrenous necrosis, caseous necrosis, fat necrosis (enzymatic of pancreas/omentum; traumatic of breast) and fibrinoid necrosis (immune-mediated vasculitis, rheumatic fever, SLE).

Morphology of coagulative necrosis (the commonest type): It is the form in which the basic cellular shape, outline and architecture are preserved for at least some days (except in brain); denaturation of structural and enzymatic proteins is dominant.

  • Gross: affected tissue is firm, dry, opaque and homogeneous; the localized area of coagulative necrosis is called an infarct (e.g. wedge-shaped kidney infarct).
  • Microscopic: preservation of basic cell outline, cytoplasm becomes opaque and acidophilic, nuclei show pyknosis, karyorrhexis or karyolysis (a necrotic cell lacks a nucleus), surrounded by a hyperemic zone with acute inflammatory cells.

Ref: V1-ch01

31 What are the features of caseous necrosis and liquefactive necrosis? Which necrosis forms pus?

Caseous necrosis:

  • A distinctive form of coagulative necrosis, most common in foci of tuberculous infection (also brucellosis, cat scratch disease).
  • Gross: white, cheesy (caseous), friable appearance.
  • Microscopic: a collection of fragmented or lysed cells and amorphous granular debris enclosed within a distinctive inflammatory border (granuloma) of epithelioid cells, lymphocytes and fibroblasts; tissue architecture and cell outline are totally lost.

Liquefactive necrosis:

  • Enzymatic digestion of dead cells transforms the tissue into a liquid, viscous mass with complete loss of cellular architecture.
  • Examples: focal bacterial or fungal infections (abscess, boil) and ischemic necrosis of brain tissue.
  • Gross: the necrotic material is creamy yellow because of the presence of dead leukocytes.

Which forms pus: Liquefactive necrosis — the creamy yellow necrotic material composed of dead leukocytes and digested tissue is called pus.

Ref: V1-ch01

32 What are the types of fat necrosis? Mention its common sites. What are the causes of gangrenous necrosis?

Types of fat necrosis:

  1. Enzymatic fat necrosis — of pancreas and omental tissue in acute pancreatitis. Activated pancreatic lipase liquefies fat cell membranes and splits triglyceride esters; the released fatty acids combine with calcium producing chalky white areas (fat saponification). Microscopically: shadowy outlines of necrotic fat cells with basophilic calcium deposits surrounded by inflammation.
  2. Traumatic fat necrosis — due to blunt injury, classically in the breast.

Common sites: pancreas, omentum and peritoneal cavity (enzymatic); breast and other subcutaneous adipose tissue (traumatic).

Causes of gangrenous necrosis: It is coagulative necrosis of a limb (usually the lower leg) after loss of blood supply — dry gangrene. Causes include:

  1. Disease of the arterial wall — atherosclerosis, arteriolosclerosis, thromboangiitis obliterans (Buerger disease); commonest example is diabetic gangrene.
  2. Arterial spasm — Raynaud disease, ergot poisoning.
  3. Obstruction of the lumen — thrombosis, embolism.
  4. External pressure — tumor, tight plaster. Superadded bacterial infection converts it to wet (moist) gangrene; Clostridium perfringens causes gas gangrene.

Ref: V1-ch01

33 Define apoptosis. Give the mechanism of apoptosis.

Definition: Apoptosis is a type of cell death induced by a tightly regulated suicide program in which cells destined to die activate intrinsic enzymes that degrade the cell's genomic DNA and nuclear and cytoplasmic proteins. Because it is genetically regulated it is also called programmed cell death.

Mechanism: Apoptosis results from activation of enzymes called caspases (which degrade proteins and DNA) followed by recognition and removal of the dead cells by phagocytes.

  1. Initiation phase — two pathways converge on caspase activation: (a) intrinsic (mitochondrial) pathway — increased mitochondrial permeability releases pro-apoptotic molecules into the cytoplasm; (b) extrinsic (death receptor-initiated) pathway — engagement of plasma membrane death receptors of the TNF receptor family.
  2. Execution phase — both pathways activate a caspase cascade that triggers cellular fragmentation. Initiator caspases are caspase-8, -9 and -10; executioner caspases are caspase-3 and -6.
  3. Dead cells are engulfed by phagocytes; apoptotic bodies are degraded by lysosomal enzymes.

Ref: V1-ch01

34 Differentiate necrosis from apoptosis.
FeatureNecrosisApoptosis
DefinitionMorphologic changes following cell death in living tissue from degradative enzyme actionDeath induced by a tightly regulated suicide program activating intrinsic enzymes
EtiologyGrossly irreversible (lethal) cellular damageSubtle cellular damage; physiologic programmed cell removal
Cells involvedMany contiguous cellsSingle cells or small clusters
Cell sizeEnlarged (swelling)Reduced (shrinkage)
Plasma membraneDisruptedIntact but altered
NucleusPyknosis, karyorrhexis, karyolysisFragmentation into round nucleosomes (chromatin condensation)
Cellular contentsEnzymatic digestion; may leak outIntact; released in apoptotic bodies
InflammationFrequentNo
SettingInvariably pathologicOften physiologic, may be pathologic
DNA fragmentationHaphazardRegular (ladder pattern)
High yield: Swelling + membrane disruption + leaked contents + inflammation = NECROSIS. Shrinkage + intact membrane + apoptotic bodies + no inflammation = APOPTOSIS.

Ref: V1-ch01

35 What is necroptosis and pyroptosis? Give the examples of different types of apoptosis.

Necroptosis: A hybrid form of cell death sharing features of both necrosis and apoptosis. Morphologically and biochemically it resembles necrosis (loss of ATP, cell and organelle swelling, ROS generation, lysosomal enzyme release, rupture of the plasma membrane), but mechanistically it is triggered by genetic signal-transduction pathways like apoptosis. Because its signals do not activate caspases it is called "caspase-independent" programmed necrosis. Examples: physiologic — formation of the mammalian bone growth plate; pathologic — steatohepatitis, acute pancreatitis, ischemia-reperfusion injury, Parkinson disease; also a backup host defense against viruses encoding caspase inhibitors (e.g. cytomegalovirus).

Pyroptosis (fever + apoptosis): A form of apoptosis accompanied by release of the fever-inducing cytokine IL-1 (pyro means fever); it occurs in cells infected by microbes.

Examples of different types of programmed cell death:

  • Apoptosis — intrinsic type (DNA damage by radiation/drugs, hypoxia, ER stress) and extrinsic type (death receptor engagement); physiologic examples include removal of supernumerary cells in development and endometrial breakdown in the menstrual cycle.
  • Necroptosis — as above; Ferroptosis — iron-dependent, lipid peroxidation-driven death, e.g. in cancer, neurodegenerative disease and stroke.

Ref: V1-ch01

36 Classify apoptosis with examples. Why does inflammation not occur in apoptosis?

Classification with examples:

  1. Physiologic apoptosis (removal of cells no longer needed) — removal of supernumerary cells during development; involution of hormone-dependent tissues on hormone withdrawal (endometrial breakdown in menstruation, ovarian follicular atresia, regression of lactating breast after weaning, prostatic atrophy after castration); cell turnover in proliferating populations (immature lymphocytes, B cells in germinal centers, intestinal crypt cells); elimination of self-reactive lymphocytes; death of neutrophils after acute inflammation.
  2. Pathologic apoptosis (cells injured beyond repair) — DNA damage from radiation, cytotoxic drugs and hypoxia; accumulation of misfolded proteins causing ER stress; infections, particularly viral hepatitis, adenovirus and HIV; pathologic atrophy after duct obstruction (pancreas, parotid, kidney).
  3. Mechanistic classification — intrinsic (mitochondrial) pathway and extrinsic (death receptor) pathway, both converging on the caspase cascade.

Why inflammation does not occur: In apoptosis the plasma membrane of the cell remains intact, so the cells cannot release their cellular contents; the cell fragments into membrane-bound apoptotic bodies that are rapidly recognized and phagocytosed by macrophages before any leakage. Since no intracellular molecules act as danger signals to trigger cytokine production, there is no inflammatory reaction — unlike necrosis, where contents leak and provoke inflammation.

Ref: V1-ch01

GENERAL PATHOLOGY — Acute Inflammation · Chronic Inflammation
ACUTE INFLAMMATION
1 Define inflammation. What are the types of inflammation? How does inflammation help our body?

Definition: Inflammation is a complex response of vascularized tissues to infections and damaged (usually necrotic) cells, consisting of vascular responses, migration and activation of leukocytes, and systemic reactions, aimed at eliminating the offending agents. The suffix -itis after an organ denotes inflammation there (e.g. appendicitis, conjunctivitis), with exceptions like boil, abscess and pneumonia.

Types of inflammation:

  • By duration: (a) Acute inflammation — serous, fibrinous, suppurative/purulent, ulcers; (b) Chronic inflammation — chronic specific (granulomatous, hallmark = granuloma, e.g. tuberculosis, leprosy, syphilis) and chronic non-specific (no granuloma, e.g. chronic osteomyelitis, chronic ulcer).
  • Other types: local inflammation and systemic inflammation (e.g. sepsis).

How it helps the body (beneficial effects):

  • Dilutes, localizes and destroys the injurious agent.
  • Limits tissue injury and removes necrotic debris (consequences of cell injury).
  • Restores the tissue towards normality; if inflammation did not occur, infections would go unchecked and wounds would never heal.

Ref: V1-ch03

2 What are the components of inflammation? How inflammation is terminated?

Components of inflammation: Two main components —

  1. Vascular (exudative) component: dilatation of blood vessels (causing redness and heat) and vascular leakage of fluid containing proteins such as fibrin and immunoglobulins into the damaged tissue, producing exudative edema (swelling); the distended tissue compresses nerve endings and causes pain.
  2. Cellular component: emigration of leukocytes from the blood into the site of injury (leukocyte extravasation), where they act as phagocytes, picking up microbes, dead cells and foreign material.

Termination of inflammation: The response is actively regulated once the purpose is achieved —

  • Removal of the stimulus by phagocytic cells ends mediator release.
  • Inactivation and enzymatic degradation of chemical mediators (histamine, kinins, prostaglandins).
  • Anti-inflammatory mediators (lipoxins, IL-10, TGF-beta) counter-regulate pro-inflammatory signals.
  • Apoptosis of neutrophils followed by their clearance (efferocytosis) by macrophages, which switch to a repair phenotype.
  • Resorption of edema fluid by lymphatics, followed by repair/regeneration.

Ref: V1-ch03

3 Define acute inflammation. What is the cell of acute inflammation? What are the outcomes of acute inflammation?

Definition: Acute inflammation is a rapid, often self-limited response to offending agents that are readily eliminated (e.g. many bacteria, fungi and dead cells). It develops within minutes to hours, lasts a few hours to a few days, and is characterized by exudation of fluid and plasma proteins (edema) and emigration of leukocytes.

Cell of acute inflammation: The neutrophil (polymorphonuclear leukocyte, PMN) — it predominates within 6–24 hours; macrophages appear after 24–48 hours; eosinophils predominate in parasitic infection and lymphocytes in viral infection.

Outcomes (fates) of acute inflammation:

  1. Complete resolution — when injury is limited, little tissue destruction, and parenchyma can regenerate (debris removed by macrophages, edema resorbed by lymphatics).
  2. Healing by connective tissue replacement (scarring/fibrosis) — when the tissue cannot regenerate.
  3. Progression to chronic inflammation — when the injurious agent persists or healing is interfered with.

Ref: V1-ch03

4 What are the vascular events of acute inflammation? How (rubor) redness develop in the area of acute inflammation?

Vascular events:

  1. Change in vascular flow and caliber: (a) Vasodilation — induced by mediators, particularly histamine and nitric oxide; (b) increased arterial blood flow to the site.
  2. Increased vascular permeability of the microvasculature — escape of protein-rich fluid (exudate) into extravascular tissue causing edema. (Increased permeability is the hallmark of acute inflammation.)
  3. Slowing of blood flow (stasis): combined effect of vasodilation and fluid loss — concentration of red cells, increased viscosity, and engorgement of small vessels with slowly moving red cells.

How rubor (redness) develops: Vasodilation (mediated by histamine, nitric oxide and prostaglandins acting on vascular smooth muscle) increases arterial blood flow to the inflamed area; the engorged, dilated superficial vessels filled with rapidly flowing oxygenated blood impart visible redness (erythema) and warmth.

Ref: V1-ch03

5 Mention the cardinal signs of acute inflammation? How does the inflamed organ become swollen?

Cardinal signs of acute inflammation:

  1. Rubor (redness) — due to vasodilation.
  2. Tumor (swelling) — due to extravascular accumulation of fluid (edema).
  3. Calor (heat) — due to increased blood flow.
  4. Dolor (pain) — due to tissue distension compressing nerve endings and by mediators (prostaglandins, bradykinin).
  5. Functio laesa (loss of function) — due to local pain and tissue destruction.

The first four signs were listed by Celsus (Roman writer) and the fifth was added by Rudolf Virchow; the first four are more prominent in acute than in chronic inflammation.

How swelling (tumor) occurs: Increased vascular permeability allows protein-rich fluid (exudate) plus dilated vessels to leak into the interstitial tissue; fluid accumulates faster than lymphatic drainage can remove it, so the tissue becomes distended and tense.

High yield: Cardinal signs mnemonic — "Celsus: Rubor, Tumor, Calor, Dolor" + Virchow's "Functio laesa" (5th sign). Vasodilation → redness and heat; permeability → edema → pain → loss of function.

Ref: V1-ch03

6 Describe the mechanism of vasodilation in acute inflammation. How calor (raised temperature) is generated in the area of acute inflammation?

Mechanism of vasodilation:

  • Vasodilation is induced by several chemical mediators acting on vascular smooth muscle — principally histamine (released from mast cell granules) and nitric oxide (NO) (produced by endothelium and macrophages, causing smooth muscle relaxation), along with prostaglandins (PGE1, PGE2, PGI2).
  • It is one of the earliest vascular changes; the resulting increase in arterial blood flow is maintained by continued mediator release and by local neuromediator (axon) reflexes (substance P).

Generation of calor (heat): Vasodilation markedly increases the delivery of warm blood from the core to the inflamed superficial tissue. Because blood normally loses heat at the skin, the sudden influx of warm blood raises local tissue temperature — hence calor. (Systemic fever is separate: IL-1, TNF and prostaglandin E2 act on the hypothalamic set point.)

Ref: V1-ch03

7 Describe the mechanism of increased vascular permeability in acute inflammation. How pain (dolor) is generated in the area of acute inflammation?

Mechanism of increased vascular permeability (vascular leakage):

  1. Contraction of endothelial cells — most common mechanism; creates interendothelial gaps; immediate transient response, short-lived (15–30 min); elicited by histamine, bradykinin and leukotrienes.
  2. Direct endothelial injury — severe insults (thermal burns, microbes, microbial toxins) damage/destroy endothelium; immediate and sustained for hours until vessels thrombose or repair.
  3. Leukocyte-mediated endothelial injury — adherent neutrophils release toxic oxygen species and proteolytic enzymes that injure or detach endothelium.
  4. Increased transcytosis — transport of fluid and proteins through endothelial cells via intracellular channels, stimulated by VEGF.

Generation of pain (dolor): (1) Exudate accumulation distends the tissue, stretching the tissue and compressing nerve endings; (2) chemical mediators — bradykinin, prostaglandins, H+, K+ and histamine — sensitize and stimulate pain fibres; (3) direct stimulation of nerve endings by the noxious agent.

Ref: V1-ch03

8 How can inflammation be harmful to the body? What are the systemic effects of inflammation.

Harmful effects of inflammation (when misdirected):

  • Pain and functional impairment.
  • Autoimmune disease.
  • Life-threatening hypersensitivity reactions to insect bites, foods, drugs and toxins.
  • Chronic diseases — rheumatoid arthritis, atherosclerosis, lung fibrosis.
  • Fibrosis causing disfiguring scars; fibrous bands causing intestinal obstruction or limiting joint mobility.
  • May contribute to cancer (for these wide-ranging harms it is called "the silent killer").

Systemic effects (acute phase response): Cytokine-induced systemic reactions (SIRS) include — fever; production of acute-phase proteins (CRP, serum amyloid A, fibrinogen); leukocytosis; septic shock in sepsis (triad: DIC, hypoglycemia, cardiovascular failure); and other changes like raised ESR, anemia, thrombocytopenia, anorexia, malaise, rigors and chills.

Ref: V1-ch03

9 Describe the cellular events of inflammation before extravasation. Which cells may play role as a phagocyte?

Cellular events before (and during) extravasation — events within the vessel lumen:

  1. Margination: as stasis develops, leukocytes that normally stream in the center of the flow take a peripheral position along the endothelial surface (redistribution along the vessel margins).
  2. Rolling: transient, loose adhesion to endothelium — bind, detach and bind again — mediated by the selectin family (L-, E-, P-selectin).
  3. Firm adhesion: tight attachment of leukocytes to endothelium mediated by integrins (LFA-1, MAC-1, VLA-4) and cytokine-activated endothelial ligands.

These are followed by transmigration (diapedesis) and then chemotaxis in the tissues.

Phagocytes: neutrophils (first, 6–24 h), monocytes/macrophages (24–48 h), eosinophils (parasites) and dendritic cells; tissue macrophages include Kupffer cells (liver), alveolar macrophages (lung), microglia (CNS), Langerhans cells (skin) and histiocytes (connective tissue).

Ref: V1-ch03

10 What is opsonization? Name the opsonins. How opsonins help in phagocytosis?

Definition: Opsonization is the process of coating a microbe (or antigen/particle) with specific molecules so that it is targeted for phagocytosis and is more easily ingested by phagocytic cells. It greatly enhances phagocytosis.

Opsonins:

  1. IgG antibody
  2. C3b (complement component; also C4b to a lesser extent)
  3. Plasma lectins — mannose-binding lectin (MBL), collectins
  4. C-reactive protein (CRP)
  5. Fibrinogen

How they help: Opsonins bind the microbial surface and then engage high-affinity receptors (Fc receptors for IgG, CR1 for C3b) on the phagocyte. This bridges the particle to the leukocyte, so recognition and attachment — the first step of phagocytosis — occur readily, trigger engulfment by pseudopods, and enhance intracellular killing (oxidative burst).

Ref: V1-ch03

11 How the harmful substances are killed and degraded within the cells?

After recognition and engulfment (phagosome → phagolysosome), the ingested material is killed and degraded by:

1. Oxygen-dependent (free-radical) mechanisms:

  • MPO-dependent: phagocytosis triggers O2 consumption (respiratory burst) → glycogenolysis → glucose oxidation via HMP shunt → superoxide anion (O2-) → H2O2; myeloperoxidase plus a halide (Cl-) converts H2O2 to hypochlorite (HOCl), a potent antimicrobial agent that kills by halogenation.
  • MPO-deficient system: killing by superoxide, hydroxyl radicals and singlet oxygen.

2. Oxygen-independent mechanisms: lysosomal/granule constituents — bactericidal permeability-increasing protein (BPI), lysozyme (hydrolyzes cell wall), lactoferrin, major basic protein (toxic to parasites), defensins and elastase.

3. Degradation: after killing, acid hydrolases of lysosomes digest the microbes within the phagolysosome.

Ref: V1-ch03

12 What is chemotaxis? Name the chemokines. Describe how chemokine help in chemotaxis?

Definition: Chemotaxis is the migration of leukocytes in the tissues toward the site of injury along a chemical (concentration) gradient. Granulocytes, monocytes and, to a lesser extent, lymphocytes respond.

Chemotactic substances:

  • Exogenous: bacterial products.
  • Endogenous: complement components (especially C5a), leukotriene B4 (LTB4), and chemokines — a family of about 40 chemoattractant cytokines classified as C-X-C (alpha, e.g. IL-8), C-C (beta, e.g. MCP-1, RANTES), C (gamma) and CX3C chemokines.

How chemokines act: They bind specific G-protein receptors on the leukocyte surface → activate phospholipase C → hydrolysis of PIP2 to IP3 and DAG → rise in intracellular Ca2+ → assembly of contractile actin filaments → the cell extends filopodia that pull it forward up the chemotactic gradient toward the focus of injury. Their second function is controlling normal cell migration through tissues.

Ref: V1-ch03

13 List the mediators of inflammation according to the source.

Mediators originate either from cells or from plasma proteins:

SourceTypeMediators
Cell-derivedPreformed (stored)Histamine, serotonin, lysosomal enzymes
Newly synthesizedProstaglandins, leukotrienes, platelet-activating factor (PAF), reactive oxygen species, nitric oxide, cytokines (TNF, IL-1), chemokines
Plasma protein-derivedComplement systemC3a, C5a (anaphylatoxins/chemotactic), C3b (opsonin), C5b-9 (membrane attack complex)
Kinin system (via factor XII)Bradykinin
Coagulation systemProteases activated during coagulation (e.g. thrombin)

Ref: V1-ch03

14 How complements are activated? Mention the role of complements in acute inflammation.

Activation of complement: More than 20 serum proteins (mainly made in the liver) circulate in inactive form and, when activated, act in an orderly sequence by three pathways —

  1. Classical pathway: activated by antigen–antibody (immune) complexes.
  2. Alternative pathway: activated by bacterial endotoxin/LPS, fungal cell wall, viral envelope, cobra venom, aggregated IgA — without antibody.
  3. Lectin pathway: activated by plasma mannose-binding lectin (MBL) bound to microbial carbohydrates.

Role in acute inflammation:

  • Vascular phenomena: C3a, C4a and C5a are anaphylatoxins — they trigger mast cell degranulation and histamine release causing vasodilation and increased permeability.
  • Chemotaxis: C5a attracts neutrophils.
  • Opsonization: C3b (and C4b) coat microbes and favor phagocytosis.
  • Cell lysis: membrane attack complex (C5b-9) makes cell membranes permeable → lysis of bacteria, RBCs, virus-infected cells.
  • Other: C5a activates arachidonic acid metabolism; C1/C4 neutralize viruses; C3b enhances antibody production.

Ref: V1-ch03

15 What is cytokine? Mention the role of cytokines in acute inflammation.

Definition: Cytokines are proteins produced by many cell types — principally activated lymphocytes, macrophages and dendritic cells, but also endothelial, epithelial and connective tissue cells — that mediate and regulate immune and inflammatory reactions. They are of two types: monokines (monocyte-derived) and lymphokines (lymphocyte-derived).

Roles in acute inflammation:

  • TNF and IL-1 (from macrophages): stimulate expression of endothelial adhesion molecules (E-selectin, ICAM) enabling leukocyte recruitment; induce fever, pain, anorexia and shock; activate endothelium.
  • IL-6: drives the systemic acute-phase response (production of CRP and fibrinogen by liver).
  • Chemokines: recruit leukocytes to the site of inflammation and direct their migration.
  • IL-17: recruits neutrophils and monocytes; IL-12 → IFN-gamma: activates macrophages to kill microbes and tumor cells.

Ref: V1-ch03

16 How are the arachidonic acid metabolites generated? List the role of histamine in acute inflammation.

Generation of arachidonic acid metabolites: Arachidonic acid (a 20-carbon fatty acid) is normally esterified in cell membrane phospholipids. On stimulation (mechanical, chemical, physical, or by mediators such as C5a), phospholipase A2 releases it; it is then metabolized by two enzyme pathways:

  1. Cyclooxygenase (COX-1/COX-2): → prostaglandins, prostacyclin (PGI2) and thromboxane A2. (Aspirin and indomethacin inhibit COX.)
  2. Lipoxygenase: → leukotrienes (LTB4, LTC4, LTD4, LTE4) and lipoxins.

Actions: vasodilation (PGI2, PGE1/2, PGD2); vasoconstriction (TXA2, LTC4/D4/E4); increased permeability (LTC4/D4/E4); chemotaxis and leukocyte adhesion (LTB4, HETE, lipoxins).

Role of histamine (source: mast cells, basophils, platelets):

  • Vasodilation of arterioles (constricts large arteries).
  • Principal mediator of the immediate transient phase of increased vascular permeability — produces interendothelial gaps in venules.
  • Endothelial activation; bronchoconstriction; hypersecretion from nasal, bronchial and gastric glands.

Ref: V1-ch03

17 What are the morphological patterns of acute inflammation? Name the acute phase proteins.

Morphological patterns of acute inflammation:

  1. Serous inflammation: exudation of cell-poor fluid into body cavities or injured spaces — effusions (pleural, pericardial, ascites); skin blister after burn or viral infection.
  2. Fibrinous inflammation: large vascular leaks allow fibrinogen to pass out and form fibrin — e.g. fibrinous pericarditis after myocardial infarction; also in meninges and pleura.
  3. Purulent (suppurative) inflammation and abscess: pus production by pyogenic bacteria (e.g. staphylococci) — e.g. acute appendicitis; abscess = localized collection of pus buried in tissue.
  4. Ulcers: local defect/excavation of a surface produced by sloughing of inflamed necrotic tissue — e.g. peptic ulcer, pressure sore, leg ulcer.

Acute phase proteins: plasma proteins synthesized mainly in the liver in response to cytokines (IL-6 for CRP and fibrinogen; IL-1/TNF for SAA) — important ones are:

  • C-reactive protein (CRP)
  • Serum amyloid A protein (SAA)
  • Fibrinogen

Ref: V1-ch03

18 Why leucocytosis occurs in a patient with acute inflammation? Describe how fever develops due to inflammation.

Why leukocytosis occurs: In acute inflammation (especially bacterial infection) the cytokines IL-1 and TNF —

  • Accelerate release of leukocytes from the bone marrow reserve pool into the circulation; and
  • Increase production of colony-stimulating factor (CSF), which stimulates proliferation of leukocyte precursors in the bone marrow.

Result: neutrophilic leukocytosis. (In typhoid, viral/rickettsial/protozoal infections and overwhelming sepsis, leukopenia occurs instead.)

How fever develops:

  1. Exogenous pyrogens (bacterial products such as LPS) stimulate leukocytes to release endogenous pyrogens — the cytokines IL-1 and TNF.
  2. These increase cyclooxygenase levels, converting arachidonic acid to prostaglandins (especially PGE2) in the hypothalamus.
  3. PGE2 stimulates neurotransmitters that reset the hypothalamic temperature set point to a higher level.
  4. Heat loss is decreased (cutaneous vasoconstriction, shivering) and heat is conserved → rise of body temperature by 1–4°C → fever.

Ref: V1-ch03

19 What is ulcer? Give some examples of inflammatory ulcer. Describe the morphology of an inflammatory ulcer.

Definition: An ulcer is a local defect, or excavation, of the surface of an organ or tissue that is produced by the sloughing (shedding) of inflamed necrotic tissue.

Examples: peptic ulcer of stomach or duodenum (acute and chronic inflammation coexist), aphthous (mouth) ulcers, pressure ulcers (bedsore/decubitus), diabetic foot ulcers, arterial and venous leg ulcers, genital ulcers and corneal ulcers. Common sites are the mucosa of mouth, stomach, intestine and genitourinary tract, and the lower-extremity skin of older persons with circulatory disturbance.

Morphology of an inflammatory ulcer:

  • Surface: covered by a fibrinopurulent exudate — necrotic/sloughed tissue debris, fibrin and neutrophils.
  • Bed/floor: granulation tissue (newly formed thin-walled capillaries, fibroblasts and an inflammatory infiltrate of neutrophils, lymphocytes, plasma cells and macrophages in edematous stroma).
  • Base and margin: underlying fibrous tissue (evidence of healing by scarring) with hyperemic inflamed edges; adjacent epithelium may regenerate over the margin.

Ref: V1-ch03

20 Give some examples of serous inflammation. What is abscess? Give the composition of pus.

Serous inflammation: marked by exudation of cell-poor fluid into spaces created by cell injury or into body cavities lined by peritoneum, pleura or pericardium. Examples — pleural effusion, pericardial effusion, ascites; and skin blisters resulting from a burn or viral infection.

Abscess: A localized collection of pus caused by suppuration, buried in a tissue, an organ or a confined space; produced by deep seeding of pyogenic (pus-producing) bacteria (e.g. staphylococci) into tissue. Examples: boil (furuncle), dental, perianal, breast, liver, splenic, lung abscess and cold abscess in TB. Histology: central necrotic leukocytes and tissue cells, surrounded by a zone of preserved neutrophils, then vascular dilation and fibroblastic proliferation (beginning repair).

Composition of pus:

  • Healthy leukocytes (mostly neutrophils)
  • Pus cells (dead leukocytes)
  • Protein-rich fluid exudate (edema fluid) and fibrin
  • Liquefied debris of necrotic cells and tissue debris
  • Organisms (many of which are living)

Ref: V1-ch03

21 What is exudate? How does it form in acute inflammation?

Definition: Exudation is the escape of fluid, proteins and blood cells from the vascular system into the interstitial tissue or body cavities. An exudate is an inflammatory extravascular fluid that has a high protein concentration (more than 3 g/dL), contains cellular debris and has a high specific gravity (above 1.020).

Formation in acute inflammation:

  1. Alteration in vascular caliber (vasodilation) → increase in blood flow.
  2. Structural change in the microvasculature → increased vascular permeability.
  3. Fluid, plasma proteins and blood cells escape through the leaky venules into the extravascular tissue → protein-rich inflammatory edema = exudate.

Characteristics: high protein, high specific gravity, plenty of inflammatory cells (neutrophils or lymphocytes depending on cause), fibrinogen present (clots spontaneously), glucose greatly reduced. It dilutes the irritant, brings mediators, antibodies and antimicrobials to the site, and carries antigens to local lymph nodes.

Ref: V1-ch03

22 What is transudate? How it forms? Name some conditions where transudate is formed.

Definition: A transudate is a fluid with low protein content (most of which is albumin), little or no cellular material, and low specific gravity (less than 1.012). It is a non-inflammatory edema fluid.

Formation: A transudate forms when fluid leaks out because of altered intravascular pressure without an increase in vascular permeability —

  • Increased hydrostatic pressure (venous outflow obstruction), or
  • Decreased plasma colloid osmotic pressure (loss or decreased synthesis of plasma proteins).

Characteristics: low protein (less than 1 g/dL), low specific gravity, no inflammatory cells, fibrinogen absent (no clot).

Conditions: congestive cardiac failure (raised venous/ hydrostatic pressure), liver cirrhosis (decreased albumin synthesis), nephrotic syndrome (urinary protein loss), and other causes of venous obstruction.

Ref: V1-ch03

23 Differentiate exudate from transudate.
FeatureExudateTransudate
CauseIncreased vascular permeability (inflammation)Increased hydrostatic pressure or decreased plasma colloid osmotic pressure
Nature of fluidInflammatoryNon-inflammatory
Total proteinHigh (more than 3 g/dL)Low (less than 1 g/dL)
Protein distributionExcess of globulinsMostly albumin
FibrinogenPresent — clots spontaneouslyAbsent — no tendency to clot
Specific gravityHigh (above 1.020)Low (below 1.012)
Inflammatory cellsPlenty (neutrophils or lymphocytes depending on cause)Absent

Ref: V1-ch03

CHRONIC INFLAMMATION
24 What is chronic inflammation? Give some common examples of chronic inflammation.

Definition: Chronic inflammation is a response of prolonged duration (weeks to months) in which inflammation, tissue injury and attempts at repair coexist in varying combinations. It may be of two types — chronic specific inflammation, which is histologically distinctive with granuloma as its hallmark (e.g. tuberculosis, leprosy, syphilis), and chronic non-specific inflammation, which has no distinctive histology or granuloma and shows granulation tissue with healing by fibrosis (e.g. chronic osteomyelitis).

Common examples:

  • Tuberculosis and other granulomatous diseases (leprosy, sarcoidosis, leishmaniasis).
  • Rheumatoid arthritis and other autoimmune/hypersensitivity diseases.
  • Atherosclerosis, chronic lung diseases, ulcerative colitis.
  • Chronic osteomyelitis, chronic cholecystitis, chronic abscess/ulcer (non-specific type).
  • Unresolved acute inflammation, e.g. chronic lung abscess; peptic ulcer shows acute and chronic inflammation together.

Ref: V1-ch03

25 What are the features of chronic inflammation? Why fibrosis is more common in chronic inflammation?

Morphologic features:

  1. Active inflammation — infiltration with mononuclear cells: macrophages, lymphocytes and plasma cells.
  2. Tissue destruction — induced by the persistent offending agent or by the inflammatory cells themselves.
  3. Attempts at healing by connective tissue replacement of the damaged tissue, accomplished by angiogenesis (proliferation of small blood vessels) and, in particular, fibrosis.

Why fibrosis is more common:

  • The stimulus persists for weeks to months, so repeated/continuous tissue injury occurs with no time for complete resolution.
  • Macrophages persist and release growth factors (PDGF, FGF, TGF-beta) and cytokines that recruit and proliferate fibroblasts.
  • Angiogenesis and granulation tissue formation proceed simultaneously with inflammation, and damaged tissue that cannot regenerate is replaced by collagen — so healing by scarring (fibrosis) dominates, unlike the short-lived, self-limited acute reaction.

Ref: V1-ch03

26 Describe the role of macrophage in chronic inflammation. How it can stimulate inflammation and healing simultaneously?

Role of macrophage (the dominant cellular player in chronic inflammation):

  1. Initiation and amplification of the inflammatory response.
  2. Phagocytosis and microbial killing by oxidative and non-oxidative mechanisms.
  3. Scavenging of necrotic and apoptotic cells; clearance of toxins and debris.
  4. Antigen presentation to helper T cells (links innate and adaptive immunity); delayed-type hypersensitivity.
  5. Production of cytokines — IL-1 (activates CD4+ T cells) and TNF-alpha (inflammatory reactions).
  6. Tissue remodeling (elastase, collagenase, matrix proteins); granuloma formation; antitumor activity; production of complement components C1–C5 and properdin.

How it stimulates inflammation and healing together: Activated macrophages release IL-1, TNF, chemotactic and permeability factors that perpetuate inflammation, while simultaneously secreting growth factors — IL-1, platelet-derived growth factor (PDGF) and fibroblast growth factor (FGF) — that act on endothelial cells and fibroblasts to promote angiogenesis, wound healing and scar formation. Thus inflammation and repair proceed side by side, as occurs in chronic inflammation.

Ref: V1-ch03

27 What are the common causes of chronic inflammation? Name the cells of chronic inflammation. Describe the role of lymphocytes in chronic inflammation.

Common causes:

  1. Persistent infections by microbes difficult to eradicate — mycobacteria, certain viruses, fungi and parasites.
  2. Hypersensitivity diseases — autoimmune (rheumatoid arthritis, multiple sclerosis) and allergic (bronchial asthma).
  3. Prolonged exposure to toxic agents — exogenous (particulate silica → silicosis) or endogenous (toxic plasma lipids → atherosclerosis).
  4. Sequel of unresolved acute inflammation (e.g. acute lung infection → chronic lung abscess).

Cells of chronic inflammation: macrophage (predominant), lymphocyte, plasma cell, mast cell and eosinophil.

Role of lymphocytes:

  • T lymphocytes: cell-mediated immunity; activated Th1 cells secrete IFN-gamma that activates macrophages (key in granuloma formation) and IL-2 that activates cytotoxic T cells; also mediate delayed hypersensitivity reactions.
  • B lymphocytes: differentiate into plasma cells and produce antibodies → humoral (antibody-mediated) immunity.
  • Together with macrophages they sustain the prolonged immune response that defines chronic inflammation.

Ref: V1-ch03

28 What is giant cell? How inflammatory giant cell is formed?

Definition: Giant cells are large cells with more than one nucleus — multinucleate giant cells. In inflammation they are formed by fusion of activated macrophages (including epithelioid cells). They may attain a diameter of 40–50 micrometers and consist of a large mass of cytoplasm with many nuclei.

How inflammatory (granulomatous) giant cells are formed:

  1. An offending agent that is difficult to eradicate (e.g. M. tuberculosis, inert foreign material) is taken up by macrophages, which present antigen to helper T lymphocytes.
  2. Activated Th1 cells release cytokines — IFN-gamma (activates macrophages) and IL-2 — so macrophages become activated and transform into epithelioid cells.
  3. Multiple activated macrophages/epithelioid cells then fusion with each other (under cytokine influence, chiefly IFN-gamma and TNF) into one large cell with many nuclei — a multinucleated giant cell, usually at the periphery (Langhans type) or center of the granuloma.

Ref: V1-ch03

29 Classify giant cells according to pathogenesis. Name various types of giant cells with the clinical conditions where formed.

Classification according to pathogenesis:

A. Physiological giant cells — normal, formed by cell fusion for a physiological function.

B. Pathological giant cells — formed in disease, subtyped as:

  1. Inflammatory/granulomatous (fusion of macrophages in chronic inflammation)
  2. Reactive/degenerative and neoplastic (formed by abnormal cell growth or nuclear division without cytoplasmic division)
Type of giant cellClinical condition
Megakaryocyte (physiological)Normal bone marrow
Osteoclastic giant cell (physiological)Normal bone (bone resorption)
Syncytiotrophoblast (physiological)Normal placenta
Langhans giant cellTuberculous granuloma (also sarcoidosis)
Foreign body giant cellForeign body granuloma (talc, suture, wood splinter)
Osteoclastic giant cell (pathological)Giant cell tumor of bone
Aschoff giant cellRheumatic (rheumatoid) lesions
Reed-Sternberg giant cellHodgkin's disease (lymphoma)
Multinucleate giant cellHSV-infected cells
Tumor giant cellsAnaplastic tumors, choriocarcinoma, poorly differentiated astrocytoma

Ref: V1-ch03

30 Define granuloma. Give examples of 5 granulomatous inflammations with etiology.

Definition: A granuloma is a focus of chronic inflammation consisting of a microscopic aggregation of activated macrophages that are transformed into epithelial-like cells (epithelioid cells), surrounded by a collar of mononuclear leukocytes — principally lymphocytes and occasionally plasma cells. Granulomatous inflammation is a distinctive pattern of chronic inflammation in which the predominant cell is the activated macrophage, often with T lymphocytes and sometimes necrosis.

Five granulomatous inflammations with etiology:

DiseaseEtiology (causative agent)
Tuberculosis (prototype of granulomatous disease)Mycobacterium tuberculosis
LeprosyMycobacterium leprae
SyphilisTreponema pallidum
Cat-scratch diseaseBartonella henselae
SarcoidosisUnknown etiology

Others: Crohn's disease (unknown), silicosis (silica dust), berylliosis (beryllium), lymphogranuloma venereum (Chlamydia trachomatis), fungal infections and foreign body granulomas (talc, suture).

High yield: Key/essential feature of a granuloma = epithelioid cells (modified activated macrophages). Lymphocytes, plasma cells, giant cells and caseation are additional — not essential — features; central caseous necrosis (soft tubercle) is the hallmark of tuberculosis.

Ref: V1-ch03

31 Give the pathogenesis of granuloma in tuberculosis. What is epithelioid cell.

Pathogenesis of the TB granuloma (immune granuloma / soft tubercle):

  1. Mycobacterium tuberculosis is inhaled and reaches the lung, where macrophages engulf the bacilli but cannot easily degrade them (cord factor and mycolic acid resist killing); alveolar macrophages process and present the antigen to helper T (CD4+) lymphocytes.
  2. Activated Th1 cells release cytokines — IL-2 (activates cytotoxic T cells) and IFN-gamma (activates macrophages); TNF is also released.
  3. Activated macrophages become epithelioid cells and fuse to form multinucleated (Langhans) giant cells.
  4. Lymphocytes, plasma cells and fibroblasts accumulate around them, forming a rim/collar.
  5. Central caseation necrosis may occur (soft tubercle, hallmark of TB); if necrosis does not occur (e.g. sarcoidosis), a non-caseating granuloma (hard tubercle) results.

Epithelioid cell: A modified activated macrophage with abundant pink granular cytoplasm and indistinct cell boundaries (resembling epithelial cells), showing little phagocytic activity, which may fuse to form multinucleated giant cells.

Ref: V1-ch03

32 Draw and label a granuloma. Name the important cytokines that play role in granuloma formation.

Labeled structure of a granuloma (arranged from center outward):

  1. Center: caseation necrosis — structureless, granular, amorphous, cheesy material with loss of cellular detail (in the caseating/soft tubercle type; absent in hard tubercle).
  2. Epithelioid cells — aggregates of modified activated macrophages with pink granular cytoplasm and indistinct borders, often merging with one another.
  3. Langhans giant cell — large cell (40–50 micrometers) with nuclei arranged peripherally in a horse-shoe pattern (foreign-body type has haphazardly scattered nuclei), lying at the periphery or center.
  4. Collar of lymphocytes (with occasional plasma cells) surrounding the epithelioid aggregate.
  5. Outer rim of fibroblasts and connective tissue in older granulomas (healing by fibrosis).

Important cytokines in granuloma formation:

  • IFN-gamma (from Th1 cells and NK cells) — activates macrophages and transforms them into epithelioid cells (the key cytokine).
  • IL-12 (dendritic cells, macrophages) — induces IFN-gamma production.
  • IL-2 — activates cytotoxic T cells and promotes T-cell growth.
  • TNF — maintains granuloma architecture and promotes caseation necrosis.
  • IL-1 — macrophage activation and inflammation; TGF-beta — fibrosis.

Ref: V1-ch03

GENERAL PATHOLOGY — Tissue Repair · Edema & Electrolyte Imbalance
HEALING AND REPAIR
1 What are the mechanisms of tissue repair? What is organization and fibrosis?

Repair (healing): Restoration of tissue architecture and function after an injury.

Mechanisms of tissue repair: Two processes — (1) Regeneration, which restores normal cells, and (2) Scarring, the deposition of connective tissue. Repair may occur by regeneration alone, by scar alone, or by a combination of the two, depending on the severity of injury and the integrity of the supporting stroma.

Organization: When fibrosis develops in a tissue space occupied by an inflammatory exudate, it is called organization (e.g. organizing pneumonia in the lung).

Fibrosis: Excessive deposition of collagen and other extracellular matrix (ECM) components in a tissue — classically in lung, liver and kidney after chronic inflammation, and in myocardium after extensive ischemic necrosis (infarction).

Ref: V1-ch04

2 What is regeneration? How does it repair stable and labile tissue?

Regeneration: The process by which damaged tissue components are replaced so that the tissue essentially returns to a normal state. It occurs by proliferation of the differentiated cells that survive the injury and retain the capacity to proliferate, with contribution from tissue stem cells.

Repair of labile tissue: These cells proliferate continuously throughout life, so after injury the ever-present progenitor (basal) cell pool simply replenishes the lost cells — regeneration is rapid and complete (e.g. skin, gut epithelium, bone marrow).

Repair of stable tissue: These cells sit in G₀ with little baseline replication, but after injury they re-enter the cell cycle and proliferate, provided the tissue framework is intact (e.g. hepatocytes, renal tubular cells, endothelium, fibroblasts).

Limitation: Mammals have a limited regenerative capacity; only some components of most tissues can fully restore themselves.

Ref: V1-ch04

3 Classify tissue according to the intrinsic proliferative capacity with examples.

Classification by intrinsic proliferative (regenerative) capacity:

Type of cellBehaviourExamples
Labile (continuously dividing)Proliferate actively throughout life; regenerate well after injuryHaematopoietic cells of bone marrow; stratified squamous epithelium of skin, oral cavity, vagina, cervix; cuboidal epithelium of exocrine ducts; columnar epithelium of GI tract, uterus, fallopian tube; transitional epithelium of urinary tract
Quiescent (stable), in G₀Low level of replication normally, but can divide rapidly in response to stimuliParenchymal cells of liver, kidney and pancreas; vascular endothelial cells; fibroblasts; smooth muscle; cartilage, connective tissue and osteoblasts
Non-dividing (permanent)Considered incapable of division and regenerationNeurons, skeletal muscle cells, cardiac muscle cells

Thus, injury to permanent cells is followed by repair by fibrosis (scar) rather than regeneration.

Ref: V1-ch04

4 What is labile tissue? Give examples of labile tissue.
