Labile (continuously dividing) tissue: Tissues whose cells proliferate actively throughout life, replacing those that are destroyed; they are capable of good regeneration after injury because a resident progenitor/basal cell population is always dividing.

Examples:

  • Haematopoietic cells in the bone marrow
  • Stratified squamous epithelium of the skin, oral cavity, vagina and cervix
  • Cuboidal epithelium of ducts draining exocrine organs (salivary glands, pancreas, biliary tract)
  • Columnar epithelium of the gastrointestinal tract, uterus and fallopian tubes
  • Transitional epithelium of the urinary tract

Because regeneration is prompt and complete, wounds made up mainly of labile cells heal with minimal scarring when the stromal framework is preserved.

Ref: V1-ch04

5 What is permanent tissue? Give examples. How is injury of permanent tissue repaired?

Permanent (non-dividing) tissue: Tissues composed of cells that have been considered incapable of division and regeneration, i.e. they are in a terminally differentiated state and do not re-enter the cell cycle.

Examples: Neurons, skeletal muscle cells and cardiac muscle cells.

Repair of injury to permanent tissue: Since these cells cannot be replaced, the lost parenchyma is removed by phagocytosis and the defect is filled by deposition of connective tissue — repair by scarring (fibrosis). This is why infarction of the heart or brain and extensive neuronal loss are followed by permanent scars; the supporting structure must be intact for any restorative process, and mammalian regeneration here is essentially absent (limited stem/progenitor activity may contribute marginally, e.g. satellite cells of skeletal muscle).

Ref: V1-ch04

6 What is a stem cell? What are the peculiarities of stem cells? How do stem cells help in repair?

Stem cell: A cell characterised by self-renewal properties and the capacity to give rise to differentiated cells and tissues.

Peculiarities:

  • Self-renewal by asymmetric division (one daughter differentiates, the other retains stemness) or symmetric division (both daughters remain stem cells)
  • Two varieties: embryonic (ES) cells — totipotent/pluripotent inner cell mass cells with virtually limitless renewal; and tissue (adult) stem cells — usually produce only cells of their own tissue
  • Slow-cycling, kept in a protected niche, and activated only when needed

Role in repair: Resident stem/progenitor cells regenerate the damaged parenchyma — haematopoietic stem cells restore blood lineages; progenitor (oval) cells in the canals of Hering regenerate hepatocytes and biliary cells; intestinal crypt stem cells rebuild the crypt in 3–5 days; satellite cells repair skeletal muscle; limbal stem cells maintain corneal epithelium; epidermal/hair-follicle bulge cells restore skin.

Ref: V1-ch04

7 What are the tissues commonly repaired by regeneration? How does the liver regenerate after partial hepatectomy?

Tissues commonly repaired by regeneration: Tissues made of labile cells (bone marrow, skin and other squamous epithelia, GI and transitional epithelia) and stable cells whose supporting framework is intact (liver, kidney, pancreas parenchyma, vascular endothelium, fibroblasts and smooth muscle). If the stromal scaffold is destroyed, regeneration is replaced by scarring.

Liver regeneration after partial hepatectomy: The remaining hepatocytes are stable cells in G₀; they are primed (by TNF-α and IL-6 from Kupffer cells) and then stimulated by growth factors — especially hepatocyte growth factor (HGF) from fibroblasts, stromal and endothelial cells, and EGF — to re-enter the cell cycle and proliferate. Repeated division restores the original liver mass and architecture within about 1–2 weeks, without a need for scarring.

Ref: V1-ch04

8 When is scar formation needed in tissue repair? Enumerate the steps of scar formation.

When scar formation is needed: When the injured tissue is incapable of regeneration (permanent cells), or when the supporting structures of the tissue are too severely damaged to support regeneration of the parenchymal cells — e.g. lungs, liver, kidney, and myocardium after extensive ischaemic necrosis/infarction. Repair then occurs by laying down connective (fibrous) tissue, which may result in a scar.

Steps of scar formation:

  1. Inflammation: complement breakdown products, platelet chemokines and other mediators recruit neutrophils (6–48 h) then monocytes, which clear microbes and debris.
  2. Cell proliferation (up to 10 days): epithelial cells migrate to cover the wound, endothelial cells and pericytes form new vessels (angiogenesis), and fibroblasts migrate in and lay down collagen.
  3. Formation of granulation tissue: migrating/proliferating fibroblasts + loose connective tissue + new vessels + mononuclear leukocytes.
  4. Deposition of connective tissue and remodelling: progressive collagen accumulation converts granulation tissue into a stable fibrous scar.

Ref: V1-ch04

9 What is granulation tissue? How does it help in wound healing?

Granulation tissue: Migration and proliferation of fibroblasts together with deposition of loose connective tissue, newly formed blood vessels and interspersed leukocytes. It is immature mesenchymal (fibrovascular) tissue with inflammatory infiltrate — the "hallmark of tissue repair"; named for its pink, soft, granular gross appearance beneath the scab of a skin wound (unrelated to granulomas).

How it helps in wound healing:

  • Resorption: clears necrotic tissue, thrombi and fibrin
  • Tissue replacement: fills the defect and replaces lost skin/mucosa, later maturing into scar
  • Localisation: walls off infection (e.g. around an abscess); monocyte coating makes it resistant to infection
  • Its fibroblasts and collagen provide the scaffold that restores wound tensile strength

Ref: V1-ch04

10 Give the composition of granulation tissue. What is angiogenesis?

Composition of granulation tissue: Fibroblasts + newly formed blood vessels + extracellular matrix + neutrophils + macrophages + plasma cells + lymphocytes.

Angiogenesis: The process of new blood vessel development from existing vessels (sprouting), which is essential for healing of ischemic wounds.

Steps of angiogenesis:

  1. Vasodilation (nitric oxide) and increased permeability induced by VEGF
  2. Separation of pericytes from the abluminal surface and breakdown of the basement membrane to allow a vessel sprout to form
  3. Migration of endothelial cells toward the area of tissue injury
  4. Proliferation of endothelial cells just behind the migrating "tip"
  5. Remodelling into capillary tubes
  6. Recruitment of periendothelial cells (pericytes for capillaries, smooth muscle cells for larger vessels)
  7. Suppression of endothelial proliferation and deposition of the basement membrane to form a mature vessel

Ref: V1-ch04

11 Describe the gross and microscopic morphology of granulation tissue. How does granulation tissue become mature scar tissue?

Gross morphology: Pink, soft, granular surface beneath the scab; bleeds easily on touch (fragile new capillaries); insensible (no nerve supply); oedematous (leaky new vessels); progressively fills the site of injury; amount depends on defect size and intensity of inflammation; much more prominent in healing by secondary union.

Microscopic morphology: Proliferating fibroblasts depositing type 3 collagen; new thin-walled delicate capillaries (angiogenesis) in a loose extracellular matrix; admixed inflammatory cells, mainly macrophages; the new vessels are leaky so the tissue is often oedematous.

Maturation into scar: With time, collagen is progressively deposited and cross-linked, cellularity and vascularity decrease, and the loose granular tissue is replaced by a dense, pale, avascular, firm fibrous scar (white scar) covered by normal epidermis. Wound tensile strength then rises slowly over months, reaching roughly 70–80% of normal and persisting for life.

Ref: V1-ch04

12 What are the types of cutaneous wound healing? What type of wound commonly heals by primary intention?

Types of cutaneous wound healing: Based on the nature and size of the wound:

  1. Primary union (healing by first intention)
  2. Secondary union (healing by second intention)

(Three principal phases apply to both: inflammation; proliferation — granulation tissue formation and re-epithelialisation; and maturation — wound contraction, ECM deposition and tissue remodelling.)

Wounds that heal by primary intention: Clean, uninfected, incised wounds whose edges are approximated (e.g. surgically incised wounds closed with sutures), with little or no tissue loss, only focal disruption of the epithelial basement membrane, and minimum bleeding, exudate and debris. Here the principal mechanism of repair is epithelial regeneration, and healing is quick with a thin, fine scar.

Ref: V1-ch04

13 Differentiate between healing by first and second intention.

Healing by first intention: occurs when injury involves only the epithelial layer of a clean, approximated wound; repair is mainly epithelial regeneration. Second intention: occurs with extensive cell/tissue loss (large wounds, abscesses, ulcers, infarction), where repair is a combination of regeneration and scarring.

FeatureHealing by first intentionHealing by second intention
Wound marginsApproximatedSeparated
Tissue damage / inflammationMinimum damage, less intense inflammationMore extensive damage, more intense inflammation
Granulation tissueLessAbundant
Bleeding and exudationMinimumMarked
Healing time / scarHeals quickly; small scarSlow; large scar; wound contraction by myofibroblasts
Principal mechanismEpithelial regenerationCombination of regeneration and scarring
ExampleClean incised (surgical) woundLarge wounds, abscesses, ulcers, ischaemic necrosis (infarction)
High yield: "First = approximated, clean, quick, regeneration; second = gap, dirty, slow, granulation + contraction." Final wound tensile strength is only about 70–80% of normal in both.

Ref: V1-ch04

14 What are the systemic factors influencing wound healing? How does diabetes delay wound healing?

Systemic factors influencing wound healing:

  1. Nutrition: protein deficiency and particularly vitamin C deficiency inhibit collagen synthesis and retard healing.
  2. Metabolic status: diabetes mellitus is associated with delayed healing.
  3. Circulatory status: inadequate blood supply (poor perfusion from arteriosclerosis, diabetes) or obstructed venous drainage (varicose veins) impairs healing.
  4. Hormones: glucocorticoids (steroids) suppress inflammation and fibroplasia and inhibit collagen synthesis.

Why diabetes delays healing: hyperglycaemia causes non-enzymatic glycosylation of collagen (weak, poorly cross-linked fibres); diabetic microangiopathy and atherosclerosis reduce tissue perfusion and oxygen delivery; neutrophil and macrophage function (chemotaxis, phagocytosis) is impaired; and there is increased susceptibility to infection with neuropathy — all leading to dehiscence, chronic ulceration (diabetic foot) and poor scar formation.

Ref: V1-ch04

15 How does nutritional deficiency impair wound healing? Why do corticosteroids delay healing?

Nutritional deficiency:

  • Protein deficiency: reduces the amino-acid substrate for synthesis of collagen, ECM and repair proteins, giving weak repair, poor granulation tissue and delayed healing.
  • Vitamin C (ascorbic acid) deficiency: ascorbate is essential for hydroxylation and cross-linking of procollagen; its lack causes defective collagen → fragile wounds that fail to gain tensile strength (as in scurvy, with wound dehiscence and poor scarring).

Why corticosteroids delay healing: glucocorticoids have well-documented anti-inflammatory effects — they suppress neutrophil and monocyte/macrophage recruitment and function, so the essential inflammatory phase of healing is blunted ("No inflammation — no healing"); they also directly inhibit fibroblast proliferation, granulation tissue formation and collagen synthesis, predisposing to poor scarring and dehiscence.

Ref: V1-ch04

16 What are the local factors influencing wound healing? Why is the organ with a wound commonly immobilized?

Local factors influencing wound healing:

  1. Infection: causes delay through persistent tissue injury and inflammation (most common cause of delayed healing).
  2. Mechanical factors: early motion, or increased local pressure or torsion, may pull wounds apart.
  3. Foreign bodies: unnecessary sutures or fragments of steel, glass or bone impede healing.
  4. Size, location and type of wound: richly vascular areas (face) heal faster than poorly vascularized ones (foot); small incisional wounds heal faster with less scar than large excisional or blunt-trauma wounds.

Why immobilization: splinting/rest of the injured organ prevents mechanical forces (motion, pressure, torsion) from pulling the wound edges apart, protects the fragile new capillaries and the young collagen fibres, and allows alignment of collagen along tension lines — thereby shortening healing time and reducing scar formation.

Ref: V1-ch04

17 Describe with examples the conditions arising from deficient wound healing.

Deficient scar formation (defective healing — chronic wounds):

  1. Wound dehiscence: a surgical incision reopens internally or externally. Risk factors: obesity, malnutrition, infection, vascular insufficiency; in abdominal wounds precipitated by vomiting and coughing.
  2. Ulceration — failure of adequate vascularisation during healing:
    • Venous leg ulcers: chronic venous hypertension from varicose veins or congestive cardiac failure (poor oxygen delivery) — most common in the elderly
    • Arterial ulcers: peripheral atherosclerosis, especially with diabetes
    • Diabetic ulcers: feet — ischaemia, neuropathy, metabolic abnormality and secondary infection
    • Pressure sores: prolonged compression of tissue against bone (e.g. bedridden elderly) causing local ischaemia and necrosis
  3. Delayed/non-union of fractures is the bone equivalent of deficient healing.

Ref: V1-ch04

18 Describe the conditions arising from formation of excessive repair components.

Excessive formation of repair components (excessive scarring):

  1. Hypertrophic scar: raised scar from accumulation of excessive collagen, but remaining within the boundaries of the original wound; regresses within 6 months.
  2. Keloid: raised, tumorous scar growing beyond the boundaries of the original wound, which does not regress and may recur after excision; collagen is haphazard; predisposed genetically (more common in young adults and Black people), often at ear lobes, neck, shoulder, upper trunk; frequent after burns and tuberculous lesions.
  3. Exuberant granulation ("proud flesh"): excessive granulation tissue protruding above the skin surface and blocking re-epithelialisation.
  4. Desmoids / aggressive fibromatoses: recurring exuberant proliferation of fibroblasts and connective tissue after excision; intermediate between benign proliferation and malignant tumour.

Ref: V1-ch04

19 What is a contracture? How does it deform the wound area?

Contracture: Contraction of a wound is a normal part of healing (carried out by myofibroblasts, which pull the wound edges together and reduce the surface area). An exaggeration of this normal process is called a contracture.

How it deforms the wound area: excessive, unchecked myofibroblast contraction shrinks the scar and the surrounding skin beyond normal limits, drawing the wound margins and adjacent structures toward the centre. This distorts the wound and surrounding tissues, produces deformity, and — because contractures are especially prone to develop on the palms, soles and anterior aspect of the thorax (common after burns) — it can limit or compromise movement of nearby joints.

Ref: V1-ch04

EDEMA AND ELECTROLYTE IMBALANCE
20 Define edema and effusion. Differentiate between transudate and exudate.

Edema: Abnormal accumulation of fluid in the interstitial tissue spaces or body cavities.

Effusion: Abnormal accumulation of fluid in the body cavities — hydrothorax (pleural cavity), hydropericardium (pericardial cavity) and hydroperitoneum, i.e. ascites (peritoneal cavity). Normally hydrostatic pressure pushing fluid out is balanced by plasma colloid osmotic pressure pulling it back; when net filtration exceeds lymphatic drainage, fluid accumulates — as edema in tissues, or as effusion at a serosal surface.

FeatureExudateTransudate
CauseIncreased vascular permeability (inflammation)Increased hydrostatic pressure or decreased plasma colloid osmotic pressure
NatureInflammatoryNon-inflammatory
Total proteinHigh (>3 g/dl)Low (<1 g/dl)
Protein distributionAlbumin and globulinMostly albumin
FibrinogenPresent — clots spontaneouslyAbsent — no clot
Specific gravityHigh (>1.020)Low (<1.012)
Inflammatory cellsPlenty (neutrophils or lymphocytes)Absent

Ref: V1-ch05 + V1-ch03

21 Mention the causes of edema. How may liver failure cause edema?

Pathophysiologic categories (causes) of edema:

  1. Increased hydrostatic pressure: impaired venous return (congestive cardiac failure, constrictive pericarditis, ascites/cirrhosis, venous obstruction or compression by thrombus or mass, prolonged dependency of lower extremities) or arteriolar dilation (heat, neurohumoral dysregulation)
  2. Reduced plasma osmotic pressure (hypoproteinaemia): nephrotic syndrome, liver cirrhosis, malnutrition, protein-losing gastroenteropathy
  3. Lymphatic obstruction: inflammatory (filariasis), neoplastic (breast carcinoma — peau d'orange), post-surgical, post-irradiation
  4. Sodium and water retention: excess salt intake with renal insufficiency, increased tubular Na⁺ reabsorption (renal hypoperfusion, increased renin–angiotensin–aldosterone)
  5. Inflammation: acute, chronic and angiogenesis-driven exudative edema

In liver failure (cirrhosis): impaired albumin synthesis → hypoalbuminaemia → decreased plasma colloid osmotic pressure; plus sinusoidal/portal hypertension, increased hepatic lymph formation, and secondary hyperaldosteronism with Na⁺ and water retention → ascites and generalized edema.

Ref: V1-ch05

22 How does edema occur in heart failure? What is pitting edema?

Mechanism of edema in heart failure:

  1. Heart failure → increased central venous pressure → increased capillary hydrostatic pressure → increased transudation of fluid into interstitium → edema.
  2. Heart failure → decreased cardiac output → decreased renal blood flow → activation of the renin–angiotensin system (and ADH release) → retention of Na⁺ and H₂O → increased blood volume → increased plasma hydrostatic pressure → edema.

Right-sided heart failure causes peripheral edema; left-sided heart failure causes raised pulmonary venous pressure and pulmonary edema.

Pitting edema: Soft, boggy generalized edema in which firm pressure over a bony prominence (e.g. shin, sacrum, ankle) displaces the interstitial fluid and leaves a persistent "pit" or indentation after the finger is removed. It indicates excess interstitial fluid with normal tissue consistency — seen in congestive cardiac failure, cirrhosis, nephrotic syndrome and malnutrition.

Ref: V1-ch05

23 What are the causes of non-pitting edema? How may lymphatic obstruction cause edema?

Causes of non-pitting (brawny) edema:

  • Lymphoedema: filariasis (e.g. elephantiasis), inflammatory, neoplastic (post-mastectomy, post-irradiation of breast carcinoma), post-surgical or post-irradiation lymphatic obstruction
  • Myxedema — hypothyroidism (accumulation of hydrophilic glycosaminoglycans in dermis)
  • Pretibial myxedema, scleredema, amyloidosis

How lymphatic obstruction causes edema: lymphatics normally drain protein-rich fluid from the interstitium. When they are blocked (inflammatory, neoplastic, surgical or irradiation-related), this protein-rich fluid accumulates in the interstitium, raising tissue colloid osmotic pressure and drawing more fluid out of capillaries. Because the trapped fluid is protein-rich and later stimulates fibrosis, the tissue becomes firm and indurated — a hard, non-pitting edema. Example: carcinoma of the breast with lymphatic involvement produces skin edema dimpled by Cooper ligaments — the peau d'orange appearance.

Ref: V1-ch05

24 How does edema occur in renal failure? What are ascites and anasarca?

Mechanism of edema in renal failure:

  • Nephrotic syndrome: massive proteinuria → hypoproteinaemia → decreased plasma colloid osmotic pressure → decreased blood volume/cardiac output → decreased effective arterial volume → (i) increased renin–aldosterone secretion, (ii) renal vasoconstriction with decreased GFR and increased tubular Na⁺ reabsorption, (iii) increased ADH and decreased ANF → retention of Na⁺ and H₂O → increased plasma hydrostatic pressure → transudation → edema.
  • Acute glomerulonephritis: acutely decreased GFR → Na⁺ and water retention → edema.
  • Chronic glomerulonephritis: hypertension and heart failure → edema.

Edema of renal origin first appears in loose tissues — periorbital (eyelid) edema is characteristic.

Ascites: accumulation of fluid (hydroperitoneum) in the peritoneal cavity. Anasarca: severe, generalized edema with profound subcutaneous tissue swelling.

Ref: V1-ch05

25 How does plasma colloid osmotic pressure help to maintain body fluid? List the causes of reduced colloid osmotic pressure.

Role of plasma colloid osmotic (oncotic) pressure: Under normal circumstances, capillary hydrostatic pressure tends to push water and salts out into the interstitial space, while plasma colloid osmotic pressure (mainly due to albumin) opposes this by pulling water and salts back into the vessels. This Starling balance keeps most fluid intravascular; if net outward movement exceeds lymphatic drainage, edema (or effusion at serosal surfaces) results.

Causes of reduced plasma colloid osmotic pressure:

  • Protein-losing glomerulopathies — nephrotic syndrome (massive proteinuria)
  • Liver cirrhosis (decreased albumin synthesis)
  • Malnutrition (protein deficiency / kwashiorkor)
  • Protein-losing gastroenteropathy

Ref: V1-ch05

26 How may inflammation cause edema? Give examples of some inflammatory edema.

Mechanisms of inflammatory (exudative) edema:

  1. Increased vascular permeability: inflammation → alteration in vascular caliber and direct endothelial injury → exudation of protein-rich fluid → edema.
  2. Increased capillary hydrostatic pressure: inflammation → vasodilation → outflow of fluid into interstitium/body cavities → edema.
  3. Decreased plasma colloid osmotic pressure: exudation and leakage of protein from vessels → edema.
  4. Increased tissue colloid osmotic pressure: leaked protein in the interstitium retains fluid → edema.

Examples of inflammatory (exudative) edema: pneumonia, malignancy, pulmonary embolism, collagen vascular diseases (RA, SLE), pancreatitis, tuberculosis, viral infection; allergic edema such as urticaria and angioedema. Edema of inflamed tissue with increased vascular permeability is called protein-rich (exudative) edema.

Ref: V1-ch05

27 A 5-year-old girl presented with periorbital edema and proteinuria that gradually became generalized edema. What is the diagnosis? What is the mechanism of edema in this case?

Diagnosis: Nephrotic syndrome (in a child — classically minimal change disease) presenting with periorbital (facial) edema, heavy proteinuria and progressive generalized edema/anasarca. Periorbital edema is characteristic of renal-type edema because it appears first in loose connective tissue (eyelids).

Mechanism of edema: Injury to the glomerular filtration barrier → massive proteinuria → hypoproteinaemia/hypoalbuminaemia → decreased plasma colloid osmotic pressure → decreased blood volume and cardiac output → decreased effective arterial volume, which triggers (i) increased renin secretion → aldosterone → renal Na⁺ reabsorption, (ii) renal vasoconstriction with decreased GFR and increased tubular Na⁺/H₂O reabsorption, and (iii) increased ADH with decreased ANF. The resulting Na⁺ and water retention raises plasma volume and plasma hydrostatic pressure, so fluid transudes into the interstitial spaces → generalized pitting edema.

Ref: V1-ch05

28 A 65-year-old patient with a history of recurrent heart attacks developed pitting edema of both legs. He also developed dyspnea that is worse at night. What is the diagnosis? What is the mechanism of edema in this case? Why did respiratory distress occur in this patient?

Diagnosis: Congestive cardiac failure following repeated myocardial infarction — biventricular failure (left heart failure with pulmonary congestion plus right heart failure with peripheral edema). Paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea/orthopnea indicates pulmonary edema.

Mechanism of peripheral (pitting) edema: right-sided failure → increased central venous pressure → increased systemic capillary hydrostatic pressure → transudation of fluid into the interstitium of both legs; together with decreased cardiac output → decreased renal blood flow → activation of the renin–angiotensin system → Na⁺ and H₂O retention → increased blood volume → more edema.

Why respiratory distress (worse at night): recurrent MI has damaged the left ventricle → left heart failure → increased pulmonary venous pressure → pulmonary edema (fluid in interstitial/alveolar spaces impairs gas exchange). When lying supine at night, gravity-dependent venous return from the legs increases, further raising pulmonary capillary hydrostatic pressure and pulmonary blood volume, so fluid accumulates and dyspnea worsens (orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea).

Ref: V1-ch05

29 A patient of chronic hepatitis recently developed ascites with gradually increasing generalized edema. What is the cause of edema? What is the mechanism of edema in this case?

Cause: Chronic hepatitis → liver cirrhosis (hepatic failure with portal hypertension); the edema/effusion is transudative.

Mechanism: Five mechanisms act together —

  1. Sinusoidal hypertension: fibrosis raises sinusoidal pressure → fluid into the space of Disse → hepatic lymph overflow into the peritoneal cavity → ascites.
  2. Percolation of hepatic lymph: hepatic lymph flow rises far above thoracic duct capacity (20 L/day vs normal 800–1000 ml) → protein-rich lymph percolates into the peritoneal cavity.
  3. Intestinal fluid leakage: portal hypertension → increased perfusion pressure in intestinal capillaries → fluid escapes into the abdomen.
  4. Secondary hyperaldosteronism: impaired hepatic metabolism of aldosterone → renal Na⁺ and H₂O retention → increased plasma hydrostatic pressure → transudation.
  5. Hypoalbuminaemia: decreased albumin synthesis → decreased plasma colloid osmotic pressure → edema (ascites and generalized pitting edema).

Ref: V1-ch05

30 What are the common electrolyte disorders? What are the causes of hypernatremia?

Common electrolyte disorders: disorders of Na⁺ (hyponatraemia, hypernatraemia), K⁺ (hypokalaemia, hyperkalaemia), Cl⁻ (hypochloraemia, hyperchloraemia), HCO₃⁻ (often reflecting acid–base disturbance — metabolic acidosis/alkalosis), Ca²⁺ (hypocalcaemia, hypercalcaemia), Mg²⁺ and phosphate — plus acid–base imbalances. Normal serum levels: Na⁺ 135–145, K⁺ 3.6–5.1, Cl⁻ 95–107, HCO₃⁻ 21–29 mmol/L, Ca²⁺ 2.1–2.6 mmol/L (9–11 mg/dl).

Causes of hypernatraemia (>150 mmol/L; relatively greater water loss than sodium):

  1. Hypovolaemic: renal Na⁺ loss — osmotic/loop diuretics, glycosuria; gastrointestinal — colonic diarrhoea; skin — excessive sweating
  2. Euvolaemic (water deficit alone): diabetes insipidus (central or nephrogenic)
  3. Hypervolaemic (Na⁺ retention with relatively less water): enteral or parenteral alimentation, IV or oral salt administration, chronic renal failure during water restriction

Ref: V2-ch24

31 What is hyponatremia and hypernatremia? What are the causes of hyponatremia.

Hyponatraemia: Serum sodium below normal (<135 mmol/L). Features: often asymptomatic; anorexia, nausea and vomiting; confusion, lethargy; seizures, coma and death if severe.

Hypernatraemia: Serum sodium above normal (>150 mmol/L). Features: cloudiness, dizziness, disorientation, convulsions, confusion, weakness, coma and death.

Causes of hyponatraemia (sodium deficit or excess water):

  1. Hypovolaemic (sodium deficit with a smaller water deficit): renal Na⁺ loss — thiazide diuretics, adrenocortical failure; GI loss — vomiting, diarrhoea; skin loss — burns.
  2. Euvolaemic (water retention alone): primary polydipsia, excess electrolyte-free water infusion, syndrome of inappropriate ADH secretion (SIADH), hypothyroidism.
  3. Hypervolaemic (Na⁺ retention with greater water retention): congestive cardiac failure, cirrhosis of liver, nephrotic syndrome, chronic renal failure.

Ref: V2-ch24

32 What is hypokalemia? What are the common causes? How is it treated?

Hypokalaemia: Decreased plasma K⁺ level below 3.5 mmol/L. Clinical effects: muscular weakness, tiredness, flaccid paralysis, mental confusion, hypokalaemic nephropathy, functional bowel obstruction from paralytic ileus, cardiac arrhythmia and finally cardiac arrest. Typical ECG changes affect particularly the T wave (flattening/inversion). Diagnosis: plasma electrolytes, bicarbonate, urine potassium (± Ca²⁺, Mg²⁺).

Common causes: inadequate dietary intake; loss of K⁺ through the GI tract (severe vomiting, diarrhoea, nasogastric aspiration, intestinal fistula, bowel obstruction); alkalosis of any cause; endocrine disorders (Cushing's syndrome, Conn's syndrome); diuretic therapy; corticosteroid therapy; prolonged IV therapy; insulin excess; Mg²⁺ depletion.

Treatment: correct the underlying cause; replace K⁺ — oral potassium chloride preferred; IV KCl (dilute, slow, via central line with ECG monitoring) when severe or unable to eat; correct alkalosis and magnesium depletion; avoid rapid infusion, which can cause fatal cardiac arrest.

Ref: V2-ch24

33 What is hyperkalemia? Mention the causes and the treatment.

Hyperkalaemia: Increased plasma K⁺ above the upper limit of normal (>5 mmol/L); severe hyperkalaemia occurs above 6.5 mmol/L and can cause cardiac arrhythmia. Clinical features: progressive muscular weakness, paralysis and diastolic cardiac arrest.

Causes: excessive IV K⁺ infusion; acute or chronic renal failure; acidosis of any cause; cellular K⁺ leakage in diabetic ketoacidosis; acute starvation; gross haemorrhage; dehydration; adrenocortical failure (Addison's disease); drugs (NSAIDs, ACE inhibitors, β-blockers, heparin, spironolactone); insulin deficiency with severe hyperglycaemia; severe tissue injury (major RTA, bomb blast, massive haemolysis, chemotherapy, severe burns).

Treatment: stop K⁺-containing fluids/drugs and treat the cause (renal failure → dialysis); IV calcium gluconate to stabilise the cardiac membrane; insulin + glucose, salbutamol nebuliser and sodium bicarbonate to shift K⁺ into cells; loop diuretics or potassium binders to remove K⁺; dialysis if life-threatening.

High yield: ECG is the bedside guide — hyperkalaemia: peaked T wave first, then widened QRS presaging arrhythmia; hypokalaemia: flattened T wave. "Hyper = more K⁺ = tight heart; hypo = less K⁺ = floppy muscles."

Ref: V2-ch24

GENERAL PATHOLOGY — Hyperemia, Congestion, Hemorrhage & Shock · Thrombosis, Embolism & Infarction
HYPEREMIA, CONGESTION, HEMORRHAGE & SHOCK
1 What is hyperemia and congestion? What are the differences between hyperemia and congestion?

Definitions: Hyperemia is an active process in which arteriolar dilation leads to increased blood flow into the tissue, so the affected part turns red (erythema). Congestion is a passive process in which there is a local increased blood volume resulting from reduced outflow of blood from a tissue.

FeatureHyperemiaCongestion
NatureActive process (arteriolar dilation)Passive process (impaired venous drainage)
CauseIncreased inflow of bloodDiminished outflow of blood
Blood in tissueEngorged with oxygenated bloodSwollen with deoxygenated venous blood
ColourRedness / erythemaDusky reddish-blue / cyanosis
ExamplesSites of inflammation; skeletal muscle during exerciseCardiac failure; isolated venous obstruction

Ref: V1-ch05

2 Give the mechanisms of hyperemia and congestion. Give examples of hyperemia.

Mechanism of hyperemia: An active arteriolar dilation increases inflow of arterial blood. In inflammation, local mediators (histamine, kinins, nitric oxide, prostacyclin) dilate arterioles; in exercise, metabolites and neural reflexes act similarly. The tissue becomes engorged with bright oxygenated blood.

Mechanism of congestion: Impaired venous outflow causes pooling of blood, rise in hydrostatic pressure and stasis of deoxygenated blood, leading to edema; chronic hypoxia causes ischemic injury and scarring, capillary rupture causes small haemorrhagic foci, and phagocytosis of extravasated red cells produces haemosiderin-laden macrophages.

Examples of hyperemia:

  • Sites of inflammation — rubor (redness) is a cardinal sign
  • Skeletal muscle during exercise (active hyperemia)
  • Skin exposed to heat / blushing (neural and axon-reflex hyperemia)
  • Alimentary tract after a meal (digestive hyperemia)

Ref: V1-ch05

3 Give examples of congestion. How brown induration of lung occurs?

Examples of congestion:

  • Systemic congestion: cardiac failure (left ventricular failure involves the lung; right heart failure involves the liver)
  • Localized congestion: isolated venous obstruction (thrombosis or external compression by a mass)
  • Acute congestion: acute left ventricular failure (lung), acute right-sided heart failure (liver)
  • Chronic congestion: rheumatic mitral stenosis (lung), chronic right-sided heart failure (liver)

Brown induration of lung: It is the firm, brown lung produced by long-standing chronic passive congestion due to left ventricular failure, mitral stenosis, myocarditis or cardiomyopathy.

  1. Pulmonary venous congestion → alveolar capillaries engorged, alveolar septal edema and focal intra-alveolar haemorrhage
  2. Distended capillaries rupture → minute intra-alveolar haemorrhage → red cells broken down
  3. Red cell debris is phagocytosed → haemosiderin-laden macrophages, the "heart failure cells", appear in alveolar spaces
  4. In long-standing congestion the septa become fibrotic and brown due to haemosiderin pigment → brown induration of lung

Ref: V1-ch05

4 What is nutmeg liver? Describe the pathogenesis.

Definition: Nutmeg liver is the gross appearance of chronic passive hepatic congestion, in which the centrilobular regions are grossly red-brown and slightly depressed (because of cell death) and are accentuated against the surrounding zones of uncongested tan liver — so called because the cut surface resembles a nutmeg with alternate red-brown and pale areas.

Pathogenesis:

  1. Chronic right heart failure or hepatic vein obstruction → chronic passive congestion of the liver
  2. Congestion of blood in the central vein of the hepatic lobules → central hepatocytes severely hypoxic → central haemorrhagic (centrilobular) necrosis → red-brown, depressed zones
  3. Peripheral hepatocytes develop fatty change due to mild hypoxia → pale zones
  4. Alternating red-brown and pale areas resembling a nutmeg → nutmeg liver

Microscopy: centrilobular haemorrhage, haemosiderin-laden macrophages and variable hepatocyte dropout/necrosis, with peripheral fatty change. Long-standing severe congestion may cause "cardiac cirrhosis".

Ref: V1-ch05

5 What are the clinical presentations of abnormal bleeding? What are the conditions where both BT and CT are prolonged?

Clinical presentations of abnormal bleeding:

  • Spontaneous bleeding into skin, mucous membranes and internal tissues
  • Excessive or prolonged bleeding following trauma or surgery
  • Bleeding from more than one site
  • Pattern: superficial mucocutaneous bleeding (petechiae, purpura, ecchymoses, epistaxis, menorrhagia, gum bleeding) indicates a vascular or platelet disorder; deep bleeding (haematoma, haemarthrosis, re-bleeding after procedures) indicates a coagulation factor disorder

Conditions in which both BT and CT are prolonged:

  • DIC — platelets and clotting factors are consumed, so BT and CT (as well as PT and APTT) rise
  • Von Willebrand disease — BT is prolonged because of defective platelet adhesion (low vWF), and CT is prolonged because of low factor VIII

Remember: BT depends on platelet count, platelet function and vessel wall integrity; CT depends on clotting factors.

Ref: V1-ch20

6 How a case of abnormal bleeding is investigated in the lab?

Approach: clinical evaluation (history taking and physical examination) followed by staged laboratory investigations to decide whether the defect is vascular, platelet or coagulation-related.

First-line (screening) tests:

  1. Prothrombin time (PT) — extrinsic pathway, normal 9–12 seconds
  2. Activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) — intrinsic pathway, normal 32–40 seconds
  3. Thrombin time (TT) — normal 10–14 seconds
  4. Full blood count, platelet count (normal 1.5–4 lakh/mm³) and examination of blood film

Second-line tests: bleeding time (Ivy/template, normal under 6 minutes), tourniquet (Hess) test, specific platelet function tests, specific factor assay, thromboplastin generation time, clot solubility test and estimation of fibrin degradation products (D-dimer).

ConditionBTCTPT / APTTPlatelet count
Thrombocytopenia (ITP)ProlongedNormalNormalReduced
HaemophiliaNormalGreatly prolongedAPTT prolonged, PT normalNormal
DICProlongedProlongedBoth prolongedReduced

Ref: V1-ch20

7 Define and classify shock. Mention the stages of shock.

Definition: Shock is a state of circulatory failure that impairs tissue perfusion and leads to cellular hypoxia — i.e. systemic hypoperfusion caused by reduction either in cardiac output or in the effective circulating blood volume.

Classification with causes:

  1. Cardiogenic shock — myocardial infarction, arrhythmia, pulmonary embolism, cardiac tamponade, ventricular rupture
  2. Hypovolemic shock — fluid loss, e.g. haemorrhage, vomiting, diarrhoea, burns, trauma
  3. Septic shock — overwhelming bacterial and fungal infections; superantigens (toxic shock syndrome)
  4. Neurogenic shock — spinal cord injury, anaesthetic accident
  5. Anaphylactic shock — generalized IgE-mediated (type I) hypersensitivity, e.g. penicillin, bee sting

Stages of shock:

  1. Initial non-progressive (compensated) stage: reflex compensatory mechanisms maintain perfusion of vital organs
  2. Progressive stage: tissue hypoperfusion with circulatory and metabolic imbalance — lactic acidosis, falling urine output, confusion; compensation is no longer adequate
  3. Irreversible stage: organ damage and metabolic disturbances so severe that survival is impossible even if hemodynamics are corrected — decreased myocardial contractility, multiorgan failure, DIC, renal shutdown

Ref: V1-ch05

8 What are the reflexes that maintain perfusion during early stages of shock? What are the clinical features of shock?

Reflexes maintaining perfusion in early (non-progressive) shock:

  • Baroreceptor reflexes → increased heart rate and increased peripheral resistance
  • Activation of the renin–angiotensin axis (with aldosterone) → renal sodium and water retention
  • Release of antidiuretic hormone (ADH / vasopressin)
  • Release of catecholamines (noradrenaline, adrenaline) and generalized sympathetic stimulation
  • Supporting mechanisms: tachycardia, vasoconstriction, venoconstriction, tachypnea (thoracic pumping), skeletal muscle pumping, and shift of interstitial fluid into capillaries

Clinical features:

  • In hypovolemic and cardiogenic shock: hypotension, weak rapid pulse, tachypnea, and cool, clammy, cyanotic skin
  • In septic and anaphylactic shock: skin is warm and flushed due to peripheral vasodilation
  • Progressive stage: confusion, oliguria, metabolic acidosis from lactic acidemia
  • Late: multiorgan failure, DIC, coma and death; prognosis depends on cause and duration

Ref: V1-ch05

9 Mention the etiology and pathogenesis of cardiogenic shock.

Etiology: myocardial infarction (most common cause), malignant arrhythmias, pulmonary embolism, cardiac tamponade, ventricular rupture, acute severe valvular regurgitation, myocarditis and cardiomyopathy.

Pathogenesis:

  1. Loss of functioning myocardium (e.g. extensive left ventricular infarction) → markedly reduced cardiac output despite an adequate blood volume
  2. Reduced perfusion → hypotension; compensatory sympathetic activation causes tachycardia and peripheral vasoconstriction → cool, clammy, cyanotic skin
  3. Backward failure → raised filling pressures → pulmonary congestion and pulmonary edema
  4. Falling coronary perfusion plus catecholamine stress cause further ischemic myocardial injury — a self-aggravating cycle
  5. When compensation fails: lactic acidosis, oliguria, confusion → progressive stage → irreversible stage with multiorgan failure and DIC

Ref: V1-ch05

10 Mention the etiology and pathogenesis of hypovolemic shock.

Etiology: any profound loss of effective circulating volume — severe haemorrhage (road traffic accident, gastrointestinal bleeding, ruptured ectopic pregnancy), severe burns, severe vomiting and diarrhoea, and third-spacing of fluid.

Pathogenesis — reversible shock:

  1. Severe haemorrhage, burn, vomiting or diarrhoea → acute hypovolemia
  2. Decreased central venous pressure → decreased cardiac filling (end-diastolic volume)
  3. Decreased cardiac output → decreased arterial pressure
  4. Compensatory autoregulation (baroreceptor reflex, catecholamines, renin–angiotensin, ADH) restores pressure → reversible stage

Pathogenesis — irreversible shock: severe hypovolemia not corrected → compensation fails → tissue hypoperfusion and acute hypoxia → endothelial injury activates the coagulation cascade → DIC; anaerobic glycolysis → low pH → vasodilation → further fall in blood pressure; severe hypoxia → cell damage, acute renal tubular necrosis, complete renal shutdown → death.

Ref: V1-ch05

11 Mention the etiology and pathogenesis of septic shock.

Etiology: endotoxin-producing Gram-negative bacteria (E. coli, Proteus, Pseudomonas), Gram-positive bacteria, fungi, and superantigens (e.g. toxic shock syndrome).

Pathogenesis:

  1. Endotoxin (cell wall lipopolysaccharide) is released when organisms degrade; free LPS binds circulating LPS-binding protein
  2. The complex binds the CD14 receptor on monocytes/macrophages, endothelial cells and other cells
  3. Cascade of cytokines (TNF, IL-1, IL-6, chemokines), complement, nitric oxide and prostaglandins is initiated
  4. Systemic vasodilation → hypotension; diminished myocardial contractility and cardiac output; increased vascular permeability and endothelial injury → ARDS
  5. Activation of coagulation → DIC → hypoperfusion → multiple organ system failure (kidney, liver, lungs, heart)
  6. Generalized organ dysfunction = septic shock (mortality about 20%)

Ref: V1-ch05

12 Mention the etiology and pathogenesis of neurogenic shock.

Etiology: spinal cord injury (especially above T6), anaesthetic accident (spinal/general anaesthesia), severe head injury — any abrupt loss of central sympathetic vasomotor control.

Pathogenesis:

  1. Loss of sympathetic vasomotor tone → sudden widespread arteriolar and venous dilation
  2. Decreased systemic vascular resistance and decreased venous return → reduced cardiac output
  3. Hypotension and inadequate perfusion of vital organs; baroreceptor compensation is blunted, so reflex tachycardia may be absent and bradycardia may occur
  4. Because vessels are dilated rather than constricted, the skin is characteristically warm and flushed ("distributive" or warm shock), unlike the cold clammy skin of hypovolemic and cardiogenic shock
  5. Prolonged hypoperfusion → metabolic acidosis, DIC and multiorgan failure if untreated

Ref: V1-ch05

13 Mention the etiology and pathogenesis of anaphylactic shock.

Etiology: a generalized IgE-mediated (type I) hypersensitivity response — drugs such as penicillin, bee sting, food allergens, latex and contrast media in a sensitized individual.

Pathogenesis:

  1. Allergen cross-links IgE on mast cells and basophils → degranulation releasing histamine, leukotrienes, prostaglandins and platelet-activating factor
  2. Systemic arteriolar vasodilation → sudden hypotension
  3. Markedly increased vascular permeability → edema, urticaria, angioedema and further fall in blood pressure
  4. Bronchial smooth muscle contraction → bronchospasm, wheeze and respiratory distress
  5. Untreated, distributive shock progresses to circulatory collapse, tissue hypoxia and death

Clinical features: urticaria, angioedema, hoarseness, wheeze, vomiting and hypotension with warm flushed skin.

Ref: V1-ch05

14 What is DIC? Mention common causes of DIC. How it is diagnosed in the lab?

Definition: Disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC) is a haemorrhagic disorder in which diffuse intravascular clotting causes a haemostatic defect resulting from the utilization (consumption) of coagulation factors and platelets in the clotting process. Synonyms: consumption coagulopathy, defibrination syndrome.

Common causes:

  • Acute: obstetric accidents (abruptio placentae, amniotic fluid embolism, abortion), heart and lung surgery, haemolytic transfusion reaction, Gram-negative and meningococcal septicaemia, pulmonary embolism, snake bite, hypersensitivity reactions, heat stroke
  • Subacute/chronic: disseminated or localized carcinoma, acute leukaemia (promyelocytic), fetal death in utero, purpura fulminans, giant haemangioma, septicaemia

Laboratory diagnosis: platelet count reduced, fibrinogen reduced, factor assay reduced; whole blood clotting time, BT, PT, APTT and thrombin time all prolonged; FDPs such as D-dimer increased in serum and urine; blood film shows microangiopathic haemolysis with fragmented red cells.

High yield: In DIC every test is prolonged EXCEPT platelet count and coagulation-factor levels, both of which fall — "times up, platelets and fibrinogen down".

Ref: V1-ch20

15 Give the pathogenesis of DIC. What is D dimer?

Pathogenesis: The key event is increased activity of thrombin in the circulation that overwhelms its normal rate of removal by natural anticoagulants. DIC is triggered by entry of procoagulant material (trauma, amniotic fluid embolism, placental separation, mucin-secreting adenocarcinoma, acute promyelocytic leukaemia, falciparum malaria, transfusion reaction, snake bite) or by widespread endothelial damage and collagen exposure (endotoxaemia, septicaemia, viral infections, burns, hypothermia).

  1. Widespread activation of coagulation → microthrombi in the circulation
  2. Consumption of clotting factors and platelets → generalized haemorrhage
  3. Secondary fibrinolysis → fibrin degradation products increased → further inhibits fibrin polymerization and worsens bleeding
  4. Fibrin strands shear red cells → microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia; organ dysfunction (lung, liver, kidney, brain) perpetuates a vicious circle

D-dimer: a fibrin degradation product formed when plasmin digests cross-linked fibrin; it is measured in serum and urine and is raised in DIC, indicating that both coagulation and fibrinolysis have been activated.

Ref: V1-ch20

THROMBOSIS, EMBOLISM & INFARCTION
16 What is thrombosis? How abnormal blood flow can generate thrombus?

Definition: Thrombosis is the formation of a solid or semisolid mass from the constituents of blood within the vascular system during life — in other words, intravascular coagulation of blood, often causing significant interruption of blood flow.

How abnormal blood flow generates a thrombus:

  • Stasis (the major contributor to venous thrombi): stasis allows clotting factors to accumulate unhindered by inflow of fresh plasma, favors activation of coagulation, increases contact of platelets with the endothelium, and produces endothelial hypoxia and injury.
  • Turbulence: causes endothelial injury and dysfunction and creates countercurrents and local pockets of stasis; it also increases platelet delivery to and contact with the vessel wall — the basis of arterial and cardiac thrombosis.

Examples: atrial fibrillation and bed rest (stasis); ulcerated atherosclerotic plaques, aneurysms and mitral stenosis (turbulence/stasis); polycythaemia and hyperviscosity (slowed flow).

Ref: V1-ch05

17 Give the pathogenesis of thrombosis. Draw and label Virchow's triad of thrombosis.

Pathogenesis: A thrombus forms when the balance between prothrombotic and antithrombotic forces tips towards coagulation on the flowing column of blood — usually after endothelial injury or activation, with platelet adhesion, tissue factor release and thrombin generation leading to a platelet-fibrin mass.

Virchow's triadMechanismExamples
1. Endothelial injury (most important)Exposes subendothelial ECM and tissue factor → platelet adhesion and aggregation, with local depletion of PGI2 and plasminogen activatorsMI, vasculitis, hypertension, dyslipidemia, smoking, homocystinuria, endotoxins
2. Stasis or turbulent blood flowStasis → pooling of clotting factors and endothelial hypoxia; turbulence → endothelial injury plus local pockets of stasisAtrial fibrillation, bed rest, atherosclerotic narrowing, aneurysm, mitral stenosis
3. HypercoagulabilityAbnormally high tendency of blood to clot from alterations in coagulation factorsFactor V / prothrombin mutations, disseminated cancer, pregnancy, OCP
High yield: Virchow's triad — endothelial injury, stasis/turbulent flow, hypercoagulability. Endothelial injury dominates in heart and arteries; stasis dominates in veins.

Ref: V1-ch05

18 How endothelial injury can generate thrombus? Mention the fates of thrombus.

Endothelial injury → thrombus: Injury exposes subendothelial extracellular matrix → platelets adhere (via vWF) and aggregate, tissue factor (factor III) is released, and local prostacyclin and plasminogen activators are depleted → platelet plug forms and the coagulation cascade is activated → fibrin deposition → thrombus. Endothelial activation by inflammatory cytokines additionally downregulates thrombomodulin, protein C and t-PA and increases PAI, reinforcing the procoagulant state. This is the dominant influence in the heart and arterial circulation, where high flow rates would otherwise impede clotting.

Fates of a thrombus:

  1. Propagation — accumulates additional platelets and fibrin, eventually causing vessel obstruction
  2. Embolization — dislodges and travels to other sites in the vasculature
  3. Dissolution — fibrinolysis causes rapid shrinkage and total disappearance of recent thrombi
  4. Organization and recanalization — ingrowth of endothelial cells, smooth muscle cells and fibroblasts; may re-establish vascular flow or be incorporated into the vessel wall

Ref: V1-ch05

19 What is hypercoagulability? What are the conditions that may lead to hypercoagulability?

Definition: Hypercoagulability is an abnormally high tendency of the blood to clot, typically caused by alterations in coagulation factors — the third component of Virchow's triad.

TypeConditions
Primary (genetic)Mutation in factor V gene and prothrombin gene; deficiency of antithrombin III, protein C and protein S; fibrinolysis defects; homozygous homocystinuria
Secondary (acquired) — strong risk factorsProlonged bed rest or immobilization (post-surgery), myocardial infarction, atrial fibrillation, tissue damage (surgery, fracture, burns), cancer, prosthetic cardiac valves, DIC, heparin-induced thrombocytopenia, antiphospholipid antibody syndrome
Secondary (acquired) — other risk factorsCardiomyopathy, nephrotic syndrome, hyperestrogenic states (pregnancy and postpartum), oral contraceptive use, sickle cell anemia, smoking

Ref: V1-ch05

20 What is thrombus? What are the morphological types of thrombus? How thrombus can be differentiated from post mortem clot?

Definition: A thrombus is a solid or semisolid mass formed within the non-interrupted cardiovascular system from the constituents of streaming blood during life.

Morphological types of thrombus:

  • By site: arterial (on ulcerated atheromatous plaque, Buerger disease, aneurysm — laminated), venous (phlebothrombosis, thrombophlebitis), cardiac (vegetations, mural, laminated, ball thrombus)
  • By lumen: occlusive (completely occludes the lumen) and mural (attached to the wall without occluding it)
  • By infection: bland/aseptic and septic/infected
  • By structure: laminated thrombi show lines of Zahn — alternating pale platelet-fibrin layers and dark red-cell-rich layers, signifying formation in flowing blood
FeatureAntemortem thrombusPostmortem clot
FormationFormed during life in streaming bloodFormed after death by mere clotting
AttachmentAttached to the wall; rough surface when pulled outNot attached; leaves a smooth surface
Lines of ZahnPresentAbsent
AppearanceFirm, friable and dry; propagated part red and soft"Chicken fat" plasma layer over "red current jelly" red cells; rubbery and moist

Ref: V1-ch05

21 Give the etiology and pathogenesis of venous and arterial thrombosis. How mural thrombi are formed?

Arterial thrombosis: arises at sites of endothelial injury or turbulence (ulcerated atherosclerotic plaque, myocardial infarction, aneurysm, trauma, thromboangiitis obliterans). Exposed subendothelium and tissue factor trigger platelet adhesion and aggregation, producing a pale, platelet-rich thrombus that grows against the direction of flow; it is frequently occlusive and occurs in coronary, cerebral and femoral arteries.

Venous thrombosis (phlebothrombosis): stasis is the most important factor; occurs in veins of the lower limb (90% of cases), upper limb, prostatic plexus, ovarian and periuterine veins, portal and hepatic veins and dural sinuses. Sequence: primary platelet thrombus → coralline (mixed) thrombus → occluding thrombus → consecutive and propagated thrombus. Venous thrombi are dark red, RBC-rich, loosely attached and almost invariably occlusive, so they readily embolize to the lungs.

Mural thrombi: when an arterial-type thrombus arises in the heart chambers or the aortic lumen it adheres to the wall of the underlying structure without completely occluding the lumen. Causes — heart: MI, atrial fibrillation, dilated cardiomyopathy; aorta: ulcerated atherosclerotic plaque, aneurysmal dilatation. Mural thrombi are the main source of systemic emboli.

Ref: V1-ch05

22 Define and classify embolism. Give the pathogenesis of thromboembolism.

Definition: Embolism is the obstruction of a vessel caused by an embolus — a detached intravascular solid, liquid or gaseous mass that is carried by the blood from its point of origin to a distant site, where it often causes tissue dysfunction or infarction.

Classification (by composition):

  1. Solid: thromboembolism (pulmonary or systemic — most frequent form), fat embolism, atherosclerotic debris (cholesterol emboli), tumour fragments, bone fragments, bone marrow, foreign bodies
  2. Liquid: amniotic fluid embolism
  3. Gaseous: bubbles of air or nitrogen (air embolism)

Pathogenesis of thromboembolism: a thrombus forms (usually in deep leg veins), a fragment detaches, and it is carried through progressively larger channels and through the right side of the heart into the pulmonary vasculature — or, arising in the left heart or aorta, into the systemic arterial circulation. On reaching a vessel narrower than the embolus it impacts and obstructs flow, producing distal ischemia and infarction; a prior pulmonary embolus puts the patient at high risk of further emboli.

Ref: V1-ch05

23 What is an embolus? Mention the types of embolus. What is paradoxical embolus?

Definition: An embolus is a detached intravascular solid, liquid or gaseous mass carried by the blood from its point of origin to a distant site, where it often causes tissue dysfunction or infarction.

Types of embolus:

  • Solid: thromboembolism (most common), fat (fracture of long bones), atherosclerotic/cholesterol debris, tumour fragments, bone fragments, bone marrow, foreign bodies (e.g. bullet)
  • Liquid: amniotic fluid embolism
  • Gaseous: air or nitrogen bubbles

Paradoxical embolus: a left-sided embolus that originates in the venous circulation but gains access to the arterial circulation through a right-to-left shunt — most often a patent foramen ovale or an atrial septal defect — and therefore lodges in systemic arteries.

Ref: V1-ch05

24 Give the etiology, pathogenesis and fates of pulmonary thromboembolism.

Etiology: deep venous thrombosis of large leg veins at or above the knee (popliteal, femoral and iliac veins) in more than 95% of instances; also pelvic and upper-limb veins. Key risk factor: prolonged immobilization (with bed rest, surgery, heart failure, trauma, burns, pregnancy, tumour and advanced age).

Pathogenesis: venous embolus from deep leg vein thrombi is carried through progressively larger channels, through the right heart into the pulmonary vasculature; depending on size it occludes the main pulmonary artery, impacts across the bifurcation (saddle embolus), or passes into smaller branching arteries. Multiple emboli frequently arise from a single large mass.

Fates / clinical effects:

  1. 60–80% of emboli are small and clinically silent
  2. Obstruction of 60% or more of the pulmonary circulation → sudden death, right heart failure (cor pulmonale) or cardiovascular collapse
  3. Obstruction of medium-sized arteries → pulmonary haemorrhage (dual blood supply prevents infarction)
  4. Obstruction of small end-arteriolar branches → haemorrhage or infarction
  5. Recurrent emboli over time → pulmonary hypertension and right ventricular failure

Ref: V1-ch05

25 Give the etiology, pathogenesis and fates of systemic thromboembolism.

Etiology / sources: systemic emboli are emboli travelling within the arterial circulation. About 80% arise from intracardiac mural thrombi — two-thirds associated with left ventricular wall infarcts and another one-fourth with left atrial dilation and fibrillation. The remainder originate from aortic aneurysms, atherosclerotic plaques, valvular vegetations, or venous thrombi (paradoxical emboli); 10–15% are of unknown origin.

Pathogenesis: the embolus arises in the left heart or aorta, moves with the arterial stream and lodges where the vessel narrows — most often in the lower extremities — obstructing flow and producing ischaemic necrosis (usually a white infarct) in the territory of an end artery.

Sites of lodgment and fates:

  1. Lower extremities (75%) — peripheral vascular disease and gangrene may develop
  2. Brain (10%) — middle cerebral artery obstruction may cause death or stroke
  3. Infarction of intestine, kidneys and spleen
  4. Upper extremities (to a lesser extent)

Ref: V1-ch05

26 Give the etiology and pathogenesis of amniotic fluid embolism. What is decompression sickness?

Amniotic fluid embolism — etiology: a complication of labour and delivery.

Pathogenesis: infusion of amniotic fluid or fetal tissue into the maternal circulation through a tear in the placental membranes or rupture of uterine veins (forced in by strong uterine contractions). The debris embolizes to the lungs and strongly activates coagulation and inflammatory mediators.

Clinical features: (i) sudden severe dyspnoea, cyanosis and shock; (ii) neurologic impairment (headache to seizures and coma); (iii) pulmonary edema; (iv) disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC).

Decompression sickness: a particular form of gas embolism occurring when individuals are exposed to sudden changes in atmospheric pressure — deep-sea and scuba divers, underwater construction workers, and unpressurized aircraft on rapid ascent. Air breathed at high pressure dissolves more nitrogen; if decompression is too rapid, nitrogen comes out of solution in tissues and blood as bubbles that obstruct vessels and cause ischemic injury.

  • Bends — gas bubbles in skeletal muscles and about joints → painful condition
  • Chokes — pulmonary edema, haemorrhage and focal atelectasis/emphysema → respiratory distress
  • Caisson disease (chronic form) — persistent emboli cause ischemic necrosis of femoral heads, tibia and humeri

Ref: V1-ch05

27 Mention the various forms of air embolism. What are the causes of marrow embolism?

Forms of air embolism:

  1. Venous air embolism — air enters the venous circulation during obstetric or laparoscopic procedures, chest wall injury or haemodialysis; gas bubbles obstruct vascular flow and cause distal ischemic injury
  2. Arterial air embolism — air reaches the arterial side (often through a right-to-left shunt) causing focal ischemia of brain and heart
  3. Decompression sickness (nitrogen gas embolism) — "chokes" (lungs), "bends" (muscles and joints), and chronic caisson disease with ischemic necrosis of bone. At least about 100 cc of air is required to produce air embolism

Causes of marrow embolism:

  • Fracture of long bones containing fatty marrow (commonest cause, e.g. femoral fracture)
  • Chest wall trauma and cardiopulmonary resuscitation / chest compressions
  • Orthopedic operations — intramedullary nailing, joint replacement and other bone surgery
  • Injury to red-marrow-containing bones (sternum, vertebrae, ribs)

Marrow emboli are a solid type of embolus and commonly accompany fat embolism after skeletal injury.

Ref: V1-ch05

28 Define infarction and infarct. Classify infarct with examples.

Definition: An infarct is an area of ischemic necrosis caused by occlusion of either the arterial supply or the venous drainage; infarction is the process by which it is produced.

Common sites / examples: heart (myocardial infarction), brain (cerebral infarction), lung (pulmonary infarction), bowel, and ischemic necrosis of the extremities (gangrene).

Classification:

  1. On the basis of colour (amount of haemorrhage):
    • Red (haemorrhagic) infarct — with venous occlusion (e.g. testicular torsion), in loose spongy tissue or tissues with dual circulation (lung, small intestine), previously congested tissue, or after re-established flow (e.g. post-angioplasty); also ovary and brain after haemorrhagic transformation
    • White (anaemic) infarct — with arterial occlusion in solid organs with end-arterial circulation, e.g. heart, spleen, kidney
  2. On the basis of infection: septic infarct (bacterial infection present in the infarcted area, e.g. from a septic embolus) and bland infarct (no infection)

Ref: V1-ch05

29 Mention the causes of infarction. What are the factors affecting the development of an infarct?

Causes of infarction:

  • Thromboembolism (99%)
  • Local vasospasm or traumatic vascular rupture
  • Obstructed hernia
  • Testicular torsion
  • Bowel volvulus

Factors affecting development of an infarct:

  1. Anatomy of the vascular supply — organs with dual blood supply (lungs, liver, extremities) are less vulnerable, whereas end-arterial supply (kidney, spleen) predisposes
  2. Rate of development of occlusion — slowly developing occlusions are less likely to infarct because time allows alternative perfusion (e.g. collateral coronary circulation)
  3. Tissue vulnerability to hypoxia — neurons undergo irreversible damage after 3–4 minutes of ischemia; myocardial cells die after 20–30 minutes; fibroblasts remain viable for many hours
  4. Hypoxemia — abnormally low blood oxygen content (from any cause) increases both the likelihood and the extent of infarction

Ref: V1-ch05

30 Name the tissue more vulnerable to develop infarct with the explanation. Mention the fates of an infarct.

Most vulnerable tissue — brain: neurons undergo irreversible injury after only 3–4 minutes of ischemia, so the brain infarcts fastest and shows liquefactive necrosis. Myocardium is the next most sensitive (myocardial cells die after 20–30 minutes of ischemia). Tissues with end-arterial, non-collateral supply and high oxygen extraction (kidney, spleen) are also vulnerable, whereas organs with dual circulation (lung, liver, intestine) are protected; hypoxemia and a rapid (rather than gradual) occlusion increase vulnerability further.

Fates of an infarct:

  • Ischaemic coagulative necrosis in most tissues (except brain), followed by an inflammatory reaction and fibroblastic response at the periphery and eventual scar formation
  • Liquefactive necrosis in the brain, leaving a cystic cavity with a glial scar
  • Abscess formation in septic infarction, later replaced by scar tissue
  • Small infarcts may be resorbed; larger ones become firm, pale or haemorrhagic scars, and may show haemorrhagic transformation

Ref: V1-ch05

GENERAL PATHOLOGY — Neoplasia · Carcinogenesis · Clinical Aspects & Diagnosis
NEOPLASIA BASICS
1 Define neoplasia according to Willis. What is tumour and cancer? Classify neoplasia according to biological behavior.

Definition (Willis): "A neoplasm is an abnormal mass of tissue, the growth of which exceeds and is uncoordinated with that of the normal tissues and persists in the same excessive manner after cessation of the stimuli which evoked the change." Exceptions: carcinoma in situ is not a mass, and hormone-dependent neoplasms regress when the stimulus ceases.

Tumour: Neoplasia means "new growth"; the mass of neoplastic cells plus its stroma is a neoplasm (synonyms: neoplasm, oncoma). Cancer: malignant tumours collectively, from Latin for crab, because they cling obstinately to any part they seize.

Classification by biological behaviour:

  1. Benign tumours (e.g. fibroma, lipoma).
  2. Malignant tumours — carcinoma (epithelial), sarcoma (mesenchymal), leukaemia, lymphoma.
  3. Tumours of intermediate/borderline malignancy — locally invasive but do not metastasize (e.g. borderline ovarian tumour, desmoid tumour).
  4. Carcinoma in situ (pre-invasive, no invasion).
  5. Latent, dormant and occult cancers.

Ref: V1-ch08

2 Give the molecular/modern definition of neoplasia. Describe the molecular basis of cancers.

Modern (molecular) definition: A neoplasm is a genetic disorder of cell growth that is triggered by acquired or, less commonly, inherited mutations affecting a single cell and its clonal progeny.

Molecular basis of cancer:

  1. Non-lethal genetic damage (mutation) acquired from chemicals, radiation, viruses or inherited — the heart of carcinogenesis.
  2. Clonal expansion of the damaged cell. Four normal regulatory genes are the principal targets: growth-promoting proto-oncogenes, growth-inhibiting tumour suppressor genes (e.g. p53), DNA repair genes, and apoptosis-regulating genes (pro- and anti-apoptotic).
  3. Consequences: failure of DNA repair, decreased apoptosis and unregulated cell proliferation.
  4. Limitless replicative potential (telomerase) and sustained angiogenesis.
  5. Local invasion and distant metastasis → malignant transformation.

Ref: V1-ch08

3 Mention the characteristics of a benign neoplasm. Give examples of 6 benign neoplasms with their cell of origin.

Characteristics of benign neoplasm: well differentiated (resembles tissue of origin), no anaplasia, few normal mitotic figures, slow growth, usually small, well-formed stroma, usually encapsulated (so mobile and surgically enucleable), no local invasion, no metastasis, and does not recur after complete removal — mostly innocent, though it may be harmful in vulnerable sites (e.g. brain).

Six examples with cell of origin:

Benign tumourCell of origin
FibromaFibroblast
LipomaAdipose (fat) cell
ChondromaCartilage cell
OsteomaBone (osteoblast)
LeiomyomaSmooth muscle cell
HaemangiomaBlood vessel endothelium

Ref: V1-ch08

4 Mention the characteristics of malignant neoplasm. Give examples of 6 malignant neoplasms with their cell of origin.

Characteristics of malignant neoplasm: variable differentiation ending in anaplasia (hallmark of malignancy); pleomorphism, hyperchromatic nuclei with N:C ratio of 1:1, atypical (tripolar/quadripolar) mitoses, loss of polarity, tumour giant cells; rapid growth; local invasion and destruction; haemorrhage and necrosis; no true capsule; frequent metastasis; almost invariably fatal and may recur after treatment.

Six examples with cell of origin:

Malignant tumourCell of origin
Squamous cell carcinomaStratified squamous epithelium
AdenocarcinomaGlandular/ductal epithelium
Transitional cell carcinomaTransitional (urothelial) epithelium
FibrosarcomaFibroblast
OsteosarcomaBone (osteoblast)
RhabdomyosarcomaSkeletal muscle cell

Ref: V1-ch08

5 Mention the basic differences between malignant and benign neoplasms.
FeatureBenignMalignant
Differentiation / anaplasiaWell differentiated, no anaplasiaWell differentiated to anaplastic
PleomorphismAbsentPresent
Rate of growthSlowRapid (some slow)
MitosesFew and normalNumerous and abnormal
N:C ratioUnaltered (1:4 to 1:6)Altered (may be 1:1)
CapsuleUsually encapsulatedNot encapsulated (rarely false capsule)
Local invasionRemains localizedInvasive and infiltrative
MetastasisNever occursFrequent (has potential)
Haemorrhage and necrosisLittle tendencyCommon
Clinical effectUsually not fatal, no recurrenceAlmost invariably fatal, may recur
High yield: The three key distinguishing features to state first in viva — (1) differentiation/anaplasia, (11) local invasion, and (12) metastasis. Metastasis is the most reliable single feature that separates a malignant from a benign tumour.

Ref: V1-ch08

6 What is mixed tumour and teratoma? Give examples of three mixed tumours and two teratomas.

Mixed tumour: A tumour with more than one neoplastic cell type, usually derived from one germ cell layer.

Three examples:

  1. Pleomorphic adenoma (mixed tumour) of salivary gland — usually benign, rarely malignant.
  2. Fibroadenoma of breast — benign.
  3. Wilms tumour (nephroblastoma) of kidney — malignant.

Teratoma: A complex tumour arising from totipotential (germ) cells, composed of mature or immature tissues representing more than one germ layer (ectoderm, endoderm, mesoderm), usually from gonads or midline rests. Well-differentiated components = mature/benign; immature component = malignant.

Two examples:

  1. Mature cystic teratoma (dermoid cyst) of ovary — benign.
  2. Immature (malignant) teratoma of ovary/testis; sacrococcygeal teratoma is the commonest teratoma of childhood.

Ref: V1-ch08

7 Define hamartoma and choristoma with examples.

Hamartoma: An excessive, focal overgrowth of mature cells and tissues native to (indigenous to) the organ in which it occurs, arranged haphazardly — a tumour-like developmental malformation. It has no capsule, growth is coordinated with surrounding tissue and stops after adolescence; it is totally benign.

Examples: pulmonary hamartoma (cartilage, blood vessels, bronchial structures, lymphoid tissue), hamartomatous polyps of the GIT, choristoma of the adrenal rest.

Choristoma: A heterotopic (misplaced) rest — microscopically normal cells or tissues present in an abnormal location; a non-neoplastic mass.

Examples: a rest of pancreatic tissue in the stomach or small intestine wall; a small mass of adrenal cells in kidney, lungs or ovary. These may be confused clinically with neoplasms and rarely are the site of origin of true neoplasms.

Ref: V1-ch08

8 What is differentiation and anaplasia? What are the features of anaplasia?

Differentiation: The extent to which neoplastic parenchymal cells resemble the corresponding normal cells morphologically and functionally. Benign tumours are well differentiated; malignant tumours range from well differentiated to undifferentiated (poor differentiation = worse prognosis).

Anaplasia: Lack of differentiation — considered the hallmark of malignancy (literally "to form backwards").

Features of anaplasia:

  1. Pleomorphism: variation in size and shape of cells and nuclei.
  2. Abnormal nuclear morphology: hyperchromatic, disproportionately large nuclei, N:C ratio 1:1 instead of normal 1:4 to 1:6, variable nuclear shape, large nucleoli.
  3. Atypical mitoses: bizarre tripolar, quadripolar or multipolar spindles.
  4. Loss of polarity: disturbed architecture, organization and orientation.
  5. Other changes: tumour giant cells; frequent haemorrhage and necrosis.

Ref: V1-ch08

9 How grading is done for malignant tumours? Name two special grading systems with the cancer name.

How grading is done: Grading is based on the degree of differentiation of the tumour cells and, in some cancers, the number of mitoses or architectural features — a histological (microscopic) exercise done by the pathologist. Cancers are classified as grade I to IV with increasing anaplasia:

  1. Grade I — well differentiated (more than 75% differentiated cells).
  2. Grade II — moderately differentiated (50–75%).
  3. Grade III — poorly differentiated (25–50%).
  4. Grade IV — undifferentiated (less than 25%).

Two special grading systems:

  1. Gleason grading system — carcinoma prostate (adds architectural patterns, score 2–10).
  2. Scarff–Bloom–Richardson (Nottingham) grading — carcinoma breast (tubule formation, nuclear pleomorphism, mitotic count).

Ref: V1-ch08

10 Define dysplasia. Give four examples with cell of origin.

Definition: Dysplasia literally means "disordered growth", characterized by a loss of uniformity of the individual cells as well as a loss of their architectural orientation. Some but not all features of malignancy are present microscopically (pleomorphism, hyperchromatic nuclei with high N:C ratio, irregular nuclear margin, clumped chromatin, increased mitoses, enlarged nucleoli, disordered architecture). It occurs only in epithelia (often in metaplastic epithelium), never in connective tissue; it may be reversible and is not invasive.

Grading: mild (lower 1/3 of epithelium), moderate (lower 2/3), severe (full thickness = carcinoma in situ).

Four examples with cell of origin:

  1. Cervical dysplasia — squamous epithelial cells of cervix.
  2. Bronchial dysplasia — respiratory bronchial epithelium.
  3. Oral leukoplakia with dysplasia — oral squamous epithelium.
  4. Intraductal dysplasia/hyperplasia — mammary duct epithelial cells.

Ref: V1-ch08

11 What is the importance of dysplasia? What is CIS?

Importance of dysplasia:

  • It is a precursor of malignant transformation, though not all dysplasia progresses to cancer.
  • Dysplastic changes are found adjacent to invasive carcinoma and often antedate cervical cancer.
  • Some mutations of full-blown cancer may already be present even in mild dysplasia.
  • It differs from cancer by two features: lack of invasiveness and reversibility — removing the inciting cause may completely reverse even moderately severe dysplasia.
  • Full-thickness dysplasia without basement membrane breach = carcinoma in situ, whose excision results in complete cure.

Carcinoma in situ (CIS): When dysplasia is severe and involves the full thickness of the epithelium but the lesion does not penetrate the basement membrane. It shows all cytologic features of malignancy but is pre-invasive; unless treated it has a high probability of progressing to invasive cancer, though it may persist for years. Examples: CIN grade III, DCIS and LCIS of breast, Bowen's disease, erythroplasia of Queyrat, CIS of urinary bladder. Diagnosis: cytology (smear) confirmed by biopsy.

Ref: V1-ch08

12 Name the precancerous conditions of various organs with the corresponding cancers.

Precancerous (pre-malignant) conditions and corresponding malignancy:

OrganPrecancerous condition/lesionCancer
SkinSolar actinic keratosis, xeroderma pigmentosum, dysplastic nevi, Bowen's diseaseSquamous cell / basal cell carcinoma, malignant melanoma
MouthLeukoplakia, Plummer–Vinson (Paterson–Kelly) syndromeCarcinoma of oral cavity
OesophagusBarrett oesophagusOesophageal adenocarcinoma
StomachChronic atrophic gastritis of pernicious anaemia, adenomatous polyp, chronic gastric ulcerGastric carcinoma
ColonAdenomatous polyp, chronic ulcerative colitis, Crohn's diseaseColorectal adenocarcinoma
LiverCirrhosis of liverHepatocellular carcinoma
Cervix / breast / bronchus / boneCervical dysplasia, intraductal hyperplasia, bronchial metaplasia and dysplasia, Paget's diseaseCervical, breast, bronchogenic carcinoma, osteosarcoma

Ref: V1-ch08

13 What is invasion? How staging varies with extent of invasion?

Invasion: Aggressive infiltration of adjacent tissues by a malignant tumour — an active process occurring along tissue planes offering least resistance (extracellular matrix, perineural spaces, vascular lamina). It begins with loosening of cell–cell contacts (loss of E-cadherin), degradation of ECM by MMPs/type IV collagenase, attachment to remodeled ECM, and migration of tumour cells. Invasion and metastasis are the biologic hallmarks of malignancy; once the basement membrane is breached the tumour is invasive (before that it is carcinoma in situ).

Staging varies with extent of invasion:

  1. Tumour confined to epithelium without BM penetration = carcinoma in situ (Tis) — best prognosis.
  2. Depth/size of local invasion increases the T category from T1 to T4 (T4 = invasion of adjacent structures).
  3. Invasion of vessels, lymphatics and nerves allows nodal (N1–N3) and distant (M1) spread, upstaging the disease.

Ref: V1-ch08

14 What are the surest signs of malignancy? What is staging of cancer? How is it done?

Surest signs of malignancy:

  1. Metastasis — the most reliable feature that differentiates malignant from benign tumours.
  2. Local invasion and destruction of surrounding tissue.
  3. Anaplasia with cytologic features — pleomorphism, high N:C ratio (1:1), hyperchromasia, atypical (multipolar) mitoses, tumour giant cells.
  4. Haemorrhage and necrosis within the tumour.

Staging of cancer: Staging is based on the size of the primary lesion, the extent of spread to regional lymph nodes, and the presence or absence of blood-borne metastases. It has greater clinical value than grading and guides treatment selection.

How it is done: Two systems are in use — UICC TNM system and AJC system (stages 0 to IV). Staging is done by the pathologist and surgeon together using clinical examination, imaging, operative findings and histopathology of the resected specimen and nodes.

Ref: V1-ch08

15 Define metastasis. Mention the routes of metastasis with examples. What is Krukenberg tumour?

Metastasis: Spread of a tumour to sites physically discontinuous with the primary tumour; metastatic deposits are implants discontinuous with the primary tumour. It unequivocally marks a tumour as malignant.

Routes of metastasis:

  1. Lymphatic spread (most common for carcinomas): carcinoma breast → axillary nodes; bronchogenic carcinoma → hilar and mediastinal nodes; gastric carcinoma → left supraclavicular (Virchow's) node, Troisier's sign.
  2. Haematogenous spread (typical of sarcomas and RCC, HCC, follicular thyroid carcinoma, prostatic carcinoma, choriocarcinoma): common sites liver, lungs, brain, bones, kidney, adrenals (2L3B); venous route is commoner than arterial because venous walls are thinner.
  3. Seeding of body cavities and surfaces: peritoneal, pleural, pericardial, subarachnoid, joint spaces.

Krukenberg tumour: Carcinoma of stomach or colon that gravitates and seeds the peritoneum to produce bilateral ovarian metastatic tumours (a form of transperitoneal seeding).

Ref: V1-ch08

CARCINOGENESIS
16 Name the groups of gene commonly mutated during carcinogenesis with examples.

Four groups of regulatory genes that are principal targets of genetic damage:

Gene groupEffect of mutationExamples
Growth-promoting proto-oncogenesGain of function → become oncogenesK-RAS (colon, lung, pancreas), MYC (Burkitt lymphoma), ERBB2/HER2 (breast), ABL (CML), BRAF (melanoma)
