Growth-inhibiting tumour suppressor genesLoss of function (usually two hits)RB (13q14), p53 (17p), APC (colon), WT1 (Wilms), VHL, NF1/NF2, BRCA1/2
DNA repair genesFailure of repair → mutator phenotypeMLH1/MSH2 (HNPCC), XPC (xeroderma pigmentosum), BRCA1/2, ATM (ataxia-telangiectasia)
Apoptosis-regulating genesDecreased apoptosisPro-apoptotic: BAX, p53 lost; anti-apoptotic: BCL2 overexpressed (follicular lymphoma)

Ref: V1-ch08

17 Name the tumour suppressor genes commonly mutated in carcinogenesis. Which gene is called guardian of the genome, and why?

Tumour suppressor genes (anti-oncogenes): normal genes that switch off cell proliferation by acting on the cell cycle at G1. Commonly mutated ones:

GeneChromosomeTumour
RB13q14Retinoblastoma
p53 (TP53)17p13.1Lung, breast, colon carcinoma
NF-1 / NF-217 / 22Neurofibromatosis 1 / 2
APC5qColonic carcinoma
WT111Wilms tumour
VHL3pHereditary renal cell cancer
BRCA1, BRCA217q21, 13q12-13Breast and ovarian tumours

p53 — "guardian of the genome": It acts at the G1/S checkpoint. On DNA damage it shuts down the cell cycle until repair is completed; if repair is impossible it triggers apoptosis, so damaged DNA is never duplicated. It is the most frequently mutated gene in human cancers (found in more than 50%) and acts as a "molecular policeman" preventing propagation of genetically damaged cells — hence the name.

Ref: V1-ch08

18 What is proto-oncogene and oncogene? Which gene is called governor of proliferation, and why?

Proto-oncogene: A normal cellular gene that promotes and regulates cellular proliferation and differentiation — a physiological regulator of cell growth.

Oncogene: A mutated or overexpressed version of a proto-oncogene that functions autonomously, having lost dependence on normal growth-promoting signals; it encodes an oncoprotein that drives growth even without growth factors. Activation occurs by point mutation, chromosomal rearrangement, chemicals, radiation or oncogenic viruses (e.g. K-RAS, MYC, ABL, ERBB2).

Governor of proliferation — RB gene: The retinoblastoma protein is the most important negative regulator of the G1/S transition. Hypophosphorylated RB sequesters E2F transcription factors and blocks entry into S phase; growth signals phosphorylate RB, releasing E2F and allowing proliferation. Loss of RB removes this on–off switch, so proliferation becomes uncontrolled (Knudson two-hit hypothesis in retinoblastoma).

High yield: Nicknames — p53 = "guardian of the genome" (most mutated gene, repair or apoptosis); RB = "governor of proliferation" (G1/S brake); APC = gatekeeper of colonic epithelium; p16 = guardian of the RB pathway.

Ref: V1-ch08

19 What is carcinogenesis? Mention the hallmarks of carcinogenesis.

Carcinogenesis: The multi-step process by which normal cells are transformed into malignant cancer cells through accumulation of genetic (and epigenetic) damage, followed by clonal expansion of the transformed clone.

Hallmarks of cancer (eight fundamental changes in cell physiology):

  1. Self-sufficiency in growth signals (usually by oncogene activation).
  2. Insensitivity to growth-inhibitory signals.
  3. Altered cellular metabolism.
  4. Evasion of apoptosis.
  5. Limitless replicative potential (immortality).
  6. Sustained angiogenesis.
  7. Ability to invade and metastasize.
  8. Ability to evade the host immune response.

Enabling characteristics: genomic instability (mutator phenotype) and tumour-promoting inflammation.

Ref: V1-ch08

20 What is initiating and driver mutation in carcinogenesis? Discuss about multistep carcinogenesis.

Initiating mutation: The first, permanent, non-lethal DNA damage produced by a sufficient dose of an initiator; it makes the cell potentially capable of giving rise to a tumour, but initiation alone is insufficient for tumour formation (it is irreversible and heritable).

Driver mutation: A mutation that gives the cell a selective growth advantage and drives malignant transformation/clonal expansion. Far more numerous passenger mutations confer no phenotype. The first driver is the initiating mutation.

Multistep carcinogenesis: Cancer arises through sequential accumulation of several driver mutations over years, together with promotion and progression. Best model — colorectal carcinoma:

  1. Normal epithelium → APC loss (initiation/early).
  2. → KRAS mutation (benign adenoma).
  3. → loss of 18q/SMAD4.
  4. → TP53 loss (late) → invasive carcinoma.

Chemical carcinogenesis likewise has initiation plus promotion (promotion alone, given before initiation, does not produce tumours).

Ref: V1-ch08

21 Classify the causes/etiology of cancer. Name some familial cancer syndromes.

Causes/etiology of cancer:

  1. Environmental factors (dominant): infectious agents (about 15% of all cancers — HPV, EBV, HBV/HCV, HHV-8, HTLV-1, H. pylori, Schistosoma), smoking (about 90% of lung cancer deaths), alcohol, diet, obesity, reproductive history, UV rays, arsenic-contaminated tube-well water, occupational exposure (asbestos).
  2. Genetic predisposition — inherited cancer syndromes.
  3. Age (common after 60 years) and sex.
  4. Acquired predisposing conditions — chronic inflammation, precursor lesions, immunodeficiency states.

Familial cancer syndromes: retinoblastoma (RB), familial adenomatous polyposis (APC), hereditary non-polyposis colon cancer/Lynch (MLH1/MSH2), multiple endocrine neoplasia 1 and 2, breast–ovarian cancer (BRCA1/2), neurofibromatosis 1 and 2, Wilms tumour (WT1), Li-Fraumeni (p53). Defective DNA-repair syndromes: xeroderma pigmentosum, ataxia-telangiectasia, Bloom syndrome, Fanconi anaemia.

Ref: V1-ch08

22 How inflammation can generate cancer? Explain with examples.

How chronic inflammation generates cancer: Chronic epithelial injury repeatedly kills and regenerates cells, so metaplasia develops, which may proceed to dysplasia and finally carcinoma. Mechanisms —

  • Reactive oxygen and nitrogen species released by inflammatory cells cause direct DNA damage and mutations.
  • Inflammatory cytokines and growth factors (TNF, IL-6, prostaglandins) drive continuous cell proliferation, giving more opportunity for replication errors.
  • Continuous cell turnover with inadequate repair and a locally immunosuppressive microenvironment allows mutated clones to escape destruction.

Examples:

  1. Barrett oesophagus (gastro-oesophageal reflux) → oesophageal adenocarcinoma.
  2. Chronic atrophic gastritis and H. pylori gastritis → gastric carcinoma and MALToma.
  3. Chronic ulcerative colitis → colorectal carcinoma.
  4. Chronic hepatitis/cirrhosis → hepatocellular carcinoma.
  5. Schistosomiasis of bladder and smoking-induced bronchial metaplasia → carcinoma.

Ref: V1-ch08

23 Discuss on microbial carcinogenesis with examples.

Microbial carcinogenesis: About 15% of human cancers are caused directly or indirectly by infectious agents. Mechanisms include viral oncoproteins that inactivate host tumour suppressors (HPV E6 degrades p53, E7 inactivates RB), insertional activation of proto-oncogenes, and chronic inflammation-driven regeneration.

Examples:

MicrobeCancer
HPV 16, 18 (DNA virus)Cervical and anogenital squamous cell carcinoma
EBV (DNA virus)Nasopharyngeal carcinoma, Burkitt lymphoma, Hodgkin lymphoma
HBV / HCVHepatocellular carcinoma
HHV-8 (KSHV)Kaposi sarcoma
HTLV-1 (RNA virus)Adult T-cell leukaemia/lymphoma
Merkel cell polyomavirusMerkel cell carcinoma
Helicobacter pyloriGastric carcinoma and gastric MALToma
Aspergillus flavus (aflatoxin B1)Hepatocellular carcinoma
Schistosoma haematobium; liver flukesSquamous cell bladder cancer; cholangiocarcinoma

Ref: V1-ch08

24 Classify the chemical carcinogens. What are the occupational cancers?

Classification of chemical carcinogens:

  1. Direct-acting (no metabolic activation needed): alkylating agents (beta-propiolactone, dimethyl sulfate, diepoxybutane, anticancer drugs such as cyclophosphamide, chlorambucil, nitrosoureas) and acylating agents (1-acetyl-imidazole, dimethylcarbamyl chloride).
  2. Indirect-acting (procarcinogens) — need metabolic activation to the ultimate carcinogen: polycyclic/heterocyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (benzo[a]pyrene, benzanthracene), aromatic amines and azo dyes (2-naphthylamine, benzidine, butter yellow), natural plant/microbial products (aflatoxin B1, griseofulvin, cycasin, safrole, betel nuts), others (nitrosamines, vinyl chloride, nickel, chromium, insecticides).

Occupational cancers:

AgentCancer
AsbestosMesothelioma, bronchogenic and gastrointestinal carcinoma
Arsenic, chromates, nickel, berylliumLung carcinoma (arsenic also skin and bladder)
Aromatic amines (dye industry)Urinary bladder carcinoma
BenzeneAcute myeloid leukaemia, Hodgkin lymphoma
Vinyl chlorideHepatic angiosarcoma
CadmiumProstate carcinoma

Ref: V1-ch08

25 What are the types of radiation? How radiation promote cancers?

Types of radiation:

  1. Ionizing radiation — electromagnetic (X-rays, gamma rays) and particulate (alpha and beta particles, protons, neutrons).
  2. Non-ionizing radiation — ultraviolet rays: UVA (320–400 nm), UVB (280–320 nm), UVC (200–280 nm).
  3. Therapeutic irradiation (radiotherapy) given for an existing tumour.

How radiation promotes cancer:

  • Ionizing radiation produces free radicals and causes DNA double-strand breaks, chromosome breakage, translocations and point mutations; it is also immunosuppressive, so damaged cells escape surveillance.
  • UV radiation causes pyrimidine dimers and, if unrepaired by nucleotide excision repair, produces characteristic UV-signature mutations.
  • The damage is cumulative and may activate proto-oncogenes or inactivate tumour suppressor genes.

Cancers produced: UV → squamous cell carcinoma, basal cell carcinoma, malignant melanoma of skin; ionizing radiation → leukaemia, breast, colon, thyroid and lung cancer; therapeutic irradiation → thyroid cancer and leukaemia.

Ref: V1-ch08

CLINICAL ASPECTS AND DIAGNOSIS OF NEOPLASIA
26 Mention the local effects of neoplasia. Give examples of some hormonal effects of neoplasia.

Local effects of neoplasia:

  • Compression of adjacent structures — pituitary adenoma causing visual field defects, intracranial tumours causing raised intracranial pressure.
  • Obstruction — bronchial tumour causing collapse (atelectasis) and post-obstructive pneumonitis; colonic tumour causing intestinal obstruction and intussusception.
  • Ulceration and haemorrhage — ulcerated gastric carcinoma causing melaena, cervical carcinoma causing offensive vaginal discharge, haematuria from bladder tumour.
  • Destruction and invasion — bone destruction, perineural invasion causing pain; desmoplasia producing a hard scirrhous mass (scirrhous carcinoma breast, linitis plastica).
  • Vascular effects — venous compression causing oedema; tumour angiogenesis feeding the mass.

Hormonal effects: functioning endocrine tumours — insulinoma (hypoglycaemia), gastrinoma (Zollinger–Ellison syndrome), glucagonoma, phaeochromocytoma (catecholamines); ectopic hormones — ACTH/Cushing syndrome and SIADH (small cell lung carcinoma), PTHrP/hypercalcaemia (squamous cell lung carcinoma), erythropoietin/polycythaemia (renal carcinoma).

Ref: V1-ch08

27 What is cancer cachexia? Mention its mechanism.

Definition: Cancer cachexia is a hypercatabolic state defined by loss of muscle mass (with or without loss of fat) that cannot be explained by diminished food intake — progressive loss of body fat and lean body mass with profound weakness, anorexia, anaemia and oedema, caused by factors released by the tumour or by host immune cells.

Key facts: occurs in about 50% of cancer patients, most commonly in advanced gastrointestinal, pancreatic and lung cancers, and is responsible for about 30% of cancer deaths; death usually follows atrophy of the diaphragm and other respiratory muscles.

Mechanism: several inflammatory mediators act together —

  • TNF-alpha (cachectin) and IL-1 released from activated macrophages promote catabolism of adipose tissue.
  • IL-6 and other cytokines drive acute-phase responses and anorexia.
  • Proteolysis-inducing factor (PIF) released by tumour cells is responsible for skeletal muscle proteolysis and loss of muscle mass.

Ref: V1-ch08

28 Define and classify paraneoplastic syndromes with examples.

Definition: Symptom complexes in cancer-bearing individuals that cannot readily be explained by the anatomic distribution of the tumour or by the elaboration of hormones indigenous to the source tissue. They occur in about 10% of persons with cancer.

Classification with examples:

  1. Endocrine/metabolic — Cushing syndrome (ectopic ACTH, small cell lung carcinoma), SIADH (ADH, small cell lung carcinoma), hypercalcaemia (PTHrP, squamous cell lung carcinoma, breast, renal carcinoma), hypoglycaemia (insulin-like factor, fibrosarcoma), polycythaemia (erythropoietin, renal carcinoma), carcinoid syndrome (serotonin).
  2. Neuromuscular — Lambert–Eaton myasthenia, cerebellar degeneration, encephalomyelitis, peripheral neuropathy, dermatomyositis.
  3. Haematologic and thromboembolic — migratory thrombophlebitis (Trousseau), DIC (APL, prostatic carcinoma), non-bacterial thrombotic endocarditis, red cell aplasia.
  4. Dermatologic — acanthosis nigricans, dermatomyositis.
  5. Skeletal — hypertrophic pulmonary osteoarthropathy and clubbing (bronchogenic carcinoma).
  6. Renal — nephrotic syndrome.

Ref: V1-ch08

29 Mention the importance of paraneoplastic syndrome. Mention some paraneoplastic endocrinopathies with the causative tumour.

Importance:

  • They may be the earliest manifestation of an occult neoplasm, sometimes preceding the discovery of the tumour by months or years.
  • In affected patients they can cause significant clinical problems and may even be lethal (e.g. hypercalcaemia, SIADH).
  • They may mimic metastatic disease and therefore confound treatment.
  • Recognising them helps localise the primary tumour and monitor its behaviour.

Paraneoplastic endocrinopathies and causative tumours:

EndocrinopathyCausative tumour
Cushing syndrome (ectopic ACTH)Small cell carcinoma of lung, pancreatic carcinoma, neural tumours
SIADHSmall cell lung carcinoma, intracranial neoplasms
Hypercalcaemia (most common paraneoplastic syndrome)Squamous cell lung carcinoma, breast, renal carcinoma, multiple myeloma, adult T-cell leukaemia/lymphoma
HypoglycaemiaFibrosarcoma, other mesenchymal sarcomas, ovarian carcinoma
PolycythaemiaRenal carcinoma, cerebral haemangioma, HCC
Carcinoid syndromeLung, gastric and pancreatic carcinoma

Ref: V1-ch08

30 What are the systemic effects of neoplasm? How are these differentiated from paraneoplastic syndromes?

Systemic effects of neoplasm: general effects of a growing tumour and host reaction — fever, night sweats, weight loss and cancer cachexia, anaemia (anaemia of chronic disease, haemolysis, marrow infiltration), raised ESR, anorexia, generalised weakness, hypercalcaemia, hypercoagulability with thrombophlebitis, granulocytosis/leukaemoid reaction, and immunosuppression. Metastatic spread causes organ-specific failure (jaundice, pathological fracture, cord compression).

Difference from paraneoplastic syndromes:

FeatureSystemic effectsParaneoplastic syndromes
ExplanationExplainable by tumour bulk, invasion, metastasis or host responseNot explained by anatomic distribution of tumour or hormones indigenous to the tissue of origin
OccurrenceRelated to tumour burden/stageAbout 10% of all cancers, may occur with small occult tumours
PresentationProgressive with the diseaseMay be the earliest manifestation and may mimic metastasis
ExamplesCachexia, fever, anaemia, raised ESREctopic ACTH, SIADH, PTHrP-mediated hypercalcaemia, Lambert–Eaton syndrome

Ref: V1-ch08

31 How body immunity is developed against tumours? What is immunosurveillance?

How immunity against tumours develops:

  1. Neoplastic cells express abnormal antigens — neoantigens from mutated proteins, tumour-associated antigens (overexpressed or embryonic proteins such as CEA, AFP), and viral antigens in virus-induced cancers.
  2. These antigens are processed and presented on MHC class I molecules, where they are recognised by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes; helper T cells provide support.
  3. Innate effectors (NK cells, activated macrophages) and tumour-specific antibodies (with ADCC) complete the response; the effector cells kill or contain the transformed clone.

Immunosurveillance: The concept that the immune system constantly watches for and destroys nascent transformed cells, keeping occult tumours in check. It has three phases (immunoediting): elimination (destroyed nascent tumour), equilibrium (dormancy, contained by immunity), and escape (selected antigen-loss variants grow into clinically evident cancer).

Ref: V1-ch08

32 What are the antitumour mechanisms of body? How tumours escape from immunosurveillance?

Antitumour mechanisms (tumour immunity):

  • Cell-mediated immunity: CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes kill tumour cells bearing tumour antigens (mainly against virus-associated neoplasms); NK cells destroy tumour cells without prior sensitisation, especially cells with reduced MHC expression; activated macrophages kill via reactive oxygen metabolites and TNF-alpha.
  • Humoral immunity: tumour-specific antibodies mediate antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC) with NK cells.

How tumours escape immunosurveillance:

  1. Loss or down-regulation of tumour antigens (antigen-loss variants) and loss of MHC class I expression.
  2. Low immunogenicity and absent costimulation, so no effective T-cell priming.
  3. Secretion of immunosuppressive factors — TGF-beta, IL-10, VEGF — and recruitment of regulatory T cells.
  4. Expression of checkpoint ligands (PD-L1 engaging PD-1, CTLA-4) that switch off T cells.
  5. Immunosuppressive tumour microenvironment, dense stroma and host immunodeficiency.

Ref: V1-ch08

33 How tumours are diagnosed in laboratory? Which is the gold standard test for tumour diagnosis?

Laboratory diagnosis of tumour:

  1. Cytopathology — exfoliative cytology (Pap smear, sputum, urine, body fluids) and aspiration cytology (FNAC, bone marrow).
  2. Histopathology — biopsy or excised mass, gross description, then sectioning and H&E staining for microscopic examination.
  3. Tumour markers (AFP, PSA, CEA, CA-19-9, CA-125) — support diagnosis, not definitive.
  4. Immunohistochemistry — pancytokeratin, desmin, vimentin, CD markers, S-100.
  5. Flow cytometry — membrane antigens and DNA content, especially for leukaemia and lymphoma.
  6. Molecular diagnosis — PCR, DNA sequencing, DNA microarray.

These are preceded by proper history, clinical examination, biochemical and radiological findings.

Gold standard test: Histopathological examination of the biopsy/excised tumour (H&E sections) — it confirms whether the cells are cancerous and gives the tumour type and grade.

Ref: V1-ch08

34 What is cytology? Mention the types of cytology with examples.

Cytology: Cytopathology is the branch of pathology that studies and diagnoses disease at the cellular level, examining individual cells or small clusters of cells for abnormalities; it is widely used for cancer screening, diagnosis and monitoring.

Types with examples:

  1. Exfoliative cytology — study of cells shed or exfoliated into body cavities or secretions. Examples: Papanicolaou (Pap) smear for dysplasia, carcinoma in situ and invasive carcinoma of the uterine cervix and vagina; sputum and bronchial washings for bronchogenic carcinoma; urine for urinary tract tumours; pleural, peritoneal and pericardial fluids, gastric washings and CSF.
  2. Aspiration cytology (fine needle aspiration) — cells obtained by a needle from lesions that do not shed cells freely, e.g. breast, thyroid, lymph nodes, liver, pancreas; bone marrow aspiration for haematological disorders.
  3. Other types — brushing cytology, scrap cytology and imprint cytology.

Stains used: Papanicolaou stain and Giemsa stain.

Ref: V1-ch08

35 What is FNAC? Why biopsy is superior to FNAC in tumour diagnosis?

FNAC: Fine needle aspiration cytology — aspiration of a small amount of fluid and very small pieces of tissue from the tumour with a thin, small-bore needle, followed by cytological examination of the stained smear (Pap or H&E). It may be image-guided (USG, CT) or non-guided, and is used for breast, thyroid, salivary gland, superficial lymph nodes and, with imaging, lung, prostate, liver and pancreas. Advantages: less invasive, quick, cheap, repeatable, no anaesthesia needed, suitable for debilitated patients.

Why biopsy is superior:

  • Biopsy yields tissue fragments that preserve architecture and the relationship to stroma and basement membrane, so invasion versus carcinoma in situ can be judged — impossible on FNAC smears.
  • Allows precise histological typing, grading and assessment of margins.
  • Permits immunohistochemistry, molecular and cytogenetic studies on the block.
  • FNAC has a small sample, risk of sampling error and false-negative results, and needs experienced hands.

Ref: V1-ch08

36 What is biopsy? Mention the types of biopsy with examples.

Definition: Removal of a mass or a piece of tissue from the diseased organ for histopathological examination, i.e. a procedure to remove tissue or a sample of cells from a living subject so that it can be analysed in a laboratory. Material is preserved in 10% formalin.

Types of biopsy:

  1. Open/direct — (a) excisional biopsy (e.g. excision of a lymph node); (b) incisional biopsy (e.g. from a soft tissue swelling of a limb).
  2. Closed — (a) true-cut/core needle biopsy, often USG or CT guided (breast, lung, liver, prostate, bone, kidney); (b) punch biopsy (skin, oral mucosa, vulva, cervix); (c) cone biopsy (cervix); (d) wedge biopsy (liver); (e) endoscopic biopsy (gastroscopy, colonoscopy, bronchoscopy, cystoscopy).
  3. Special types — frozen section (intra-operative/on-table diagnosis, margin clearance), sentinel lymph node biopsy (frozen section of the first draining node).

Ref: V1-ch08

37 Name the molecular diagnostic tools in tumour diagnosis with examples. How immunohistochemistry help in tumour diagnosis?

Molecular diagnostic tools:

  • PCR — detection of oncogenic mutations and translocations, e.g. BCR-ABL in chronic myeloid leukaemia, PML-RARA in APL, HPV DNA in cervical lesions, clonality in B- and T-cell neoplasms.
  • DNA sequencing — mutation analysis (KRAS, BRAF, EGFR) for diagnosis, targeted therapy and detecting hereditary predisposition.
  • DNA microarray / gene expression profiling — classification and prognosis of tumours.
  • FISH and other cytogenetic tools — HER2 amplification, chromosomal translocations.
  • Flow cytometry — DNA content and surface antigens, especially leukaemias and lymphomas.

Role of immunohistochemistry (IHC):

  1. Categorizes undifferentiated malignant tumours — pancytokeratin for carcinoma, desmin and vimentin for sarcoma, CD markers for lymphoma, S-100 for melanoma.
  2. Identifies the site of origin of metastatic tumours — PSA for prostate carcinoma, thyroglobulin for thyroid carcinoma.
  3. Detects prognostic/therapeutic molecules — estrogen and progesterone receptors and HER2 in breast cancer, which guide hormone or antibody therapy.

Ref: V1-ch08

38 Define and classify tumour markers with examples. How these contribute in tumour diagnosis and management?

Definition: Tumour markers are biochemical substances (proteins, hormones, enzymes, antigens or genetic products) released by tumours and detected in blood, urine, stool, other body fluids or tissues that indicate the presence of a tumour.

Classification with examples:

ClassMarkerAssociated tumour
Oncofetal antigensAFP; CEAHepatocellular carcinoma and non-seminomatous germ cell tumours; colorectal, pancreatic, lung, gastric carcinoma
HormoneshCG; calcitonin; catecholaminesTrophoblastic and non-seminomatous testicular tumours; medullary thyroid carcinoma; phaeochromocytoma
EnzymesPSA; prostatic acid phosphatase; neuron-specific enolaseProstate cancer; prostate cancer; small cell lung cancer, neuroblastoma
Glycoprotein (CA) antigensCA-125; CA-19-9; CA-15-3Ovarian cancer; colon and pancreatic cancer; breast cancer
ImmunoglobulinsMonoclonal immunoglobulinMultiple myeloma and other gammopathies

Contribution: They are not primary/definitive diagnostic tests, but they (1) support the diagnosis and localise the primary site, (2) assess response to therapy and prognosis, and (3) detect recurrence early. Mutations such as p53 and RAS in stool, serum, sputum or urine aid diagnosis of colon, pancreatic, lung and bladder cancers.

Ref: V1-ch08

39 How neoplasia can be detected at early stage? Give examples of precancerous lesions that can be detected early and cancer can be prevented.

Early detection of neoplasia:

  • Cancer screening of asymptomatic and high-risk populations (cytology, imaging, tumour markers in selected groups).
  • Vigilance for paraneoplastic syndromes and unexplained systemic symptoms.
  • Identifying and removing precancerous lesions and precursor conditions, since dysplasia is reversible.
  • Diagnostic work-up of any lump, ulcer or abnormal bleeding — cytology followed by biopsy.

Examples of precancerous lesions that allow early detection and prevention:

  1. Cervical dysplasia / carcinoma in situ (CIN) — detected by Pap smear; excision prevents cervical cancer.
  2. Adenomatous polyp of colon — detected by colonoscopy; polypectomy prevents colorectal adenocarcinoma.
  3. Oral leukoplakia and solar actinic keratosis — detected clinically; removal prevents oral and skin carcinoma.
  4. Barrett oesophagus — endoscopic surveillance prevents oesophageal adenocarcinoma.
  5. Familial adenomatous polyposis — prophylactic colectomy; xeroderma pigmentosum — strict sun protection.

Ref: V1-ch08

40 What is cancer screening? Give some examples of cancer screening with their effects in cancer control.

Definition: Cancer screening is the systematic testing of an apparently asymptomatic population to detect cancer or its precursor lesions before clinical symptoms appear.

Goals:

  • Catch dysplasia before it becomes carcinoma (dysplasia is reversible).
  • Detect carcinoma before clinical symptoms, when it is still localised and curable.

Examples and their effect in cancer control:

Screening testCancerEffect
Pap smear (and HPV DNA test)Cervical cancerDetects dysplasia/CIS; screening has markedly reduced cervical cancer incidence and mortality
MammographyBreast cancerDetects small, early, operable tumours → better survival
PSA with digital rectal examinationProstate cancerDetects cancer before urinary obstruction; incidental early diagnosis
Faecal occult blood test and colonoscopyColorectal cancerDetects occult bleeding and adenomatous polyps; polypectomy prevents cancer

Ref: V1-ch08

41 What is the difference between grade and stage? What does TNM stand for?
FeatureGradeStage
MeaningLevel of differentiation of the tumour cellsExtent of spread of the cancer within the body
Based onDegree of differentiation and mitotic count (histology)Size of primary lesion, node involvement and metastasis (TNM)
MethodMicroscopy — by the pathologistClinical and investigational — by pathologist and surgeon
ExampleG1 (well differentiated) to G4 (undifferentiated)Stage 0 to stage IV (AJC)
ValueLess clinical value (except soft tissue sarcomas)Greater value; guides treatment and prognosis

TNM stands for: T — extent/size of the primary tumour (Tis = carcinoma in situ, T1–T4 with increasing size and local invasion); N — regional lymph node involvement (N0 = no nodes, N1–N3 = increasing number and range of nodes); M — distant metastasis (M0 absent, M1 present, Mx = cannot be assessed).

High yield: "Grading = microscope, Staging = TNM". Staging is the more important prognostic indicator because it reflects spread and metastasis and determines the treatment plan; both are used to select the best modality of treatment.

Ref: V1-ch08

42 What is tumour immunity? Why are immunodeficient patients more prone to certain cancers?

Tumour immunity: The body's immune response against cancerous cells, comprising — (1) cell-mediated immunity: cytotoxic CD8+ T lymphocytes (protective against virus-associated neoplasms), NK cells (kill without prior sensitisation, especially cells with reduced MHC expression) and activated macrophages (killing via reactive oxygen metabolites and TNF-alpha); and (2) humoral immunity, where antibodies mediate antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC).

Why immunodeficient patients are more prone: Defective immunosurveillance means transformed or virally infected cells are not eliminated, so mutations accumulate. The risk is greatest with T-cell immune deficits, and the cancers are mainly virus-induced lymphomas and some carcinomas.

  • HIV/AIDS: Kaposi sarcoma (HHV-8), primary CNS and non-Hodgkin lymphoma, Hodgkin lymphoma, cervical, vulval, anal and penile cancer (HPV), hepatocellular carcinoma and germ cell tumours.
  • Also seen after organ transplantation (EBV-driven lymphomas) and in primary immunodeficiencies such as SCID and ataxia-telangiectasia.

Ref: V1-ch07

GENERAL PATHOLOGY — Genetic · Environmental · Nutritional Disorders · Childhood
GENETIC DISORDERS
1 What is a gene? Classify genetic disorders with examples.

Gene: A gene is the basic unit of heredity — a discrete segment of DNA at a fixed chromosomal locus whose nucleotide sequence encodes a functional product (usually a protein, or an RNA such as rRNA/tRNA). Genes are transmitted from parent to offspring.

Classification of genetic disorders:

  1. Single-gene (Mendelian) disorders: mutation of one gene with a large effect — autosomal dominant (Huntington disease, von Willebrand disease), autosomal recessive (thalassaemia, cystic fibrosis), X-linked recessive (haemophilia A and B), X-linked dominant (vitamin D-resistant rickets).
  2. Complex / multigenic (multifactorial) disorders: interaction of susceptible genes with environment — cleft lip and palate, congenital heart disease, pyloric stenosis, diabetes mellitus, hypertension, coronary heart disease.
  3. Chromosomal (cytogenetic) disorders: numerical or structural chromosome change — Down syndrome (trisomy 21), Edwards syndrome (trisomy 18), Patau syndrome (trisomy 13), Klinefelter syndrome, Turner syndrome.

Ref: V1-ch06

2 What is a chromosome? Classify chromosomal abnormalities with examples.

Chromosome: A chromosome is a thread-like structure of DNA coiled around histone proteins that carries genetic information in the eukaryotic nucleus. A normal human somatic cell contains 46 chromosomes — 22 homologous pairs of autosomes plus two sex chromosomes (XX in female, XY in male).

Classification of chromosomal abnormalities:

TypeDefinitionExamples
Numerical — aneuploidyGain or loss of individual whole chromosomes (nullisomy, monosomy, trisomy, tetrasomy)Trisomy 21 (Down), trisomy 18 (Edwards), monosomy X (Turner, 45,X)
Numerical — polyploidyGain of one or more complete chromosome setsTriploidy 69,XXY (usually aborts)
Structural — deletionLoss of a chromosome segmentCri-du-chat syndrome (5p−)
Structural — duplicationSegment present more than onceCharcot-Marie-Tooth duplication of 17p
Structural — inversion / translocationReversal of a segment, or transfer of a segment to another chromosomePhiladelphia chromosome t(9;22); Robertsonian translocation in Down syndrome

Ref: V1-ch06

3 Classify numerical chromosomal abnormalities with examples.

Numerical chromosomal abnormalities result from gain or loss of whole chromosomes or chromosome sets, mainly by nondisjunction (failure of chromatid separation at anaphase) and anaphase lag.

Classification:

GroupDefinitionExamples
Nullisomy (2n−2)Loss of both members of a pairLethal, never seen in live births
Monosomy (2n−1)Loss of one chromosomeTurner syndrome 45,X
Trisomy (2n+1)Gain of one chromosomeDown (47,+21), Edwards (47,+18), Patau (47,+13), Klinefelter (47,XXY), triple X (47,XXX)
Tetrasomy (2n+2)Gain of two homologous chromosomes48,XXXX
PolyploidyGain of a whole set of chromosomesTriploidy (69,XXY), tetraploidy (92,XXYY) — cause spontaneous abortion

Note: If two genetically different cell lines exist (e.g. 46,XX / 47,XX,+21) it is called mosaicism, due to mitotic nondisjunction after fertilisation.

Ref: V1-ch06

4 What is mutation? Mention the types of mutation with examples.

Mutation: A mutation is a permanent change in the DNA sequence of a gene (or the chromosome) that is transmissible to daughter cells and to offspring.

Types (based on extent of genetic change):

  1. Genome mutation: loss or gain of whole chromosomes, producing monosomy or trisomy (e.g. Down syndrome).
  2. Chromosomal mutation: rearrangement of genetic material producing visible structural changes (deletion, duplication, inversion, translocation — e.g. Philadelphia chromosome).
  3. Gene mutation: partial/complete deletion of a gene or change in a single base.
    • Frameshift mutation: insertion or deletion of one or two base pairs shifts the reading frame (e.g. Tay-Sachs 4-base duplication).
    • Point mutation: substitution of one nucleotide — missense (one amino acid replaced — sickle cell anaemia), nonsense (creates stop codon — β0-thalassaemia), silent (no amino acid change).

Causes: spontaneous errors during DNA synthesis, or mutagens — UV rays, ionising radiation, anticancer drugs and viruses (e.g. rubella).

Ref: V1-ch06

5 What are the criteria of Mendelian disorders? Give classification with examples.

Criteria of Mendelian (single-gene) disorders:

  1. Result from mutation in a single gene with a large effect (monogenic).
  2. Phenotype is determined by the genotype at one locus, with little environmental contribution.
  3. Transmitted vertically through generations in a predictable Mendelian ratio (1:1 for autosomal dominant; 3:1 segregation with 25% affected for autosomal recessive).
  4. Distinct, consistent and recognisable phenotype; rare in the population.
  5. Both sexes are affected if the gene is autosomal; sex ratio is skewed if sex-linked.
  6. Occurs in homozygous (recessive) or heterozygous (dominant) state; a carrier state exists for recessive traits.

Classification with examples:

  1. Autosomal dominant — von Willebrand disease, Huntington disease.
  2. Autosomal recessive — thalassaemia, cystic fibrosis, sickle cell anaemia.
  3. X-linked recessive — haemophilia A and B, G6PD deficiency.
  4. X-linked dominant — vitamin D-resistant rickets, Alport syndrome (very rare).

Ref: V1-ch06

6 What are the criteria of autosomal dominant disorders? Give the examples of common autosomal dominant disorders.

Criteria / features of autosomal dominant inheritance:

  1. Manifested in the heterozygous state, so at least one parent of an index case is usually affected (vertical transmission).
  2. Both males and females are affected, and both can transmit the condition.
  3. No true carrier state — the heterozygote shows the phenotype.
  4. When an affected person marries an unaffected one, each child has a 1 in 2 (50%) chance of inheriting the disease; the risk is the same for every successive pregnancy.
  5. Clinical picture may be modified by reduced penetrance and variable expressivity.
  6. Onset may be delayed to adulthood (e.g. Huntington disease).
  7. Relatively rare and usually less severe than autosomal recessive disorders.

Common examples: Huntington disease, neurofibromatosis, myotonic dystrophy, tuberous sclerosis, polycystic kidney disease, familial polyposis coli, hereditary spherocytosis, von Willebrand disease, Marfan syndrome, Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, osteogenesis imperfecta, achondroplasia, familial hypercholesterolaemia, acute intermittent porphyria.

Ref: V1-ch06

7 What are the criteria of autosomal recessive disorders? Give the examples of common autosomal recessive disorders.

Criteria / features of autosomal recessive inheritance:

  1. Manifested only in the homozygous state; both alleles at the locus must be mutated.
  2. Heterozygotes are phenotypically normal carriers — parents of an affected child are usually unaffected, but siblings may show the disease.
  3. Recurrence risk is 25% for each birth (1 in 4 affected); 50% of sibs are carriers.
  4. Both sexes are affected with equal frequency.
  5. Increased risk among offspring of consanguineous marriages.
  6. Expression is more uniform than in dominant disorders, with complete penetrance and early onset in life.
  7. Largest category of Mendelian disorders — includes almost all inborn errors of metabolism.

Common examples: cystic fibrosis, phenylketonuria, galactosaemia, homocystinuria, lysosomal storage diseases (Tay-Sachs, Gaucher, Niemann-Pick, Hurler), α1-antitrypsin deficiency, Wilson disease, haemochromatosis, glycogen storage diseases, sickle cell anaemia, thalassaemias, congenital adrenal hyperplasia, alkaptonuria, Friedreich ataxia, spinal muscular atrophy.

Ref: V1-ch06

8 What are the criteria of X-linked disorders? Give the examples of common X-linked disorders.

Criteria / features of X-linked inheritance:

  • All sex-linked disorders are X-linked and almost all are recessive.
  • Affected individuals are usually male; females are usually carriers (heterozygous females do not express the full phenotype because of the paired normal allele and random X inactivation).
  • No male-to-male transmission — an affected male never passes the disorder to his sons, but all his daughters are carriers.
  • Sons of a heterozygous (carrier) woman have a 1 in 2 chance of receiving the mutant gene; her daughters have a 1 in 2 chance of being carriers.
  • For X-linked dominant conditions: both sexes affected (more females), all children of an affected female are affected, all daughters but none of the sons of an affected male are affected.

Examples: haemophilia A and B, Duchenne muscular dystrophy, G6PD deficiency, chronic granulomatous disease, agammaglobulinaemia, Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome, Lesch-Nyhan syndrome, fragile-X syndrome, colour blindness; dominant: vitamin D-resistant rickets, Alport syndrome.

Ref: V1-ch06

9 Mention the differences between autosomal dominant and autosomal recessive disorders. Why is there no Y-linked disorder?

Differences:

FeatureAutosomal dominantAutosomal recessive
Genotype expressed inHeterozygous stateHomozygous state only
Carrier stateNo carrier stateHeterozygous carrier, phenotypically normal
Parents of index caseAt least one usually affectedUsually unaffected
Recurrence risk50% of each pregnancy25% of each birth (50% carriers)
Sex distributionBoth sexes, equal; both transmitBoth sexes, equal
ConsanguinityNot increasedIncreased risk
Onset and severityOften adult onset, variable penetrance/expressivity, usually milder and rarerEarly onset, uniform expression, complete penetrance, often severe
TransmissionVertical, generation to generationHorizontal — appears in sibs of unaffected parents

Why no Y-linked disorder: The Y chromosome is small and carries very few genes (mainly genes for spermatogenesis such as DAZ in the AZF region). Most genes present on Y also have homologues on the X chromosome (pseudoautosomal region), so their function is duplicated and mutation does not produce a distinct disease phenotype. Y-linked traits that exist are non-disease traits (e.g. hairy ear tufts) or male infertility, so no classic Y-linked disease is recognised.

Ref: V1-ch06

10 What are the tests used to diagnose genetic diseases? What is a Barr body? How does Barr body detection help to diagnose genetic diseases?

Tests used to diagnose genetic diseases: karyotyping, gene sequencing, FISH (fluorescence in situ hybridisation), amniocentesis, chorionic villus sampling, umbilical cord blood sampling (antenatal diagnosis), Barr body study from buccal mucosa, and biochemical enzyme assays.

Barr body (sex chromatin): A clump of highly coiled, genetically inactive X chromatin seen in the interphase nuclei of somatic cells of females. Out of the two X chromosomes, one is inactivated and remains condensed, appearing as a Barr body. It is found in buccal mucosal cells and polymorphonuclear leucocytes, and is used for nuclear sexing.

How detection helps: The number of Barr bodies = total number of X chromosomes − 1, so it screens sex-chromosome aneuploidies in cases of ambiguous sexual development, infertility and primary amenorrhoea, and confirms genetic sex.

High yield: Barr bodies = number of X chromosomes − 1. Normal female (XX) = 1; normal male (XY) = 0; Klinefelter (XXY) = 1; triple X (XXX) = 2; Turner (XO) = 0.

Ref: V1-ch06

11 What is karyotyping? How is it performed? What are diseases diagnosed by karyotyping?

Karyotyping: The study of the complete set of chromosomes of a cell, presented as a cellular chromosome profile in which photographed metaphase chromosomes are arranged in order of decreasing length, pairs 1–22 followed by the sex chromosomes. It is the basic tool of the cytogeneticist.

Procedure:

  1. Dividing cells (peripheral blood lymphocytes, amniocytes, chorionic villi, fibroblasts) are cultured and mitosis is arrested in metaphase with a mitotic spindle inhibitor (colcemid/colchicine).
  2. Hypotonic treatment swells the cells; cells are fixed and spread on a slide.
  3. Chromosomes are stained, commonly with Giemsa (G-banding).
  4. The metaphase spread is photographed and each pair is arranged by length; the sex chromosomes are added last.
  5. Notation: total chromosome number first, then sex complement, then abnormality in ascending order — e.g. 47,XY,+21.

Diseases diagnosed: Down, Edwards and Patau syndromes; Turner and Klinefelter syndromes; other sex-chromosome aneuploidies; large structural errors such as cri-du-chat (5p−) and Robertsonian translocation Down syndrome; unexplained infertility, recurrent abortion and ambiguous genitalia.

Ref: V1-ch06

12 Shortly describe the principle of PCR. How does it help in genetic disease diagnosis?

Principle of PCR (polymerase chain reaction): It is an in vitro method for exponential amplification of a specific segment of DNA. The reaction contains the target DNA, two sequence-specific oligonucleotide primers, deoxynucleotides and a heat-stable DNA polymerase (Taq polymerase from Thermus aquaticus). Each cycle has three steps:

  1. Denaturation at 94–96 °C — separation of the double-stranded DNA.
  2. Annealing at 50–60 °C — binding of primers to the complementary target sequences.
  3. Extension at 72 °C — Taq polymerase copies each strand, doubling the amount of target DNA. After 30–35 cycles, millions of copies are produced.

Help in genetic disease diagnosis: needs only a minute amount of DNA (even degraded/paraffin tissue) and gives rapid results; detects point mutations, small deletions and expanded trinucleotide repeats (sickle cell mutation, β-thalassaemia, Huntington/CAG repeats); used for prenatal diagnosis from chorionic villi or amniotic fluid, carrier and neonatal screening, HLA typing, and detection of microbial DNA (HPV, TB, HIV) and in forensic identification.

Ref: V1-ch06

13 Briefly describe the various types of sexual ambiguity. What are the features of Klinefelter syndrome?

Types of sexual ambiguity (intersex states):

  1. Male pseudohermaphroditism: 46,XY genotype with testes present but imperfectly masculinised external genitalia — androgen insensitivity syndrome, 5α-reductase deficiency.
  2. Female pseudohermaphroditism: 46,XX genotype with ovaries present but virilised external genitalia — usually congenital adrenal hyperplasia.
  3. True hermaphroditism: both ovarian and testicular tissue (ovotestes) present, with ambiguous genitalia.
  4. Gonadal dysgenesis: streak (fibrous) gonads with ambiguous or infantile genitalia — e.g. Turner syndrome 45,X.

Features of Klinefelter syndrome: Male hypogonadism with two or more X chromosomes and at least one Y (karyotype 47,XXY in about 90%); incidence 1 in 600 male births. Features include small atrophic testes, small penis, infertility with reduced spermatogenesis, lack of secondary male characteristics (deep voice, beard, male pubic hair pattern), gynaecomastia, tall elongated body with long legs, slightly low IQ (mental retardation uncommon), increased type 2 diabetes and metabolic syndrome, mitral valve prolapse, osteoporosis and fractures.

Ref: V1-ch06

14 What is the Lyon hypothesis of X inactivation? What are the features of Turner syndrome?

Lyon hypothesis (X inactivation): In normal females one of the two X chromosomes in each somatic cell is randomly and permanently inactivated early in embryonic development; the inactive, highly coiled X appears as the Barr body. Consequences: (1) females are functional mosaics — some cells express the maternal X, others the paternal X; (2) heterozygous carriers of X-linked disorders may show patchy expression because cells expressing the normal allele survive in some tissue areas (e.g. anhidrotic ectodermal dysplasia, ocular albinism); (3) this is why carrier females are usually mildly affected or unaffected.

Features of Turner syndrome: Complete or partial monosomy X (45,X) in a phenotypic female; 1 in 2000 live-born females — the most common sex-chromosome abnormality in females and the most common cause of primary amenorrhoea. Features: short stature, webbed neck, low posterior hairline, broad chest with widely spaced nipples, peripheral lymphoedema at birth, cubitus valgus, pigmented nevi, coarctation of aorta, infantile genitalia and poor breast development, streak ovaries, infertility, primary amenorrhoea.

Ref: V1-ch06

15 Write the karyotypes of Klinefelter and Down syndromes. Give the features of Down syndrome.

Klinefelter syndrome karyotype: classic pattern 47,XXY (about 90% of cases); mosaic 46,XY/47,XXY (about 15%); other variants 47,XXY/48,XXXY.

Down syndrome karyotype:

  1. Trisomy 21 type (about 95%) — 47,XY,+21 or 47,XX,+21.
  2. Translocation type (about 4%) — 46,XX,der(14;21)(q10;q10),+21.
  3. Mosaic type (about 1%) — 46,XX/47,XX,+21.

Features of Down syndrome: Most common chromosomal disorder (1 in 700 births) and a major cause of intellectual disability.

  • Flat facial profile, oblique palpebral fissures and epicanthic folds.
  • Single palmar (simian) crease; increased gap between first and second toe.
  • Severe mental retardation (IQ 25–50); muscular hypotonia.
  • Congenital heart disease — ostium primum defect, atrial and septal defects, AV valve malformations.
  • 10–20 fold increased risk of acute leukaemia (both ALL and AML).
  • Alzheimer-type neuropathology after 40 years; umbilical hernia; abnormal immune response predisposing to serious infection.

Ref: V1-ch06

16 Define hereditary and congenital diseases with examples. Name some familial cancers.

Hereditary disease: A disease caused by a mutant gene transmitted in the germ line from parent to offspring in a Mendelian pattern; the mutant gene is present in every cell of the body. Examples: sickle cell anaemia, thalassaemia, cystic fibrosis, Huntington disease, phenylketonuria.

Congenital disease: Any abnormality present at birth, whether inherited or acquired during intrauterine life from an environmental/teratogenic cause. Examples: congenital heart defects (rubella, fetal alcohol syndrome), cleft lip and palate, congenital hypothyroidism, neural tube defects.

Key difference: All hereditary diseases are not necessarily apparent at birth, and all congenital diseases are not hereditary.

Familial cancers: cancers that cluster in families because of an inherited predisposition —

  • Breast and ovarian cancer (BRCA1/BRCA2)
  • Colorectal cancer — familial adenomatous polyposis (APC gene) and Lynch syndrome (hMLH1, hMSH2 mismatch repair genes)
  • Retinoblastoma (RB1) and osteosarcoma
  • Li-Fraumeni syndrome (TP53) — sarcoma, leukaemia, breast and brain tumours
  • Medullary thyroid carcinoma and phaeochromocytoma (MEN 2 — RET)
  • Familial gastric cancer, familial prostate cancer

Ref: V1-ch06

ENVIRONMENTAL DISORDERS
17 What are the various forms of tobacco used? List main diseases caused or aggravated by tobacco use.

Forms of tobacco used:

  • Smoked: cigarettes, bidis, cigars, pipe, hookah (waterpipe).
  • Smokeless / oral: chewing tobacco, gutka and betel quid with tobacco, snuff (smoked or smokeless), mishri, tooth powder with tobacco.
  • Sniffed: nasal snuff.

Diseases caused or aggravated by tobacco use:

  • Respiratory — asthma, COPD (emphysema, chronic bronchitis), worsens pneumoconiosis.
  • Cardiovascular — atherosclerosis, myocardial infarction, peripheral vascular disease, cerebrovascular disease (stroke), hypercholesterolaemia, hypertension.
  • Gastrointestinal — peptic ulcer disease, increased gastro-oesophageal reflux.
  • Maternal smoking — abortion, preterm birth, intrauterine growth retardation, low birth weight, placenta praevia and abruption.
  • Cancers — lung, lip and oral cavity, larynx, pharynx, oesophagus, pancreas, urinary bladder, kidney, cervix and bone marrow (AML).
High yield: Carcinogens in tobacco — polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons, NNK, N-nitrosonornicotine (NNN), polonium-210, 4-aminobiphenyl and 2-naphthylamine.

Ref: V1-ch09

18 What are the diseases caused by chronic arsenic exposure? How does chronic arsenic exposure commonly occur?

How chronic exposure commonly occurs:

  • Most commonly by drinking arsenic-contaminated groundwater (arsenic-bearing artesian/bore-well wells — a major problem in Bangladesh and West Bengal); also contaminated well-irrigated crops.
  • Occupational — smelting of copper, lead and zinc ores, mining, pesticide/herbicide manufacture and spraying, wood preservatives (chromated copper arsenate), glass and semiconductor industries.
  • Other sources — contaminated wine and beer, cigarette smoke, burning of arsenic-treated wood.

Diseases of chronic arsenic exposure:

  • Skin (earliest signs): raindrop pigmentation of the trunk, palmo-plantar hyperkeratosis, leukomelanosis, arsenical keratoses — which may undergo malignant change to squamous cell carcinoma and basal cell carcinoma; Bowen disease (in situ carcinoma).
  • Nervous system: peripheral sensory neuropathy (glove-and-stocking), encephalopathy.
  • Cancers: lung, urinary bladder, kidney, skin and liver (angiosarcoma, especially with vinyl chloride exposure).
  • Circulation: Blackfoot disease — peripheral vascular disease with gangrene of the lower extremity; also diabetes mellitus and cardiovascular disease.
  • Blood: anaemia, leukopenia, thrombocytopenia.

Ref: V1-ch09

19 What are the forms of radiation exposure? Mention main biological effects of ionising radiation and examples.

Forms of radiation exposure:

  1. By type — ionising (x-rays, gamma rays, alpha and beta particles, high-energy neutrons) and non-ionising (ultraviolet, infrared, radiofrequency/microwaves).
  2. By route — external exposure (whole-body or partial-body irradiation from a source outside the body) and internal exposure (inhaled or ingested radionuclides such as radon, I-131, Sr-90).
  3. By source — diagnostic/therapeutic (medical), occupational (radiographers, nuclear industry), accidental (radiological disaster, nuclear explosion) and environmental (cosmic rays, radon gas).

Main biological effects of ionising radiation:

  • Acute radiation syndrome (large whole-body dose) — subclinical stage (mild nausea, lymphocytes below 1500/µL), haematopoietic stage (petechiae, infection; neutrophil and platelet depression at 2 weeks), gastrointestinal stage (bloody diarrhoea, haemorrhage and infection in 1–3 weeks), CNS stage (confusion, convulsions, coma and death).
  • Local deterministic effects — erythema, epilation, skin desquamation, cataracts, sterility, growth retardation in children, delayed fibrosis and ulceration.
  • Mutagenesis — heritable point mutations and chromosomal breaks (e.g. chromatid gaps, translocations).
  • Carcinogenesis — leukaemia (2–5 years later), thyroid, breast, lung and bladder cancer.
  • Teratogenesis — microcephaly, growth retardation and organ malformations when exposure is in 8–15 weeks of gestation.

Ref: V1-ch09

20 Mention two chronic effects of alcohol on the liver. Name the cancers occurring from tobacco use.

Two chronic effects of alcohol on the liver:

  1. Alcoholic hepatitis (on a background of fatty change/steatosis): ballooning hepatocytes, Mallory-Denk bodies, neutrophilic infiltration and perivenular fibrosis — the characteristic lesion of alcoholic liver injury.
  2. Alcoholic cirrhosis: micronodular cirrhosis that later becomes macronodular, leading to portal hypertension and carrying an increased risk of hepatocellular carcinoma.

(Other chronic hepatic consequences are portal hypertension, ascites and hepatic encephalopathy; the whole sequence is fatty liver → hepatitis → cirrhosis.)

Cancers occurring from tobacco use: cancer of the lung, lip and oral cavity, larynx, pharynx, oesophagus, pancreas, urinary bladder, kidney, cervix, and bone marrow (acute myeloid leukaemia). Secondhand smoke also causes lung cancer in non-smokers.

Ref: V1-ch09

NUTRITIONAL DISORDERS
21 What is malnutrition? Mention its various forms. What are common causes of malnutrition in Bangladesh?

Malnutrition: Malnutrition is any condition resulting from an inadequate, excess or unbalanced intake of energy and nutrients — it includes undernutrition, overnutrition and micronutrient imbalance.

Forms:

  1. Undernutrition: protein–energy malnutrition (kwashiorkor, marasmus, marasmic kwashiorkor), growth failure (wasting, stunting, underweight), and micronutrient deficiency (iron, iodine, vitamin A, zinc).
  2. Overnutrition: overweight and obesity, and hypervitaminosis/toxic excess of nutrients.
  3. Specific deficiency states: scurvy, beriberi, pellagra, rickets (disease caused by a single nutrient defect).

Common causes of malnutrition in Bangladesh:

  • Poverty, food insecurity and inadequate diet in both quality and quantity.
  • Frequent infections and parasitic diseases — diarrhoea, measles, intestinal worms (increased requirements + reduced intake/absorption).
  • Early weaning, premature termination of breastfeeding and failure of lactation; poor infant and young child feeding practice.
  • Ignorance and lack of nutrition education, illiterate mothers, large family size.
  • Poor environmental and sanitation conditions, recurrent floods and river erosion, high prices of food.
  • Maternal malnutrition and low birth weight babies; chronic disease and malabsorption.

Ref: V1-ch09

22 Define and classify PEM. Mention the differences between the types.

Protein energy malnutrition (PEM): A range of clinical syndromes characterised by inadequate dietary intake of protein and calories to meet the body's needs.

Classification (WHO): kwashiorkor, marasmic kwashiorkor, marasmus, nutritional dwarfing, underweight child.

Differences between marasmus and kwashiorkor:

FeatureMarasmusKwashiorkor
Basic defectTotal calorie deprivationProtein deprivation greater than calorie loss
OedemaNoneDependent (lower legs), sometimes face/generalised
WastingMarked — severe muscle wasting ("skin and bones")Less obvious, masked by oedema
WeightFalls to about 60% of normal60–80% of normal
Mental changesUsually nil, child is alertUsually present — apathy and irritability
AppetiteUsually goodUsually poor (anorexia)
Skin and hairUsually no skin change; hair may change texture"Flaky paint" dermatosis, diffuse pigmentation, sparse straight silky hair, moon face
LiverNot enlargedEnlarged fatty liver
High yield: Kwashiorkor = PROTEIN off → oedema, flaky-paint skin, fatty liver, apathy. Marasmus = CALORIES off → severe wasting, no oedema, alert but hungry child. Marasmic kwashiorkor = both.

Ref: V1-ch09

23 Define obesity. How is BMI calculated? Classify obesity according to BMI.

Obesity: Obesity is a disorder of energy regulation, defined as accumulation of adipose tissue of sufficient magnitude to impair health.

Calculation of BMI: BMI (body mass index) is the ratio of body weight in kilograms to the square of height in metres — expressed in kg/m².

BMI = body weight (kg) / (height in m)². Example: 70 kg / (1.7 m)² = 24.2 kg/m².

Classification according to BMI:

CategoryBMI (kg/m²)
Underweight< 18.5
Normal18.5–24.9
Overweight25.0–29.9
Obesity class I30.0–34.9
Obesity class II35.0–39.9
Extreme (morbid) obesity≥ 40.0

Complications: type 2 diabetes, insulin resistance, dyslipidaemia, hypertension, atherosclerosis and coronary artery disease, NAFLD, gallstones, obstructive sleep apnoea, osteoarthritis, and increased risk of cancers of colon, breast, uterus, kidney and gallbladder.

Ref: V1-ch09

24 What are common causes of vitamin deficiency? What are the manifestations of folic acid deficiency?

Common causes of vitamin deficiency:

  1. Inadequate intake: poverty, monotonous/poor diet, exclusive tea and rice diet, alcoholism, starvation, strict vegetarianism (B12), old age with poor dentition.
  2. Malabsorption: coeliac disease, tropical sprue, chronic diarrhoea, pancreatic or biliary insufficiency, short bowel/gastric bypass.
  3. Increased demand: pregnancy, lactation, infancy, adolescence, infection, hyperthyroidism.
  4. Impaired utilisation or storage: chronic liver disease; inborn errors of vitamin metabolism.
  5. Drugs: methotrexate (folate), isoniazid (B6), phenytoin and oral contraceptives (folate), trimethoprim, nitrous oxide (B12).

Manifestations of folic acid (vitamin B9) deficiency:

  • Megaloblastic (macrocytic) anaemia with hypersegmented neutrophils; glossitis, sore tongue, mouth ulcers; in severe cases leucopenia and thrombocytopenia.
  • Raised homocysteine — increased cardiovascular risk.
  • Deficiency in early pregnancy causes neural tube defects — spina bifida, anencephaly, encephalocele — plus low birth weight and spontaneous abortion.
  • Impaired cell division also causes diarrhoea, poor growth and possibly cognitive decline/depression.

Ref: V1-ch09

25 What are the manifestations of vitamin A deficiency? Why is vitamin A supplementation needed during measles/pneumonia?

Manifestations of vitamin A deficiency:

  • Eye: night blindness (earliest symptom) → xerophthalmia — dryness of conjunctiva (xerosis), Bitot spots (foamy keratinised patches), keratomalacia, corneal ulceration and irreversible blindness.
  • Skin and mucosa: follicular and papular dermatosis (phrynoderma), epithelial metaplasia and keratinisation, squamous metaplasia of respiratory and urinary tract.
  • Urinary: renal and urinary bladder stones.
  • Immune: immune deficiency with vulnerability to infection, particularly measles, pneumonia and infectious diarrhoea.

Why supplementation during measles and pneumonia: Measles rapidly depletes hepatic vitamin A stores and greatly increases requirement and loss; deficiency increases the severity and mortality of measles, pneumonia and persistent diarrhoea (the "vicious cycle" of infection and deficiency), and causes xerophthalmic blindness. WHO therefore recommends high-dose vitamin A — 200,000 IU orally on each of two consecutive days for children aged 1 year and above (100,000 IU for infants 6–12 months) — in every child with measles; it reduces case fatality, diarrhoea, lung complications and mortality, and is a cheap, high-impact measure.

Ref: V1-ch09

26 Why is vitamin A prohibited during pregnancy? What vitamins and mineral supplementation are needed during pregnancy?

Why vitamin A is prohibited (avoided) in pregnancy: Preformed vitamin A (retinol) and its metabolite retinoic acid are teratogenic, especially during the first trimester when organogenesis occurs. Excess intake (generally above 10,000 IU/day) causes the retinoic-acid (isotretinoin) embryopathy — craniofacial malformations (microtia, ear anomalies), cardiac conotruncal defects, hydrocephalus and thymic aplasia, leading to miscarriage and fetal malformation. Because retinol is fat-soluble and stored in the maternal liver (no safe large bolus), high-dose supplements and retinoid drugs (isotretinoin, etretinate) are contraindicated. Provitamin A (β-carotene) is safe because its conversion to retinol is regulated.

Vitamin and mineral supplementation needed in pregnancy:

  • Folic acid 400 µg/day (4 mg/day for high-risk women with previous neural tube defect), started before conception and continued to at least 12 weeks.
  • Iron 30–60 mg/day (with vitamin C) — prevents iron-deficiency anaemia, reduces preterm birth and low birth weight.
  • Calcium 1000–1200 mg/day — prevents pre-eclampsia and maternal bone loss.
  • Iodine 250 µg/day — prevents goitre and cretinism.
  • Vitamin D 400 IU/day (more if deficient) for calcium homeostasis; vitamin B12 in vegetarians; zinc as part of antenatal micronutrients.

Ref: V1-ch09

27 What are the manifestations of vitamin D deficiency? Shortly describe vitamin D metabolism.

Manifestations of vitamin D deficiency:

  • Children (rickets): impaired mineralisation of growing bone — craniotabes, frontal bossing, delayed fontanelle closure, rachitic rosary (beading at costochondral junctions), pigeon chest, widened wrists, bowing of legs, delayed walking.
  • Adults (osteomalacia): bone pain, muscle weakness, pseudofractures (Looser zones), fractures.
  • Metabolic: hypocalcaemia → paraesthesiae, tetany, carpopedal spasm, seizures; secondary hyperparathyroidism → osteoporosis and renal calcium stones; in children hypocalcaemic seizures.

Vitamin D metabolism:

  1. Skin: 7-dehydrocholesterol is converted by UVB sunlight to vitamin D3 (cholecalciferol); dietary D3 (fish, egg yolk, fortified milk) and D2 (ergocalciferol, plants/yeast) also contribute.
  2. Liver: 25-hydroxylation forms 25-hydroxyvitamin D (calcidiol) — the circulating form measured in blood.
  3. Kidney: 1α-hydroxylase (stimulated by PTH and low serum calcium/phosphate; inhibited by FGF-23) forms the active hormone 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D (calcitriol).
  4. Action: calcitriol binds the nuclear vitamin D receptor in intestinal mucosa to increase calcium and phosphate absorption, acts on bone (mineralisation and resorption with PTH) and kidney (calcium reabsorption), and feeds back to suppress PTH. Excess is inactivated by 24-hydroxylation.

Ref: V1-ch09

28 What are the manifestations of iron and iodine deficiency? What are the manifestations of vitamin C deficiency?

Iron deficiency: Iron-deficiency anaemia — microcytic, hypochromic anaemia with raised RDW and target/pencil cells; pallor, fatigue, exertional dyspnoea and palpitation. Tissue signs: koilonychia (spoon-shaped nails), glossitis, angular stomatitis/cheilitis, dysphagia (Paterson-Kelly syndrome), brittle hair and hair fall. Behavioural: pica and pagophagia (ice craving), restless legs, irritability, impaired growth and cognitive development in children; increased susceptibility to infection. Iron deficiency is the most common nutritional deficiency worldwide.

Iodine deficiency: endemic goitre (diffuse → nodular), hypothyroidism and myxoedema; in pregnancy and infancy it causes cretinism — intellectual disability, deaf-mutism, squint, growth retardation and spasticity; also increased spontaneous abortion, stillbirth, placental abruption and low birth weight.

Vitamin C deficiency (scurvy): Haemorrhage from mucous membranes of mouth and GIT; swollen, spongy, bleeding gums with loose teeth; subcutaneous haemorrhage, ecchymoses and perifollicular haemorrhage; corkscrew hairs with follicular keratosis; delayed wound healing with breakdown of old scars; muscle weakness; anaemia (impaired iron and folate metabolism); in children the scorbutic rosary, epiphyseal separation and irritability. Prevention is by citrus fruits and fresh vegetables; treatment is vitamin C 250 mg orally three times daily.

Ref: V1-ch09

DISORDERS OF CHILDHOOD
29 What are the common congenital anomalies? What is TORCH infection?

Common congenital anomalies:

  • Congenital heart disease — VSD (most common), ASD, PDA, tetralogy of Fallot, coarctation of aorta.
  • Neural tube defects — anencephaly, meningomyelocele, encephalocele.
  • Cleft lip and cleft palate; tracheo-oesophageal fistula; imperforate anus.
  • Down syndrome and other chromosomal disorders; congenital hypothyroidism, phenylketonuria.
  • Orthopaedic — congenital dislocation of hip, club foot (talipes equinovarus), polydactyly/syndactyly.
  • Genitourinary — hypospadias, cryptorchidism, hydronephrosis, PUJ obstruction; also congenital infections, retinoblastoma and neonatal respiratory distress syndrome.

TORCH infection: A group of infections acquired transplacentally (or during delivery) from mother to fetus that produce a characteristic, overlapping set of congenital abnormalities.

Common features of TORCH infections: intrauterine growth retardation and low birth weight, prematurity, hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice/hepatitis, thrombocytopenic purpura ("blueberry muffin" rash), microcephaly or hydrocephaly, chorioretinitis, cataracts, sensorineural deafness, congenital heart defects, intellectual disability and neonatal seizures.

  • T — Toxoplasma: chorioretinitis, hydrocephalus, intracranial calcification (classic triad).
  • O — Other: syphilis (snuffles, Hutchinson teeth), varicella-zoster, parvovirus B19 (hydrops fetalis), Zika (microcephaly), HIV.
  • R — Rubella: cataracts, sensorineural deafness, PDA (Gregg's triad).
  • C — Cytomegalovirus: sensorineural deafness, periventricular calcifications, microcephaly, hepatosplenomegaly — the commonest congenital infection.
  • H — Herpes simplex: neonatal hepatitis, vesicular skin lesions, encephalitis.
High yield: TORCH = Toxoplasma, Other, Rubella, CMV, Herpes. All cross the placenta and cause IUGR, hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice, thrombocytopenia and eye/CNS damage — remember "petechiae + chorioretinitis + deafness".

Ref: V1-ch10

30 Classify the tumors of childhood. What are the small round blue cell tumors?

Classification of tumors of childhood (by organ/system):

  1. Haematolymphoid (most common childhood cancer group): acute lymphoblastic leukaemia (most common childhood cancer overall), non-Hodgkin lymphoma (Burkitt lymphoma), Langerhans cell histiocytosis, histiocytic disorders.
  2. Nervous system: astrocytoma, medulloblastoma, primitive neuroectodermal tumour (PNET), neuroblastoma (adrenal/sympathetic chain), retinoblastoma (eye).
  3. Renal: Wilms tumour (nephroblastoma), mesoblastic nephroma.
  4. Liver: hepatoblastoma, hepatocellular carcinoma.
  5. Bone and soft tissue: osteosarcoma, Ewing sarcoma, rhabdomyosarcoma (embryonal and alveolar), desmoplastic small round cell tumour.
  6. Germ cell and trophoblastic: mature teratoma, yolk sac tumour.
  7. Other: thyroid carcinoma, melanoma.

Small round blue cell tumors (SRBCT): A group of highly cellular, primitive malignant tumors, mainly of childhood, whose cells are small and round with scant cytoplasm and hyperchromatic nuclei, so they appear blue on H&E staining. The group includes: neuroblastoma, Wilms tumor, Ewing sarcoma, rhabdomyosarcoma (embryonal/alveolar), lymphoblastic lymphoma/leukaemia, medulloblastoma/PNET, retinoblastoma and desmoplastic small round cell tumor. They are distinguished by immunohistochemistry and cytogenetics — e.g. desmin and myogenin (rhabdomyosarcoma), CD45 (lymphoma), WT1 (Wilms and DSRCT), PAS-positive glycogen (Ewing), NSE/S-100 (neuroblastoma); t(11;22) in Ewing sarcoma and t(2;13) in alveolar rhabdomyosarcoma.

Ref: V1-ch10

ITEM 21 — Lymphoreticular System
1 Name the organs of the lympho-reticular system. Describe the features of chronic non-specific lymphadenitis.

Organs of the lympho-reticular (lymphoid) system:

  • Central (generative) organs: thymus and bone marrow — the sites of lymphocyte production and maturation.
  • Peripheral (secondary) organs: lymph nodes, spleen and mucosa-associated lymphoid tissue (tonsils, Peyer's patches, appendix, Waldeyer's ring).
  • Diffuse / communicating lymphoid tissue: lymphatic vessels and lymphoid follicles in the gut, skin and other viscera.

Chronic non-specific lymphadenitis: reactive, non-granulomatous enlargement of a lymph node in response to a non-specific antigenic stimulus; the architecture is preserved and no organism-specific lesion is formed.

  • Nodes are enlarged, soft to firm, painless, discrete or slightly matted.
  • Follicular (cortical) hyperplasia: numerous enlarged secondary follicles with active germinal centres.
  • Paracortical hyperplasia: diffuse increase of T-zone lymphocytes and immunoblasts.
  • Sinus histiocytosis: sinuses distended with macrophages — very characteristic.
  • Mixed infiltrate of lymphocytes, plasma cells and macrophages; no granuloma, no caseation, no Reed-Sternberg cells.
  • Regresses when the stimulus is removed; long-standing cases show fibrosis of the node.
High yield: the three patterns of reactive (non-specific) lymphadenitis = follicular hyperplasia + paracortical hyperplasia + sinus histiocytosis. A granuloma means chronic specific lymphadenitis (TB, sarcoidosis, syphilis).

Ref: V2-ch03

2 What is generalized lymphadenopathy? What are the causes of generalized lymphadenopathy?

Definition: Generalized lymphadenopathy is enlargement of lymph nodes belonging to two or more anatomical groups (regions), in contrast to localized enlargement of a single group such as isolated cervical nodes.

Causes:

  • Infection:
    • Viral — infectious mononucleosis, CMV, AIDS (HIV)
    • Bacterial — disseminated tuberculosis, brucellosis, cat-scratch disease
    • Spirochaetal — secondary syphilis
    • Protozoal — toxoplasmosis; Helminthic — filariasis
    • Fungal — histoplasmosis, coccidioidomycosis
  • Malignant disease: lymphoma, leukaemia.
  • Collagen (autoimmune) disease: systemic lupus erythematosus, rheumatoid arthritis.
  • Miscellaneous: sarcoidosis, thyrotoxicosis, drugs (e.g. phenytoin).

Ref: V2-ch03

3 What is lymphoma? Classify it. Write down the causes of splenomegaly.

Definition of lymphoma: Neoplastic proliferation of the cells native to the lymph nodes — the lymphocytes and their precursors and the histiocytes (monocyte-derived cells). It is a malignant (clonal) proliferation.

Classification of lymphoma:

GroupSubtypesHallmark
Hodgkin lymphomaClassical: nodular sclerosis, mixed cellularity, lymphocyte rich, lymphocyte depleted; Non-classical: lymphocyte predominantReed-Sternberg (RS) giant cells with a pleomorphic background of lymphocytes, eosinophils, macrophages, plasma cells and follicular dendritic cells
Non-Hodgkin lymphomaBy REAL/WHO: precursor B-cell, mature (peripheral) B-cell (85%), precursor T-cell, mature T/NK-cell (15%); by Working formulation: low, intermediate and high gradeMonomorphic proliferation; RS cells absent

Causes of splenomegaly:

  • Infections: infectious mononucleosis, infective endocarditis, TB, typhoid, brucellosis, CMV, syphilis, malaria, histoplasmosis, toxoplasmosis, kala-azar, trypanosomiasis, schistosomiasis, echinococcosis.
  • Congestive (portal hypertension): cirrhosis, portal or splenic vein thrombosis, cardiac failure.
  • Lympho-haematogenous: Hodgkin and non-Hodgkin lymphoma, multiple myeloma, myeloproliferative disorders, haemolytic anaemias.
  • Immunologic-inflammatory: rheumatoid arthritis, SLE. Storage: Gaucher, Niemann-Pick, mucopolysaccharidoses.
  • Miscellaneous: amyloidosis, primary and secondary neoplasms and cysts.

Ref: V2-ch03

4 Give the differences between Hodgkin and non-Hodgkin lymphoma.
TraitHodgkin lymphomaNon-Hodgkin lymphoma
Reed-Sternberg cellsPresent (hallmark)Absent
Age incidenceYoung adults, 20-40 yearsAny age; peak 65-70 years
SpreadContiguous, to anatomically neighbouring lymphoid tissueRapid, non-contiguous (skip) spread
Extra-nodal involvementLess commonMore common (marrow, gut, stomach, lung, thyroid, skin, testis, CNS)
Systemic (B) symptomsMore common (fever, night sweats, weight loss)Less common
PruritusMore commonLess common
Bone marrow / leukaemic transformationRareCommon
PrognosisHigh cure rateLow cure rate

Ref: V2-ch03

5 Give the lab diagnosis of lymphoma. How staging is done in lymphoma cases?

Laboratory diagnosis:

  • CBC with ESR and peripheral blood film (eosinophilia, atypical cells, raised ESR).
  • Chest X-ray (bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy, mediastinal mass) and USG of the whole abdomen (hepatosplenomegaly, para-aortic nodes).
  • CT scan of neck, chest, abdomen and pelvis and PET scan — required for staging.
  • Excisional lymph node biopsy (preferred) or image-guided core needle biopsy, followed by histopathology and immunohistochemistry — confirmatory; FNAC is less useful.
  • Bone marrow biopsy (trephine from iliac crest), LFT and RFT.

Staging — Ann Arbor classification:

StageDistribution of disease
ISingle lymph node region (I) or single extralymphatic organ/site (IE)
IITwo or more node regions on the same side of the diaphragm, with or without localized extralymphatic involvement (IIE)
IIINode regions on both sides of the diaphragm, with (IIIE) or without extralymphatic involvement
IVDiffuse involvement of one or more extralymphatic organs, with or without lymph node involvement

Each stage is subdivided into A (no systemic symptoms) or B (unexplained fever, drenching night sweats, weight loss of more than 10% of body weight).

Ref: V2-ch03

6 Classify Hodgkin lymphoma. Give the salient features of Hodgkin lymphoma.

WHO classification: (A) Classical Hodgkin lymphoma — 95% of cases: nodular sclerosis, mixed cellularity, lymphocyte rich, lymphocyte depleted; (B) Non-classical — lymphocyte predominant (5%), where classic RS cells are absent or rare.

SubtypeFrequencyKey features
Nodular sclerosisMost common (about 65%)Young women; broad fibrous bands producing a nodular pattern; lacunar cells; T cells, eosinophils, macrophages
Mixed cellularity20-25%Plenty of classic RS cells in a background of T lymphocytes, eosinophils, macrophages, plasma cells
Lymphocyte rich5%Scanty RS cells, background rich in T lymphocytes
Lymphocyte depletedRareFew lymphocytes, numerous RS cells, extensive necrosis and fibrosis
Lymphocyte predominant5%RS cells of L and H (popcorn) type only; classic RS cells absent; behaves like NHL

Salient features:

  • Affects young adults (20-40 years); males about twice as commonly as females.
  • Arises in a single node or group of nodes and spreads first to anatomically contiguous lymphoid tissue.
  • Painless, rubbery, discrete peripheral lymphadenopathy (may become matted); hepatosplenomegaly.
  • Systemic B symptoms — fever (Pel-Ebstein type), night sweats, weight loss, pruritus.
  • Hallmark: Reed-Sternberg cells, which are CD15 and CD30 positive but CD45 negative.

Ref: V2-ch03

7 Classify non-Hodgkin lymphoma. Which lymphoma has better prognosis and why?

Classification: NHL is a monoclonal proliferation of B cells (85%) or T/NK cells (15%). Two systems are used:

  • Working classification — three prognostic groups according to the rate of cell division (see table).
  • REAL / WHO classification — based on immunophenotype, genetic aberrations and morphologic-clinical features: precursor and mature B-cell lymphomas (follicular, mantle cell, marginal zone, diffuse large B-cell, Burkitt) and precursor and mature T/NK-cell lymphomas (adult T-cell, cutaneous T-cell — mycosis fungoides and Sezary syndrome, anaplastic large cell).
GradeBehaviourExamples
Low gradeLow proliferation; asymptomatic for years; slow, indolent course; incurable but long survivalFollicular lymphoma, small lymphocytic lymphoma, marginal zone (MALT) lymphoma
Intermediate gradeModerate proliferationDiffuse large B-cell lymphoma
High gradeHigh proliferation; early symptoms; fatal if untreated, but potentially curableBurkitt lymphoma, lymphoblastic lymphoma

Better prognosis: The low-grade (indolent) NHL has the best natural-history prognosis: a low cell proliferation rate means years of asymptomatic, slow disease and long survival, although it is ultimately incurable and may transform into a high-grade lymphoma. High-grade NHL is fatal if untreated, but it responds better to treatment and is potentially curable. Overall, Hodgkin lymphoma still has a higher cure rate than NHL.

Ref: V2-ch03

8 Name some tumour giant cells. Draw a classical Reed-Sternberg cell.

Tumour giant cells: large, often bizarre cells formed by nuclear division without division of the cytoplasm; the nuclei show the characteristics of malignancy. Examples:

  • Reed-Sternberg cell — the tumour giant cell of Hodgkin lymphoma.
  • Pleomorphic (anaplastic) giant cells in undifferentiated tumours, e.g. poorly differentiated astrocytoma and pleomorphic sarcoma.
  • Syncytiotrophoblastic giant cells in choriocarcinoma; osteoclast-like giant cells in giant cell tumour of bone.
  • Unlike Langhans and foreign-body giant cells (inflammatory, formed by fusion of macrophages), tumour giant cells show malignancy.

Drawn and labelled classical (typical) RS cell — a large tumour giant cell about 45 micrometre in diameter, with:

  • A bi-lobed nucleus, the two halves appearing as mirror images of each other.
  • Each lobe containing one large inclusion-like nucleolus surrounded by a clear halo.
  • Abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm around the nucleus.
  • Reactive background of non-malignant T-lymphocytes, plasma cells and eosinophils.
  • Variants: mononuclear RS cell, lacunar cell (nodular sclerosis) and L and H popcorn cell (lymphocyte predominant).
High yield: nucleolus + clear halo = classic "owl's eye" appearance; RS cells are of B-cell origin, CD15 and CD30 positive but CD45 negative. RS-like cells also occur in infectious mononucleosis and CLL.

Ref: V2-ch03

ITEM 22 — Haematopoiesis and Bone Marrow
1 What is hematopoiesis? How it occurs (steps of hematopoiesis)? How it is examined in laboratory?

Definition: Haemopoiesis (haematopoiesis) is the formation of the cellular elements of blood. It encompasses proliferation, differentiation and functional maturation of stem cells into terminally differentiated blood cells, and includes erythropoiesis (RBC), granulopoiesis (neutrophil, eosinophil, basophil), thrombopoiesis/megakaryopoiesis (platelets) and lymphopoiesis (T and B lymphocytes).

Steps:

  1. Totipotent → pluripotent haemopoietic stem cell (HSC) — self-renewing and very few in number (about 0.01% of marrow cells).
  2. Commitment to a common lymphoid progenitor or a common myeloid progenitor.
  3. Formation of committed colony-forming units (CFU-E, BFU-E, CFU-GM, CFU-G, CFU-M, CFU-Mk) under specific growth factors (EPO, G-CSF, GM-CSF, thrombopoietin, IL-3).
  4. Appearance of morphologically recognisable precursors — proerythroblast, myeloblast, monoblast, megakaryoblast and lymphoblast.
  5. Sequential maturation with functional differentiation, ending in release of mature RBC, granulocytes, monocytes, platelets and lymphocytes into the circulation.

Examination in laboratory: complete blood count (Hb, PCV, RBC/WBC/platelet counts and red cell indices), reticulocyte count (rate of erythropoiesis), peripheral blood film (cell morphology, TC, DC, inclusions, parasites), bone marrow aspiration or trephine biopsy (cellularity, M:E ratio, blast percentage, iron stores), iron studies and ESR.

Ref: V1-ch12; V1-ch13

2 Mention the normal sites of hematopoiesis according to age. How the normal bone marrow cellularity changes according to age?

Sites of haematopoiesis according to age:

Age / periodNormal site of haematopoiesis
0–2 months (first 6 weeks)Yolk sac (mesoblastic erythropoiesis, 3rd–6th week)
2–7 months (6th week to birth)Liver (main site) and spleen (spleen active 6th–24th week)
From 16th week of intrauterine lifeBone marrow, gradually increasing and becoming the main site after birth
At birth (5–9 months of fetal life onward)Red bone marrow in practically all bones
AdultsFlat bones and ends of long bones — vertebrae, ribs, sternum, clavicles, skull, sacrum, pelvis, proximal femur and humerus
