Cellularity with age: In the newborn the whole skeleton is red, almost fully cellular marrow. Fatty (yellow) marrow starts appearing in long bones in childhood, and by age 20 most marrow is yellow — adipose cells with macrophages, mesenchymal and reticular cells — with red marrow retained in the skull diploe, ribs, sternum, vertebrae, cancellous short bones and ends of long bones. In adults haemopoietic cells form 30–70% of marrow cells (the rest is fat), and cellularity falls progressively with age as fat replaces haemopoietic tissue.

Ref: V1-ch12; V1-ch13

3 What is myeloid erythroid ratio? How is it calculated? What are the conditions that can change myeloid erythroid ratio?

Definition: The myeloid:erythroid (M:E) ratio is the ratio of cells of the myeloid (granulocytic) series to cells of the erythroid series in the bone marrow. Normal value: 3–4 : 1 (recorded as 3/4:1 in routine marrow report).

Calculation: On a Romanowsky (Leishman/Giemsa) stained marrow aspirate film, at least 500 nucleated marrow cells are tallied. Numerator = myeloid series from myeloblast to segmented neutrophil (including eosinophil and basophil stages); denominator = erythroid series from proerythroblast to orthochromatic normoblast. M:E = number of myeloid cells ÷ number of erythroid cells. Immature granulocytes and mature cells outside these two series are excluded.

Conditions changing M:E ratio:

ChangeMeaningCommon conditions
M:E increasedMyeloid hyperplasia or erythroid hypoplasiaAcute and chronic bacterial infection, inflammation, myeloproliferative disorders (CML, polycythaemia), acute myeloid leukaemia, marrow infiltration (myelophthisis), corticosteroid / G-CSF therapy, stress
M:E decreasedErythroid hyperplasiaAcute haemorrhage, haemolytic anaemia, iron deficiency anaemia, megaloblastic anaemia, thalassaemia, sideroblastic anaemia, erythroleukaemia
High yield: M:E is a see-saw — M↑ = marrow busy making white cells (infection, inflammation, CML/AML); M↓ = marrow busy making red cells (bleeding, haemolysis, iron/B12/folate deficiency). A ratio above 4:1 or below 2:1 is always reported.

Ref: V1-ch13

4 Describe the structure of normal bone marrow. Give the indications of bone marrow examination.

Structure of normal bone marrow: Bone marrow is a spongy organ filling the centre of bones and is the primary site of haemopoiesis in adults; it replaces about 1% of the body's RBC daily, producing more than 220 million RBC/day. It consists of:

  • Specialised connective tissue (stromal) cells, reticulin fibrils and adventitial reticular cells — the microenvironment that supports stem cell survival and self-renewal.
  • Vascular sinusoids and blood vessels — the exit route for mature blood cells; nerve fibres.
  • Fat cells (adipocytes) — the yellow marrow component.
  • Macrophages and cells of the lymphoid and haematopoietic series (myeloid, erythroid, megakaryocytic).
  • Two types: red marrow (haemopoietic) and yellow marrow (mainly adipose with macrophages, mesenchymal and reticular cells).

Indications of bone marrow examination:

  • Diagnosis — red cell disorders: aplastic, megaloblastic and sideroblastic anaemia, pancytopenia. White cell disorders: leukaemia, subleukaemic acute leukaemia, agranulocytosis, unexplained leucopenia or leucocytosis, leucoerythroblastic picture.
  • Platelet disorders: ITP, unexplained thrombocytopenia or thrombocytosis.
  • Others: multiple myeloma, myelodysplasia, lymphoma, myelofibrosis (trephine), secondary bone carcinoma, iron stores (deficiency/overload), hypersplenism, miliary tuberculosis, pyrexia of unknown origin, kala-azar (LD bodies), storage disorders.
  • Prognosis: aplastic anaemia, agranulocytosis. Effect of treatment: acute leukaemia. Recurrence: follow-up of treated disease.

Ref: V1-ch13

5 Mention the steps of erythropoiesis. What are the factors effecting erythropoiesis?

Steps of erythropoiesis:

  1. Totipotential stem cell → blast-forming unit-erythroid (BFU-E) → colony-forming unit-erythroid (CFU-E).
  2. Proerythroblast (14–20 µm) — first cell recognisable under the microscope; fine chromatin, 1–5 nucleoli, basophilic cytoplasm.
  3. Early normoblast / basophil erythroblast (12–16 µm) — rapid proliferation, more basophilic cytoplasm.
  4. Intermediate normoblast / polychromatic erythroblast (12–14 µm) — haemoglobin synthesis begins here (blue RNA + pink Hb give polychromatic look).
  5. Late normoblast / orthochromatic erythroblast (8–12 µm) — final nucleated stage, pyknotic nucleus, acidophilic cytoplasm full of Hb; nucleus is extruded.
  6. Reticulocyte — first non-nucleated cell, stays in marrow 24 hours then enters blood; matures into RBC in 2–3 days.
  7. Mature RBC (6.7–7.7 µm), biconcave, non-nucleated, life span 120 days.

Factors affecting erythropoiesis:

  • Vitamins: folic acid, B12 (maturation/DNA synthesis), B6 (pyridoxine, haem synthesis), B2 (riboflavin).
  • Minerals: iron (haem), copper (iron incorporation into haem), zinc, cobalt.
  • Other: amino acids (globin), bile salts (absorption of vitamins and trace metals).
  • Growth factors: IL-1, IL-3, IL-6, GM-CSF, G-CSF.
  • Hormones: erythropoietin, thyroxine, anabolic steroids.

Ref: V1-ch12

6 What are the hematinics? How anaemia occurs due to deficiency of hematinics?

Haematinics: These are the substances essential for haematopoiesis and haemoglobin formation — the nutritional requirements of RBC production.

  • Iron — synthesis of haem.
  • Vitamin B12 and folic acid — DNA synthesis and nuclear maturation (maturation factors).
  • Vitamin B6 (pyridoxine) — haem synthesis; vitamin B2 (riboflavin).
  • Amino acids — globin synthesis; protein.
  • Copper — facilitates iron incorporation into haem; cobalt — catalyses Hb synthesis; zinc.
  • Bile salts — help absorption of vitamins and trace metals.

How anaemia occurs: Deficiency of any haematinic impairs a specific step of red-cell production — haem, globin or DNA synthesis — leading to ineffective erythropoiesis, reduced number/shortened survival of red cells and haemoglobin below the cut-off for age and sex, i.e. anaemia. Examples: iron deficiency → microcytic hypochromic iron deficiency anaemia; B12 or folate deficiency → megaloblastic (macrocytic) anaemia; vitamin B6 deficiency → sideroblastic anaemia; copper deficiency → microcytic anaemia; combined protein–iron–vitamin deficiency → nutritional (multinutrient) anaemia.

Ref: V1-ch12

7 Describe the role of erythropoietin in hematopoiesis. Why anaemia occurs in patients having chronic kidney disease?

Role of erythropoietin (EPO): EPO is the principal hormone regulating erythropoiesis — a heavily glycosylated polypeptide of 165 amino acids, molecular weight 34 kDa. Sources: kidney 90% (peritubular interstitial fibroblast-like cells) and liver 10%. Stimuli for release: hypoxia (the basic stimulus), anaemia, alkalosis and androgens. Site of action: bone marrow RBC precursors — it acts as a colony-stimulating factor for development of BFU-E to CFU-E, increasing the number of progenitors committed to erythropoiesis and thereby increasing RBC output. Clinical uses: anaemia of chronic renal disease, myelodysplastic syndrome, anaemia of malignancy/chemotherapy, anaemia of chronic disease, anaemia of prematurity.

Anaemia in chronic kidney disease: Diseased renal parenchyma produces inadequate erythropoietin, so the marrow gets an insufficient stimulus for red-cell production → a normocytic, normochromic, hypoproliferative anaemia. It is compounded by uraemic toxins that shorten red-cell survival and suppress marrow, chronic blood loss with iron and folate loss (especially during dialysis), and secondary hyperparathyroidism; hence iron/folate deficiency may make it hypochromic.

Ref: V1-ch12

8 How iron is absorbed, stored and metabolized in the body? How body iron status is evaluated?

Absorption: Daily intake 10–20 mg, of which only 1–2 mg is absorbed, mainly in the duodenum and proximal jejunum. Haem iron (meat, liver) is absorbed better than non-haem iron; acid pH, vitamin C, sugars and amino acids convert ferric to ferrous (absorbable) iron, while tea, alkali/antacids and pancreatic secretion reduce absorption. Absorption depends on iron stores, rate of erythropoiesis, hypoxia and pregnancy (regulated by hepcidin–ferroportin). Excretion is limited to 0.5–1 mg/day, mostly in stool.

Storage and metabolism: Total body iron 3–5 g — haemoglobin iron 60–70%, storage iron (ferritin + haemosiderin) 1–2 g, myoglobin and enzymes 0.5 g, plasma transport iron 3–4 mg. Ferritin (water-soluble, readily mobilisable) and haemosiderin (insoluble, stable, Perls-positive) are stored in liver, spleen, bone marrow and muscle. Iron travels bound to transferrin (β-globulin made in liver), is used for haem synthesis and is recycled from senescent RBCs by macrophages.

Evaluation of iron status: serum iron, TIBC, transferrin saturation, serum ferritin (best index of stores), free erythrocyte protoporphyrin, and marrow iron by Perl's stain. In deficiency: serum iron and ferritin low, TIBC high, saturation low, marrow iron absent; in overload: ferritin, iron and saturation raised.

Ref: V1-ch14

9 What are the steps of WBC production? What is leukocytosis? Mention the causes of leukocytosis.

Steps of WBC (granulocyte) production:

  1. Totipotent stem cell → myeloid stem cell → colony-forming unit granulocyte-monocyte (CFU-GM) → colony-forming unit granulocyte (CFU-G).
  2. Myeloblast → promyelocyte → myelocyte → metamyelocyte → band (stab) form → segmented polymorphonuclear neutrophil, eosinophil or basophil.
  3. Monocyte line: CFU-M → monoblast → promonocyte → monocyte → macrophage.
  4. Lymphoid line: common lymphoid progenitor → lymphoblast → prolymphocyte → mature B or T lymphocyte.

Leukocytosis: Increase in the total number of WBC above 11,000/mm³ of blood.

Causes of leukocytosis:

  • Physiological: exercise, newborn, pregnancy and parturition, ingestion of food, emotional stress, slight afternoon (diurnal) rise.
  • Pathological: acute pyogenic bacterial infection (most common), non-infectious inflammation (rheumatic fever, collagen disorders, gout, glomerulonephritis), tissue injury (myocardial infarction, burns, surgery), haemorrhage, stress (labour, colic, convulsions), diabetic ketoacidosis, malignant neoplasms, high-dose corticosteroids, chronic myeloid leukaemia and other myeloproliferative disorders, polycythaemia; lymphocyte-predominant rise in viral infection, pertussis, thyrotoxicosis, CLL.

Ref: V1-ch12; V1-ch14

10 What is neutrophilic leukocytosis? What are the common causes of neutrophilic leukocytosis?

Definition: Neutrophilic leukocytosis (neutrophilia) is an increase in the number of circulating neutrophils above the upper limit of the absolute count, i.e. above 7,500/mm³ (it is the commonest component of a total leukocytosis above 11,000/mm³). Often accompanied by a "left shift" — increased band (stab) forms and younger myeloid cells.

Causes:

  • Physiological: exercise, newborn, pregnancy and parturition, heavy meal, emotional stress, mild afternoon rise.
  • Infection: acute pyogenic bacterial infection — the most common cause — abscess, acute appendicitis, tonsillitis, bacterial pneumonia, pyelonephritis, septicaemia (S. aureus, streptococci, S. pneumoniae, E. coli, Proteus, Pseudomonas, N. meningitidis).
  • Non-infectious inflammation: rheumatic fever, collagen disorders, gout, acute glomerulonephritis.
  • Tissue injury/necrosis: myocardial infarction, burns, surgery; haemorrhage; stress (labour, severe colic, convulsions).
  • Metabolic: diabetic ketoacidosis.
  • Others: malignant neoplasm, high-dose corticosteroid therapy, chronic myeloid leukaemia, polycythaemia, chronic granulomatous/ulcerative conditions, smoking.

Ref: V1-ch12

11 Mention the role of eosinophil in host defense. What are the causes of eosinophilia?

Role of eosinophil in host defence: Eosinophils are granulocytes produced in the marrow (CFU-GM → CFU-Eo under IL-5, GM-CSF). Their main defence roles are:

  • Anti-parasitic (especially helminth) defence: they bind via Fc receptors to antibody-coated worms and degranulate, releasing major basic protein, eosinophil peroxidase, cationic protein and collagenase that damage the parasite tegument — antibody-dependent cytotoxicity.
  • Modulation of allergic/inflammatory reactions: their enzymes (histaminase, arylsulphatase) inactivate histamine and slow-reacting substance of anaphylaxis, limiting immediate hypersensitivity.
  • They participate in asthma, atopic disease and mucosal defence, and are part of the phagocyte group of leucocytes.

Causes of eosinophilia (absolute eosinophil count above 500–700/mm³):

  • Parasitic infections — hookworm, ascariasis, tapeworm, hydatid, filariasis, schistosomiasis, trichinosis, scabies.
  • Allergic reactions — asthma, hay fever, urticaria, atopic dermatitis, angioneurotic oedema; drug/foreign-protein allergy.
  • Skin diseases — psoriasis, pemphigus, dermatitis herpetiformis.
  • Loeffler's syndrome and other pulmonary infiltrates; bronchopulmonary aspergillosis.
  • Neoplasms — AML, CML, Hodgkin lymphoma, rarely carcinoma; vasculitis/collagen disorders (polyarteritis nodosa).
  • Recovery from acute infection, post-splenectomy, hypereosinophilic syndrome, familial, graft-versus-host disease.

Ref: V1-ch12

12 How lymphocytes are formed and matured? Mention the types of lymphoid cells with their functions.

Formation and maturation: Lymphopoiesis begins in the pluripotent haemopoietic stem cell, which gives rise to the common lymphoid progenitor → lymphoblast → prolymphocyte → mature lymphocyte. B (bursa-equivalent) lymphocytes mature in the bone marrow (bone marrow lymphopoiesis) and T (thymic) lymphocytes leave the marrow and mature in the thymus. Both then populate lymph nodes, spleen and mucosa-associated lymphoid tissue, where antigenic stimulation makes them enlarge into large lymphocytes and, for B cells, differentiate into plasma cells. Small lymphocytes are 9–12 µm, large lymphocytes 12–16 µm.

Types of lymphoid cells and their functions:

Lymphoid cellMaturation siteFunction
B lymphocyteBone marrowHumoral immunity — differentiates into plasma cells secreting immunoglobulin (IgG, IgM, IgA, IgD, IgE); antigen presentation and memory
T lymphocyte (CD4 helper)ThymusCell-mediated immunity — activates B cells, macrophages and cytotoxic T cells by cytokines
T lymphocyte (CD8 cytotoxic)ThymusKills virus-infected, graft and tumour cells; regulatory/suppressor T cells dampen the response
NK (natural killer) cellBone marrowInnate immunity and antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity without prior sensitisation
Plasma cellB lineage (lymph node, marrow)Antibody factory of the humoral response

Ref: V1-ch12

13 How lymphocytes play role in host defense? Mention the causes of lymphocytosis.

Role in host defence: Lymphocytes are the immunocytes of the blood and provide both arms of specific immunity.

  • Humoral immunity (B cells / plasma cells): secrete antibodies that neutralise toxins and viruses, opsonise bacteria for phagocytosis, activate complement and mediate antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity.
  • Cell-mediated immunity (T cells): CD4 helper cells co-ordinate the immune response by cytokines; CD8 cytotoxic cells directly kill virus-infected, graft and tumour cells; regulatory T cells limit excessive responses; memory cells give a faster secondary response.
  • NK cells provide innate, non-specific killing; lymphocytes also perform immune surveillance and recognise "self" vs "non-self".

Causes of lymphocytosis (absolute lymphocyte count above 4,000/mm³):

  • Bacterial: whooping cough (pertussis — characteristically marked), typhoid and paratyphoid fever, tuberculosis, secondary syphilis.
  • Viral: viral hepatitis, chicken pox, mumps, rubella, infectious mononucleosis (also influenza, adenovirus, CMV).
  • Protozoal: chronic malaria, kala-azar.
  • Malignant: chronic lymphatic leukaemia, lymphoma.
  • Endocrine: thyrotoxicosis; also convalescence from infection and post-infective states.

Ref: V1-ch12

14 What is leukopenia? What may be the conditions causing leukopenia? What is leukopenia with relative lymphocytosis?

Definition: Leukopenia is a reduction in the total WBC count below 4,000/mm³ of blood; it is most often due to neutropenia (neutrophils below 1,500/mm³).

Causes / conditions:

  • Viral infections — viral hepatitis, dengue, influenza, measles, atypical pneumonia, HIV.
  • Bacterial infections — typhoid and paratyphoid fever.
  • Protozoal infections — chronic malaria, kala-azar.
  • Bone marrow failure — aplastic anaemia, megaloblastic anaemia, marrow infiltration by leukaemia/carcinoma, myelofibrosis.
  • Hypersplenism (splenic sequestration), systemic lupus erythematosus.
  • Drugs — analgesics/NSAIDs, antibiotics, antimalarials, cytotoxic agents (also cause agranulocytosis, e.g. aminopyrine, phenothiazines, antithyroid drugs, sulphonamides).
  • Excessive irradiation and marrow transplantation; anaphylaxis; chronic idiopathic neutropenia.

Leukopenia with relative lymphocytosis: The total count is low (below 4,000/mm³) but the percentage of lymphocytes is raised because neutrophils have fallen disproportionately — the absolute lymphocyte count may be normal. It is the classic blood picture of viral infections, typhoid fever, brucellosis and tuberculosis, and is also seen in megaloblastic anaemia and aplastic anaemia ("neutropenia with relative lymphocytosis").

High yield: Low WBC + high lymphocyte percentage does not mean lymphocytosis — check the absolute count. Relative lymphocytosis = neutrophils lost the race (virus, typhoid, TB, megaloblastic/aplastic anaemia).

Ref: V1-ch12; V1-ch14

15 What are the indications of bone marrow examination? What is the common site for bone marrow sampling?

Indications of bone marrow examination:

  • Diagnosis: unexplained anaemia (aplastic, megaloblastic, sideroblastic), pancytopenia, leukaemia and subleukaemic acute leukaemia, agranulocytosis, unexplained leucopenia or leucocytosis, leucoerythroblastic picture, ITP and unexplained thrombocytopenia/thrombocytosis, multiple myeloma, myelodysplasia, lymphoma, myelofibrosis (trephine), secondary bone carcinoma, assessment of iron stores, hypersplenism, miliary tuberculosis, pyrexia of unknown origin, kala-azar (LD bodies), storage disorders.
  • Absolute indications: aplastic anaemia, megaloblastic anaemia, confirmation of any leukaemia, subleukaemic acute leukaemia, ITP, kala-azar, myelofibrosis, multiple myeloma, secondary marrow deposits.
  • Prognosis: aplastic anaemia, agranulocytosis. Effect of treatment: acute leukaemia. Recurrence: during follow-up.

Common sites of sampling:

Age groupSite of marrow puncture
AdultsBody of sternum opposite the 2nd or 3rd intercostal space; iliac crest
Adults and older childrenIliac crest (posterior superior iliac spine — usual site for trephine biopsy)
Children under 1 yearMedial aspect of the proximal part of the tibia

Ref: V1-ch13

16 Describe the normal bone marrow examination findings. What are infections that may involve bone marrow?

Normal marrow findings:

ParameterNormal finding
CellularityHaemopoietic cells 30–70% (rest fat); normocellular
M:E ratio3–4 : 1
ErythropoiesisActive, normoblastic (proerythroblast → normoblast series), occasional polychromatic cells
GranulopoiesisActive and mature — myelocytes, metamyelocytes, band and segmented forms with maturation gradient; blasts 1–2%, eosinophils 1–3%, basophils under 1%
LymphocytesNormal (about 10–20%), mature forms only
Plasma cellsNormal (under 2%), mature
MegakaryopoiesisNormal; mature megakaryocytes with platelet budding
Iron storesPresent (Perl's stain positive in macrophages/erythroblasts)
Abnormal elementsNo excess undifferentiated/disintegrating cells, no metastatic cells, no parasites

Infections involving bone marrow: miliary tuberculosis (tubercle bacilli with granulomas), kala-azar (Leishmania donovani bodies in macrophages of the buffy coat), malaria, brucellosis, histoplasmosis and other disseminated fungal infections, HIV infection, infective endocarditis/septicaemia, and viral infections causing marrow suppression (e.g. parvovirus B19). Special examination of the film looks for these organisms, iron stores and cytogenetics (Philadelphia chromosome in CML).

Ref: V1-ch13

17 What is dry tap? Mention its causes. How bone marrow examined in case of dry tap?

Definition: Dry tap is failure to aspirate any marrow material at all through the needle. (Aspiration of blood without marrow particles is a "blood tap".)

Causes of dry tap:

  • Technical: needle tip not properly sited inside the marrow cavity, or insufficient suction applied.
  • Pathological (increased connective tissue / fibrosis or packing of marrow): myelofibrosis, hairy cell leukaemia, leukaemia and other myeloproliferative disorders, metastatic carcinoma, lymphoma, multiple myeloma, Hodgkin's disease, bone marrow tuberculosis, and densely hypercellular marrow. In metastatic carcinoma, leukaemia, lymphoma or TB the needle enters neoplastic/infiltrated tissue instead of marrow particles.

Examination when dry tap occurs: A trephine (core) biopsy is done — the usual site is the posterior iliac spine; the biopsy needle is advanced with a to-and-fro rotation to obtain a solid core of bone with marrow, which is fixed in formalin, decalcified, sectioned and stained with H&E and reticulin (silver) stain. The core shows overall marrow architecture, cellularity, fibrosis and abnormal infiltrates. Imprint smears from the core (stained with Leishman) supply individual cell detail.

Ref: V1-ch13

18 How bone marrow sample is taken? What are entities included in bone marrow examination?

Taking the sample (aspiration):

  1. Patient lies on the back; skin over sternum/iliac crest is cleaned with 70% alcohol or 0.5% chlorhexidine and anaesthetised locally down to the periosteum.
  2. A strong, wide-bore, short-bevelled needle with stilette and adjustable guard is used; after touching the periosteum the guard is set to allow about 5 mm further penetration, and the needle is pushed with a boring motion into the bone cavity.
  3. The stilette is removed and about 0.2 ml of liquid marrow is aspirated with a syringe.
  4. Films are made on glass slides, fixed in methyl alcohol for 2 minutes and stained by Romanowsky technique (Leishman's stain is most common; Giemsa, Wright, PAS, Perl's for iron).
  5. Examine under oil immersion. Methods: needle aspiration, trephine biopsy, surgical biopsy. Complications: pain, local infection, haematoma, over-penetration.

Entities included in examination:

  • Routine: cellularity (normo/hyper/hypocellular), myeloid:erythroid ratio, erythropoiesis (normoblastic, micro/macro-normoblastic, megaloblastic, active/depressed), granulopoiesis (active, hyperactive, depressed, mature or immature), lymphocytes, plasma cells, megakaryopoiesis, and undifferentiated or disintegrating cells.
  • Special: parasites/organisms (LD bodies, malarial parasite, tubercle bacilli), malignant/secondary cells, iron stores (Perl's stain), chromosomal study (Philadelphia chromosome in CML).

Ref: V1-ch13

19 What is extra medullary hematopoiesis? What may be its causes?

Definition: Extramedullary haemopoiesis (EMH) is haematopoiesis occurring in organs outside the bone marrow. It may be physiological or pathological.

Types, sites and causes:

  • Physiological: during fetal development before mature marrow is formed — yolk sac, fetal liver and spleen.
  • Pathological: a compensatory response to failure or inadequate function of the bone marrow. Causes include haemoglobinopathies (e.g. sickle cell disease), thalassaemia and other chronic haemolytic anaemias, myeloproliferative disorders (chronic myeloid leukaemia, myelofibrosis, polycythaemia vera), and bone marrow infiltration/fibrosis by carcinoma, leukaemia or granuloma.

Sites: spleen, liver and occasionally lymph nodes (rarely other viscera).

Corresponding findings: hepatosplenomegaly; peripheral blood shows tear-drop cells, nucleated red cells and immature myeloid cells — the leucoerythroblastic blood picture; marrow may show a dry tap when infiltration/fibrosis is dense.

Ref: V1-ch12

20 What are the RBC indices? How these are calculated?

Definition: Red cell indices (absolute values) are the measured mean corpuscular volume (MCV), mean corpuscular haemoglobin (MCH) and mean corpuscular haemoglobin concentration (MCHC). They are calculated from three measured data — haemoglobin concentration, packed cell volume (PCV) and total RBC count.

IndexFormulaNormal valueInterpretation when abnormal
MCV — average volume of each RBCMCV = (PCV × 10) ÷ RBC count in millions/mm³76–96 fL (45 × 10 ÷ 5 = 90 fL)Microcyte under 76 fL (iron deficiency, thalassaemia); macrocyte over 96 fL (megaloblastic anaemia, liver disease)
MCH — average Hb per RBCMCH = (Hb in g% × 10) ÷ RBC count in millions/mm³27–32 pg (15 × 10 ÷ 5 = 30 pg)Low in iron deficiency and thalassaemia; raised in macrocytic states
MCHC — average Hb concentration per cell volumeMCHC = (Hb in g% × 100) ÷ PCV31–35 g/dL (33.3%)Hypochromic under 31 g/dL; hyperchromic over 35 g/dL (polycythaemia)

Uses: morphological classification of anaemia — e.g. Hb 15 g/dL, PCV 45%, RBC 5 million/mm³ gives MCV 90 fL, MCH 30 pg and MCHC 33.3% (all normal).

Ref: V1-ch15

21 What is anaemia? How anaemia is classified according to red cell indices?

Definition: Anaemia is a reduction of haemoglobin below the cut-off point for the age and sex of the individual (male under 13 g/dL, female under 11.5 g/dL, newborn under 15 g/dL, 3-month infant under 9.5 g/dL, pregnancy under 10.5 g/dL), so that the oxygen-carrying capacity of blood is inadequate for the tissues. A cut-off of 13 g/dL (male) and 11.5 g/dL (female) is most used.

Classification according to red cell indices:

TypeRed cell indicesCommon examples
Normocytic normochromic anaemiaMCV 76–96 fL, MCH 27–32 pg, MCHC 31–35 g/dL — all normal (RBC count low)Aplastic anaemia, haemolytic anaemia, acute blood loss, renal disease, bone marrow failure/infiltration, anaemia of chronic disease (early)
Microcytic hypochromic anaemiaAll indices decreased — MCV under 76 fL, MCH under 27 pg, MCHC under 31 g/dLIron deficiency anaemia, thalassaemia, sideroblastic anaemia, anaemia of chronic disease, lead poisoning
Macrocytic (normochromic) anaemiaMCV over 96 fL and MCH increased, MCHC normalMegaloblastic anaemia due to B12 or folate deficiency; liver disease, alcoholism, hypothyroidism, drugs (methotrexate, phenytoin)

Ref: V1-ch15

22 Describe the structure of hemoglobin. What are the normal forms of hemoglobin found according to age?

Structure of haemoglobin: Hb is a conjugated protein with 4 sub-units; each sub-unit = one globin chain + one haem group (porphyrin ring with central ferrous iron — the site where oxygen attaches). Adult Hb-A = 2 alpha + 2 beta chains (alpha 141, beta 146 amino acids). Alpha/zeta genes are on chromosome 16; beta, gamma, delta and epsilon genes on chromosome 11.

Normal forms of haemoglobin according to age:

StageHaemoglobinStructureProportion
EmbryonicHb Gower 1, Hb Gower 2, Hb Portlandα2ε2, ζ2ε2, ζ2γ2Early embryo, then disappear
FetalHb Fα2γ270–80% at birth; under 2% by 1 year
At birthHb A, Hb F, trace Hb A2α2β2, α2γ2, α2δ2Hb A ~25%, Hb F ~75%, A2 trace
Adult (pattern set by 6 months)Hb Aα2β297% at 1 year and in adults
Adult minor fractionHb A2α2δ21.5–3.5% (raised in β-thalassaemia trait, B12/folate deficiency)
High yield: chain switching = ζ/ε (embryonic) → γ (fetal) → β (adult), alpha chains throughout. Hb F binds 2,3-DPG poorly, so it has higher O₂ affinity and is alkali resistant; Hb F stays high in thalassaemia, sideroblastic/aplastic/megaloblastic anaemia and leukaemia.

Ref: V1-ch15

23 What is PBF? What are the information included in a PBF report?

Definition: A peripheral blood film (PBF) is a thin stained smear of peripheral blood (made by finger prick, fixed by the methyl alcohol of Leishman's stain and examined under the oil-immersion lens) used for morphological study of the cellular elements of blood. Indications: unexplained cytopenia, severe bacterial sepsis, malignancy with possible marrow involvement, aplastic anaemia, unexplained leukocytosis.

Information included in a PBF report:

ElementInformation reported
RBCSize — normocytes, anisocytosis (microcytosis MCV under 76 fL, macrocytosis over 96 fL); shape — poikilocytosis (spherocyte, target, sickle, tear-drop, schistocyte, pencil cell); staining — normochromic, hypochromic, polychromasia; inclusions — Howell-Jolly bodies, Heinz bodies, Pappenheimer bodies, Cabot ring, basophilic stippling; abnormal cells normally absent — normoblasts, immature cells
WBCMaturity (mature/immature), differential count (neutrophil 40–75%, lymphocyte 20–50%, monocyte 2–10%, eosinophil 1–6%, basophil 0–1%), total count (normal, decreased, increased), morphology of segmented forms, and cells normally absent (blasts, atypical lymphocytes, hypersegmented neutrophils)
PlateletsRelative number — normal (1,50,000–4,00,000/mm³), increased or decreased; morphology — normal or enlarged/giant forms
ParasitesMicrofilariae, trypanosomes in plasma; malarial parasite and Babesia in RBC; Leishmania donovani bodies in monocytes of the buffy coat

Ref: V1-ch14

24 What is total count (TC) of WBC? How it is calculated? Name the common pathological changes in TC.

Definition: The total count (TC) of WBC is the number of circulating leucocytes per mm³ of blood. Normal adult value 4,000–11,000/mm³ (at birth 10,000–25,000; 1 year 6,000–18,000; 4–7 years 5,000–15,000; 8–12 years 4,500–13,500/mm³).

Calculation (haemocytometer method):

  1. Blood is diluted 1:20 with Turk's diluting fluid (3% acetic acid with gentian violet — lyses RBCs and stains leucocyte nuclei).
  2. The diluted blood fills a Neubauer counting chamber; the film is left to settle so cells lie on the grid.
  3. WBCs are counted in the 4 corner large squares under low power; N = total counted.
  4. WBC/mm³ = (N ÷ 4) × dilution (20) × 10 = N × 50.

Common pathological changes in TC:

ChangeDefinitionCommon causes
Increased (leukocytosis)Over 11,000/mm³Bacterial infection, inflammation, tissue injury, haemorrhage, steroids, leukaemia, myeloproliferative disorders, polycythaemia
Decreased (leukopenia)Under 4,000/mm³Viral infection, typhoid, malaria, kala-azar, aplastic and megaloblastic anaemia, hypersplenism, SLE, drugs, irradiation, marrow infiltration

Ref: V1-ch14

25 What is differential count (DC) of WBC? How it is performed? Name the common pathological changes in DC.

Definition: The differential count (DC) of WBC is the percentage — and corresponding absolute number — of each type of leucocyte (neutrophil, lymphocyte, monocyte, eosinophil, basophil) in peripheral blood.

How it is performed:

  1. A PBF is made by the wedge technique and stained with Leishman/Romanowsky stain.
  2. Examined under the oil-immersion lens; cells are identified by size, nuclear shape, chromatin pattern and cytoplasmic granules.
  3. At least 100 consecutive leucocytes (200 if the picture is abnormal) are classified one by one in a zig-zag sweep through the central film; results are recorded as percentages and as absolute counts (absolute value = percentage × TC ÷ 100). Automated counters do the same by flow cytometry.

Normal DC (adults): neutrophil 40–75% (2,000–7,500/mm³), lymphocyte 20–50% (1,500–4,000/mm³), monocyte 2–10% (200–800/mm³), eosinophil 1–6% (40–400/mm³), basophil 0–1% (0–100/mm³).

Common pathological changes in DC:

  • Neutrophilia (infection, inflammation, steroids, CML) with left shift (↑ bands, metamyelocytes); neutropenia (viral infection, typhoid, drugs, aplastic anaemia, agranulocytosis).
  • Lymphocytosis (viral infection, pertussis, TB, CLL) or lymphopenia; relative lymphocytosis in leucopenia.
  • Monocytosis (chronic infection, tuberculosis, monocytic leukaemia); eosinophilia (parasites, allergy); basophilia (CML, allergy).
  • Abnormal cells — blasts (acute leukaemia), atypical lymphocytes (infectious mononucleosis), hypersegmented neutrophils (megaloblastic anaemia).

Ref: V1-ch14

ITEM 23 — Red Cell Disorders
THEORY
1 Define anaemia. Classify anaemia according to WHO. Mention the common clinical features of anaemia.

Definition: Anaemia is a clinical condition in which the blood haemoglobin level is below the lower extreme of the normal range for the age and sex of the individual.

WHO classification: WHO defines anaemia by an Hb cut-off below normal for age, sex and physiological state, and grades its severity as follows:

Group / gradeHaemoglobin
Adult male (15 years and above)Anaemia if Hb < 13.0 g/dL
Non-pregnant female (15 years and above)Anaemia if Hb < 12.0 g/dL
Pregnant womanAnaemia if Hb < 11.0 g/dL
Children 6–59 months / 5–11 years / 12–14 years< 11.0 / < 11.5 / < 12.0 g/dL
Mild (adult non-pregnant woman)Hb 11.0–11.9 g/dL
ModerateHb 8.0–10.9 g/dL
SevereHb < 8.0 g/dL (very severe below 5 g/dL)

Clinical features: depend on the degree and speed of the anaemia and on the causative disorder.

  • Common: pallor (lower palpebral conjunctiva, palm, tongue, nail beds), fatigue, tiredness, effort intolerance, effort dyspnoea and palpitations.
  • Less common: faintness, giddiness, pounding in the ears and effort angina; severe or long-standing anaemia causes a high-output cardiac state and congestive cardiac failure.

Ref: V1-ch16

2 Classify anaemia morphologicallly. How red cells indices can detrermine the morphological types?

Morphological classification: based on RBC size and the amount of haemoglobin inside the cells, i.e. on MCV, MCH and MCHC.

TypeIndicesCommon examples
Microcytic hypochromicMCV < 76 fL, MCH < 27 pg, MCHC < 31 g/dLIron deficiency anaemia (most common), thalassaemia, anaemia of chronic disease, sideroblastic anaemia
Normocytic normochromicMCV 76–96 fL, MCH 27–32 pg, MCHC 31–35 g/dL (all normal, RBC count low)Acute blood loss, haemolytic anaemia, aplastic anaemia, renal failure, bone marrow failure
MacrocyticMCV > 96 fL, MCH increased, MCHC normalMegaloblastic (B12/folate deficiency), normoblastic — liver disease, alcoholism, hypothyroidism

How indices determine the type: all three are calculated from Hb, PCV and RBC count — MCV = PCV × 10 ÷ RBC count (million/mm³) gives cell size; MCH = Hb in g% × 10 ÷ RBC count gives Hb per cell; MCHC = Hb in g% × 100 ÷ PCV gives Hb concentration. All three low = microcytic hypochromic; all within normal limits = normocytic normochromic; raised MCV with raised MCH but normal MCHC = macrocytic.

Ref: V1-ch15

3 Give the etiological classification of anaemia. How iron and protein deficiency causes anaemia?

Aetiological classification (according to cause):

GroupExamples
1. HaemorrhagicAcute (trauma, surgery); chronic (bleeding peptic ulcer, hookworm infestation, piles, menorrhagia)
2. Haemolytic (excess RBC destruction)Intracorpuscular/hereditary (thalassaemia, sickle cell anaemia); extracorpuscular/acquired (HDN, incompatible transfusion)
3. Dyshaemopoietic — deficiency of essential elementsIron deficiency, megaloblastic (B12 and folic acid), nutritional anaemia of PEM, anaemia of scurvy
3. Dyshaemopoietic — bone marrow disturbanceAplastic, sideroblastic, anaemia of renal failure (reduced erythropoietin), anaemia of endocrine disorders

Iron deficiency: increased physiological demand (growth, menstruation, pregnancy, lactation), pathological blood loss or inadequate intake/absorption depletes body iron, so haemoglobin synthesis falls — successive stages of iron depletion, iron-deficient erythropoiesis and finally microcytic hypochromic iron deficiency anaemia.

Protein deficiency: globin chains, transferrin and erythropoietic enzymes are proteins; in protein-energy malnutrition globin synthesis and red-cell production fall, giving a mild-to-moderate (often normocytic, later hypochromic) nutritional anaemia, usually compounded by concurrent iron, folate or vitamin C deficiency.

Ref: V1-ch16

4 Give the lab diagnosis of anaemia / How to approach an anaemia patient in laboratory.

Step 1 — Confirm and grade anaemia: Hb below the cut-off for age and sex (male below 13 g/dL, non-pregnant female below 11.5 g/dL, pregnancy below 10.5 g/dL); look for pallor of conjunctiva, palm, tongue and nail beds. Grade clinically: mild (12–9 g%), moderate (9–6 g%), severe (below 6 g%).

Step 2 — Morphological type: red cell indices (MCV, MCH, MCHC) → microcytic hypochromic, normocytic normochromic or macrocytic.

Step 3 — Peripheral blood film: size, shape (anisocytosis, poikilocytosis, pencil, target, sickle or schistocyte), staining (hypochromia, polychromasia), inclusions, hypersegmented neutrophils, WBC and platelets.

Step 4 — Reticulocyte count: low or inappropriately normal = deficient production; high = blood loss or haemolysis.

Step 5 — Biochemical tests: iron profile (serum iron, ferritin, TIBC, saturation); bilirubin, LDH and haptoglobin; serum B12 and folate; urea and creatinine.

Step 6 — Marrow and cause-directed tests: marrow with iron stain for haemolytic, dyshaemopoietic or infiltrative causes; then stool occult blood and ova, Hb electrophoresis, Coombs test, Schilling test, biopsy.

High yield: approach = confirm (Hb) → classify (indices) → characterise (PBF) → reticulocyte count → iron profile, bilirubin/LDH/haptoglobin, B12/folate → marrow and cause-directed tests.

Ref: V1-ch16

5 Give the lab diagnosis of iron deficiency anaemia.

A) Blood picture: Hb reduced; RBC count reduced to a lesser degree; MCV, MCH and MCHC all reduced (microcytic hypochromic).

B) Peripheral blood film: microcytic hypochromic cells with anisocytosis and poikilocytosis; occasional target cells and pencil-shaped cells; reticulocyte count normal or reduced; WBC and platelet count usually normal (platelets may be moderately increased if the patient is bleeding).

C) Biochemical findings — serum iron profile (confirmatory):

TestIDA finding
Serum ironReduced (below 10 µmol/L; normal 50–175 µmol/L)
Serum ferritinReduced (below 12 µg/L; normal 15–300 µg/L)
TIBCIncreased (80 µmol/L or more; normal 40–80 µmol/L)
Percentage saturationReduced (below 16%; normal about 33%)

D) Bone marrow: not needed for diagnosis; shows erythroid hyperplasia (micro-normoblastic, polychromatic normoblasts predominate) with absent bone marrow iron stores.

E) Special tests: stool R/E and occult blood, proctoscopy and USG to find occult blood loss; hookworm ova.

Ref: V1-ch16

6 Give the lab diagnosis of megaloblastic anaemia.

A) Blood picture: Hb often much reduced; MCV increased (above 96 fL, often 110–140 fL), MCH increased (33–38 pg), MCHC normal.

B) Peripheral blood film: macrocytosis with many oval macrocytes, anisocytosis and poikilocytosis; megaloblasts and Howell-Jolly bodies may be present; reticulocyte count low; neutropenia with relative lymphocytosis and hypersegmented neutrophils; mild symptomless thrombocytopenia.

C) Bone marrow (confirmatory): hypercellular marrow, M/E ratio decreased, erythropoiesis hyperactive and predominantly megaloblastic; granulopoiesis abnormal with giant metamyelocytes giving "giant stab" forms; atypical megakaryocytes; increased number and size of iron granules on marrow iron stain.

D) Biochemical findings: serum iron and ferritin increased, bilirubin slightly increased, plasma LDH invariably increased, serum haptoglobin reduced.

E) Special tests for the cause: serum vitamin B12 assay, serum and red cell folate assay; radioactive B12 absorption (Schilling) test, intrinsic factor and parietal cell antibodies, tests of malabsorption.

Ref: V1-ch16

7 How to diagnose the cause of a microcytic hypochromic anaemia in laboratory?

Step 1 — Confirm the morphology: MCV below 76 fL, MCH below 27 pg, MCHC below 31 g/dL; film shows microcytic hypochromic cells with anisopoikilocytosis, pencil cells and target cells.

Step 2 — Keep the differential diagnosis: iron deficiency anaemia (most common), thalassaemia, anaemia of chronic disease and sideroblastic anaemia.

Step 3 — Iron profile (most discriminating):

TestIDAACDThalassaemia
Serum ironLowLowNormal
Serum ferritinLowNormal/highNormal (high if transfused)
TIBCHighLowNormal
% saturationLowLowNormal

Step 4 — Special tests: Hb electrophoresis (raised Hb-A2 in β-thalassaemia trait, raised Hb-F in thalassaemia major); marrow iron stain (absent stores in IDA, ring sideroblasts in sideroblastic anaemia); stool for ova and occult blood to demonstrate chronic loss.

Ref: V1-ch16

8 How to diagnose the cause of a normocytic normochromic anaemia in laboratory?

Step 1 — Confirm: MCV 76–96 fL, MCH 27–32 pg, MCHC 31–35 g/dL with a low Hb and low RBC count; film shows normocytic normochromic cells with mild anisocytosis and poikilocytosis.

Step 2 — Reticulocyte count separates under-production from loss/destruction:

  • High reticulocyte count (erythroid hyperactivity): acute/post-haemorrhagic anaemia (history of bleeding, polychromasia) or haemolytic anaemia — check bilirubin, LDH, haptoglobin, urinary urobilinogen, osmotic fragility, direct Coombs test and Hb electrophoresis; film shows reticulocytosis, spherocytes, nucleated RBCs.
  • Low/inadequate reticulocyte count (hypoproliferative): aplastic anaemia (pancytopenia with hypocellular marrow), anaemia of chronic disease/renal failure (low serum iron with low TIBC, low erythropoietin), marrow infiltration or failure (trephine biopsy) and early iron deficiency or megaloblastic anaemia before indices change.

Step 3 — Supportive tests: ESR, serum urea/creatinine and erythropoietin level, serum iron profile, marrow examination, and appropriate imaging/biopsy to identify the underlying systemic disease.

Ref: V1-ch16

9 How to diagnose the cause of a macrocytic anaemia in laboratory?

Step 1 — Confirm macrocytosis: MCV above 96 fL with increased MCH and normal MCHC.

Step 2 — Is it megaloblastic or normoblastic? On PBF the macrocytes are oval and macrocytosis is marked in megaloblastic anaemia, with hypersegmented neutrophils, neutropenia and thrombocytopenia; normoblastic (non-megaloblastic) macrocytosis shows round macrocytes without hypersegmentation. Bone marrow shows megaloblastic versus normoblastic erythropoiesis.

Step 3 — If megaloblastic, find the deficiency: serum vitamin B12 assay, serum and red cell folate assay (low B12 with normal/raised serum folate = B12 deficiency; low folate with normal/borderline B12 = folate deficiency).

Step 4 — Find the cause of the deficiency: Schilling (radioactive B12 absorption) test, intrinsic factor and parietal cell antibodies, gastric analysis, tests of malabsorption (jejunal biopsy, barium studies) and dietary/drug history.

Step 5 — If normoblastic: look for haemolysis or recent blood loss, liver disease, alcoholism, hypothyroidism, myelodysplasia, marrow infiltration or drug/cytotoxic therapy with appropriate tests.

Ref: V1-ch16

10 Classify macrocytic anaemia. Describe the PBF and bone marrow findings of megaloblastic anaemia.

Classification of macrocytic anaemia (MCV above 96 fL):

TypeCauses
1. Megaloblastic macrocyticVitamin B12 deficiency (poor intake, gastrectomy/pernicious anaemia, malabsorption, fish tapeworm); folate deficiency (poor diet, alcohol, pregnancy, malabsorption, drugs such as phenytoin and methotrexate); inborn/acquired defects of DNA synthesis
2. Normoblastic (non-megaloblastic) macrocyticHaemolytic and post-haemorrhagic anaemia, alcoholism, liver disease, hypothyroidism, aplastic anaemia, sideroblastic anaemia, myelodysplastic syndromes, marrow infiltration, cytotoxic drugs, scurvy

PBF findings: macrocytosis with many oval macrocytes, anisocytosis and poikilocytosis; megaloblasts and Howell-Jolly bodies may be present; hypersegmented neutrophils (more than 5% with 5 lobes, or a 6-lobed cell); neutropenia with relative lymphocytosis; mild thrombocytopenia; reticulocyte count low.

Bone marrow (confirmatory): hypercellular marrow with decreased M/E ratio; erythropoiesis hyperactive and predominantly megaloblastic; giant metamyelocytes ("giant stab" forms) and atypical megakaryocytes; increased iron granules in erythroid precursors and reticular cells.

Ref: V1-ch16

11 What is megaloblast? Why it is found in vit B12 and folic acid deficiency? What are the causes of non-megaloblastic macrocytic anaemia?

Megaloblast: an abnormal nucleated red cell precursor found in the bone marrow of patients with vitamin B12 or folate deficiency, abnormal both in appearance and in function. They are large cells with abundant cytoplasm and an open, stippled (finely reticular) chromatin network, mitoses are frequent and sometimes abnormal, maturation is arrested and there is dissociation of nuclear and cytoplasmic maturation (the nucleus lags behind the cytoplasm); Howell-Jolly bodies may be present.

Why they appear in B12/folate deficiency: both vitamins are essential for DNA synthesis — folate as a one-carbon donor for thymidylate/purine synthesis and B12 as cofactor for methionine synthase (deficiency traps folate as methylfolate). Impaired DNA synthesis with preserved RNA and protein synthesis slows nuclear maturation while cytoplasm keeps growing, producing these abnormal large precursors, ineffective erythropoiesis, intramedullary destruction and macrocytic megaloblastic anaemia.

Causes of non-megaloblastic (normoblastic) macrocytic anaemia: haemolytic anaemia, post-haemorrhagic anaemia, alcoholism, liver disease, hypothyroidism, aplastic anaemia, sideroblastic anaemia, myelodysplastic syndromes, marrow infiltration or replacement, cytotoxic drug therapy, chronic obstructive airway disease and scurvy.

Ref: V1-ch16

12 What is pernicious anaemia? What morphological type of anaemia occur in pernicious anaemia? How can it be confirmed?

Definition: Pernicious anaemia is a chronic disorder in which the basic lesion is atrophy of the gastric mucosa (an autoimmune attack on the stomach) leading to failure of intrinsic factor secretion, vitamin B12 deficiency and a combination of macrocytic anaemia, glossitis and nervous system involvement. It is a disorder of middle and old age, peaking at about 60 years.

Morphological type: macrocytic (megaloblastic) normochromic anaemia — MCV 110–140 fL, MCH 33–38 pg, MCHC normal, with oval macrocytes, hypersegmented neutrophils, neutropenia and thrombocytopenia on the film; marrow shows megaloblastic erythropoiesis.

Confirmation:

  • Serum vitamin B12 assay — low.
  • Radioactive vitamin B12 absorption (Schilling) test — abnormal; correction with intrinsic factor confirms pernicious anaemia.
  • Anti-intrinsic factor antibody (present in most cases) and antibody against gastric parietal cell in serum/gastric juice (about 85%).
  • Elevated serum gastrin (about 80% of patients); marrow shows megaloblastic erythropoiesis.

Ref: V1-ch16

13 What are the nutritional anaemias? How vit B12 and folic acid deficiency may cause anaemia?

Nutritional anaemias: anaemias caused by a deficiency of substances required for haemopoiesis, due to inadequate intake, poor absorption or increased demand. They include:

  • Iron deficiency anaemia — the commonest nutritional anaemia worldwide and in Bangladesh.
  • Megaloblastic anaemia due to vitamin B12 deficiency, folate deficiency, or both.
  • Nutritional anaemia of protein-energy malnutrition (PEM) and anaemia with vitamin C deficiency (scurvy).

How B12/folate deficiency causes anaemia: folate supplies one-carbon units for thymidylate and purine synthesis, and B12 is the cofactor for methionine synthase (its deficiency causes a "methylfolate trap" so folate cannot be reused). Deficiency therefore impairs DNA synthesis while RNA and protein synthesis continue — nuclear maturation lags behind cytoplasmic maturation, producing megaloblasts, ineffective erythropoiesis with intramedullary haemolysis, and release of large macrocytic red cells: macrocytic megaloblastic anaemia with leucopenia and thrombocytopenia. B12 deficiency additionally causes glossitis, peripheral neuropathy and subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord.

Ref: V1-ch16

14 Why anaemia occurs in chronic disease? Name some diseases commonly causing anaemia of chronic disease.

Definition: the mild-to-moderate anaemia of infectious, inflammatory or neoplastic disease persisting for more than 1–2 months — the 2nd most common anaemia after iron deficiency.

Pathogenesis:

  • Inflammation raises cytokines (mainly IL-6), which increase hepcidin; hepcidin blocks iron export from macrophages and gut cells, so iron is sequestrated in the reticuloendothelial system — serum iron and TIBC fall while ferritin stays normal or raised, and little iron reaches the erythroid marrow (functional iron deficiency).
  • Cytokines blunt the erythropoietin response and directly suppress erythroid proliferation.
  • Mildly shortened red cell survival and reduced erythropoietin production add to the anaemia.
  • The result is a normocytic (rarely microcytic, MCV rarely below 75 fL), normochromic or mildly hypochromic anaemia with Hb rarely below 9 g/dL; marrow storage iron is normal but sideroblast iron is reduced.

Commonly causing diseases: chronic infections — tuberculosis, bacterial endocarditis, osteomyelitis, lung abscess, pneumonia, HIV; chronic inflammatory disorders — rheumatoid arthritis, SLE, vasculitis, polyarteritis nodosa; malignant diseases — carcinoma, Hodgkin disease, leukaemia, multiple myeloma; and miscellaneous — alcoholic liver disease, congestive heart failure, ischaemic heart disease.

Ref: V1-ch16

15 How iron profile may help to differentiate the causes of microcytic hypochromic anaemia? What is sideroblastic anaemia?

Iron profile differentiation of microcytic hypochromic anaemia:

FeatureIron deficiencyAnaemia of chronic diseaseThalassaemiaSideroblastic anaemia
MCV, MCHReducedNormal or mild reductionVery low for degree of anaemiaLow (congenital), normal (acquired)
Serum ironReducedReducedNormalIncreased
Serum ferritinReducedNormal or increasedNormalIncreased
TIBCIncreasedReducedNormalNormal
% saturationReducedReducedNormalNormal or increased
Marrow iron storesAbsentPresentPresentPresent
SideroblastsAbsentAbsentPresentRing forms
Hb electrophoresisNormalNormalRaised Hb-A2 in β-traitNormal

Sideroblastic anaemia: a group of disorders of varying aetiology in which the marrow shows marked dyserythropoiesis with abnormal intramitochondrial iron accumulation in erythroid precursors — ring sideroblasts on Perls stain. Types: hereditary; and acquired (primary or secondary) due to drugs/chemicals (isoniazid, cycloserine, lead, ethanol, chloramphenicol), haematological disorders (myelofibrosis, polycythaemia vera, myeloma), inflammatory disease (RA, SLE), or carcinoma, myxoedema and malabsorption. The film shows a dimorphic picture of hypochromic and normochromic cells.

High yield: iron low + TIBC high + ferritin low = iron deficiency; iron low + TIBC low + ferritin normal/high = chronic disease; both normal with very low MCV = thalassaemia; high iron with ring sideroblasts = sideroblastic.

Ref: V1-ch16

16 What is polycythemia? Mention its types and causes. Give the clinical features of polycythemia.

Definition: Polycythaemia is an increase in the haemoglobin concentration above the upper limit of normal for the patient's age and sex (an increase in total red cell mass), usually accompanied by a raised PCV; the term also covers a rise in other cellular elements of blood.

Types and causes:

TypeMechanism and causes
A. True polycythaemiaIncrease in total red cell number
1. Primary (polycythaemia rubra vera)Idiopathic myeloproliferative neoplasm — decreased/absolute erythropoietin with autonomous marrow erythropoiesis
2. Secondary (erythrocytosis)Increased erythropoietin: hypoxia (high altitude, congenital heart disease, COPD, hypoventilation/obesity, smoking) or inappropriate EPO production (renal cell cancer, renal cysts, hepatocellular carcinoma, cerebellar haemangioblastoma)
3. Benign familial polycythaemiaInherited
B. Relative polycythaemiaReduced plasma volume with normal red cell mass — dehydration, burns, reduced fluid intake, redistribution of body fluids, spurious (stress) polycythaemia

Clinical features: headache and dizziness, cardiovascular symptoms, red face or bloodshot eyes, weakness, lassitude and tiredness, dyspepsia, visual disturbance, pruritus (often after a hot bath), thrombotic and haemorrhagic manifestations, peripheral vascular disease; occasionally splenomegaly, gout and psychiatric manifestations.

Ref: V1-ch16; V1-ch18

17 Mention the lab diagnosis of polycythemia. How is it treated?

A) Blood picture: Hb increased (18–24 g/dL or higher), PCV raised (60–70% or more), ESR decreased (about 1 mm in the 1st hour); MCV, MCH and MCHC may be reduced (microcytosis); RBC count increased, with leukocytosis, thrombocytosis and reticulocytes 1.5% or above on the film.

B) Bone marrow (trephine biopsy may be needed): hypercellular marrow with increased erythropoiesis, granulopoiesis and megakaryopoiesis (M/E normal or reduced).

C) Other tests: raised serum uric acid and LDH; JAK2 mutation present in over 95% of polycythaemia vera cases; low serum erythropoietin helps separate primary from secondary polycythaemia; PaO2 and oxygen saturation are low in hypoxia-driven secondary cases.

Treatment:

  • Venesection (phlebotomy) to keep PCV below 45% is the mainstay, with aspirin to reduce thrombosis.
  • Hydroxyrea (cytoreduction) when indicated; allopurinol for hyperuricaemia/gout.
  • Treat the underlying cause (stop smoking, oxygen for lung disease, remove the EPO-secreting tumour); for relative polycythaemia, correct dehydration.

Ref: V1-ch16; V1-ch18

SCENARIO / PROBLEM-BASED QUESTIONS
18 A 18 yrs old lady presented to you with palor and fatigue. Her Hb%, MCV, MCHC and MCH are all reduced. What may be the diagnosis? What are the PBF findings you may get in her blood?

Diagnosis: Iron deficiency anaemia — a microcytic hypochromic anaemia (all three indices reduced) in a young woman is most commonly due to iron loss from menorrhagia or inadequate dietary iron; thalassaemia trait and anaemia of chronic disease are the main differentials.

Reasoning / expected findings: pallor and fatigue are the cardinal features of anaemia, and low MCV, MCH and MCHC confirm microcytic hypochromic cells. Expected PBF findings:

  • Microcytic hypochromic red cells (increased central pallor) with marked anisocytosis and poikilocytosis.
  • Occasional target cells and pencil-shaped (elongated) cells.
  • Reticulocyte count normal or reduced; WBC and platelet count normal (platelets may be raised if there is bleeding).
  • Iron profile for confirmation: low serum iron, low ferritin, high TIBC and low percentage saturation.

Ref: V1-ch16

19 A 12 yrs girl presented to you with microcytic hypochromic anaemia in PBF. What is/are the diagnosis? How you will confirm the diagnosis?

Diagnosis: microcytic hypochromic anaemia — in a 12-year-old girl the leading causes are iron deficiency anaemia (most common, e.g. poor diet or menorrhagia) and β-thalassaemia trait, with anaemia of chronic disease (e.g. TB) and sideroblastic anaemia as other possibilities.

Reasoning / expected findings — how to confirm:

  • Serum iron profile: low iron, low ferritin, high TIBC and low saturation = iron deficiency; low iron with low TIBC and normal/high ferritin = anaemia of chronic disease.
  • Hb electrophoresis: raised Hb-A2 (above 3.5%) confirms β-thalassaemia trait, raised Hb-F supports thalassaemia major — this distinguishes thalassaemia from iron deficiency.
  • Bone marrow iron stain: absent stores in iron deficiency; ring sideroblasts in sideroblastic anaemia.
  • To find the cause: stool for ova and occult blood, dietary history, serum ferritin after iron therapy response.

Ref: V1-ch16

20 The CBC of an anaemia patient shows Hb conc: 08mg/dl, PCV: 30%, RBC count: 4 million/mm3. Calculate the red cell indices.

Diagnosis: Microcytic hypochromic anaemia (most likely iron deficiency anaemia).

Reasoning / expected findings — calculation of red cell indices (Hb 8 g/dL, PCV 30%, RBC 4 million/mm³):

  • MCV = PCV × 10 ÷ RBC count = 30 × 10 ÷ 4 = 75 fL (normal 76–96 fL) → microcytic.
  • MCH = Hb (g%) × 10 ÷ RBC count = 8 × 10 ÷ 4 = 20 pg (normal 27–32 pg) → hypochromic.
  • MCHC = Hb (g%) × 100 ÷ PCV = 8 × 100 ÷ 30 = 26.7 g/dL (normal 31–35 g/dL) → hypochromic.
  • All three indices are low, so the morphological type is microcytic hypochromic anaemia; confirm with an iron profile (low serum iron and ferritin, high TIBC, low saturation) and examine the film for microcytic hypochromic cells with anisopoikilocytosis and pencil cells.

Ref: V1-ch15

21 A pulmonary TB patient taking anti TB drug for 3 months presented with mild anaemia. The PBF shows mostly normocytic normochomic cells with mild microcytosis. The ESR is 88 in 1st hour. What is the pathogenesis of his anaemia?

Diagnosis: Anaemia of chronic disease (anaemia of chronic infection) due to pulmonary tuberculosis — a mild, non-progressive, normocytic normochromic (sometimes mildly microcytic) anaemia. Chronic anti-TB therapy with isoniazid may additionally cause a sideroblastic/dimorphic picture.

Reasoning / expected findings — pathogenesis: the chronic infection raises cytokines (mainly IL-6), which increase hepcidin; hepcidin traps iron within macrophages, so serum iron and TIBC fall while ferritin stays normal or high and little iron reaches the marrow, limiting haemoglobin synthesis (hence the mild microcytosis). Cytokines also blunt the erythropoietin response, suppress erythropoiesis and mildly shorten red cell survival. The raised ESR (88 mm 1st hour) reflects the fibrinogen and acute-phase proteins of active TB, which itself raises ESR. Expected findings: Hb mildly reduced, iron profile with low serum iron and low TIBC but normal/high ferritin, and marrow with normal storage iron but reduced sideroblast iron.

Ref: V1-ch16

22 A young male having road traffic accident was brought to emergency with massive bleeding. The Hb conc is 7mg/dl. What is the pathogenesis of his anaemia? Write the expected PBF findings and red cell indices in this case.

Diagnosis: Acute post-haemorrhagic (haemorrhagic) anaemia due to traumatic acute blood loss.

Reasoning / expected findings — pathogenesis: sudden loss of a large volume of blood removes red cells along with plasma, so the circulating red cell mass and oxygen-carrying capacity fall (Hb 7 g/dL). Plasma is refilled from the interstitial fluid, initially producing a normocytic normochromic anaemia. Within 3–5 days the marrow responds with marked reticulocytosis and erythroid hyperplasia; if bleeding continues or iron stores are exhausted, a secondary microcytic hypochromic anaemia develops.

  • PBF: normocytic normochromic red cells with anisocytosis and poikilocytosis, polychromasia (reticulocytosis, reticulocytes 5–20%), nucleated red cells in severe haemorrhage; WBC may be increased; platelets may rise.
  • Red cell indices: normal — MCV 76–96 fL, MCH 27–32 pg and MCHC 31–35 g/dL (normocytic normochromic), until iron stores are depleted.

Ref: V1-ch16

23 A 56 yrs old female presented with moderatre anaemia. The PBF shows macrocytic anaemia with many macro-ovalocytes and few hypersegmented neutrophils. What is the diagnosis? How to confirm the diagnosis?

Diagnosis: Megaloblastic anaemia — macro-ovalocytes together with hypersegmented neutrophils are the hallmark of megaloblastic (macrocytic) anaemia, most commonly due to vitamin B12 deficiency (e.g. pernicious anaemia or gastrectomy) or folate deficiency.

Reasoning / expected findings — how to confirm:

  • Blood indices: MCV above 96 fL (often 110–140 fL), MCH raised (33–38 pg), MCHC normal; Hb moderately reduced.
  • PBF: oval macrocytes with anisocytosis and poikilocytosis, hypersegmented neutrophils (more than 5% with 5 lobes or a 6-lobed cell), neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, low reticulocyte count.
  • Bone marrow (confirmatory): hypercellular with megaloblastic erythropoiesis, giant metamyelocytes and increased marrow iron granules.
  • To find the cause: serum vitamin B12 and serum/red cell folate assays, then Schilling test and intrinsic factor/parietal cell antibodies if pernicious anaemia is suspected; LDH raised and haptoglobin reduced.

Ref: V1-ch16

ITEM 24 — Hemolytic Anaemia
THEORY
1 Define and classify hemolytic anaemia. Why reticulocyte count increase in hemolytic anaemia?

Definition: Haemolytic anaemias are anaemias that result from an increase in the rate of red cell destruction — i.e. a reduction of red cell survival below the normal of 120 days. Anaemia becomes apparent only when red cell lifespan falls below about 30 days, because the marrow can partly compensate.

Classification:

TypeDefect / mechanismExamples
Intracorpuscular (intrinsic, mostly hereditary)Defect within the red cell — membrane, haemoglobin or enzymeMembrane: hereditary spherocytosis, hereditary elliptocytosis; Haemoglobin: sickle cell anaemia, thalassaemia, Hb-C/E/D disease, unstable haemoglobin disease; Enzyme: G6PD and pyruvate kinase deficiency; Acquired: paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria
Extracorpuscular (extrinsic, mostly acquired)Abnormal plasma/environment damages a normal red cellImmune: AIHA (warm/cold), haemolytic disease of the newborn, incompatible transfusion, drug-induced; Non-immune: cardiac and microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia, march haemoglobinuria, malaria, clostridia, burns, lead poisoning

Why reticulocytes increase: Red cell destruction produces tissue hypoxia and increased erythropoietin output, causing marked erythroid hyperplasia (stress erythropoiesis) in the marrow. The marrow then releases reticulocytes — and even nucleated red cells — prematurely into the blood to replace the destroyed cells. The count rises to 5–20% (normal 0.2–2%), so reticulocytosis with hyperbilirubinaemia is the main criterion suggesting overt haemolytic anaemia.

High yield: Hallmarks of haemolysis = ↑ unconjugated bilirubin + ↑ LDH + ↑ reticulocytes + ↑ urinary urobilinogen + ↓ serum haptoglobin.

Ref: V1-ch16

2 What are the causes of intravascular hemolysis? How intravascular hemolysis can be diagnosed in laboratory?

Causes of intravascular haemolysis:

  • Immune: mismatched/incompatible blood transfusion; complement-fixing (cold) autoimmune haemolytic anaemia.
  • Acquired red cell membrane defect: paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria.
  • Mechanical: microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia (HUS, TTP, DIC, malignant hypertension, eclampsia, disseminated carcinoma, SLE), prosthetic/cardiac valves, march haemoglobinuria.
  • Toxic, infective and thermal: Clostridium welchii sepsis, severe burns, snake and spider bites, blackwater fever/malaria, oxidant drug crisis in G6PD deficiency.

Laboratory diagnosis:

  • Free haemoglobin in plasma → haemoglobinaemia (Schumm's test positive) and methaemalbuminaemia.
  • Haemoglobinuria (dark urine) and persistent positive urine haemosiderin — the most specific evidence, lasting several days after an episode.
  • Plasma haptoglobin and haemopexin fall (they bind free haemoglobin); plasma LDH rises sharply.
  • Raised unconjugated bilirubin, urinary urobilinogen and reticulocytes.
  • Renal: raised serum urea and creatinine, oliguria; dark urine gives a positive benzidine test.

Ref: V1-ch16

3 Classify the hemolytic anaemia due to intracorpuscular defect. How hereditary spherocytosis can be confirmed in laboratory?

Classification of intracorpuscular (intrinsic) haemolytic anaemia:

GroupDisorders
Membrane defects (congenital)Hereditary spherocytosis, hereditary elliptocytosis, hereditary xerocytosis and hydrocytosis
Haemoglobin defectsHaemoglobinopathies (sickle cell anaemia, Hb-C/E/D disease, unstable haemoglobin disease); thalassaemia (β-thalassaemia major, Hb-H disease); double heterozygous disorders (sickle-cell β-thalassaemia)
Enzyme defectsG6PD deficiency, pyruvate kinase deficiency (non-spherocytic congenital haemolytic anaemia), drug-induced haemolytic anaemia and favism
AcquiredParoxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria

Confirmation of hereditary spherocytosis in laboratory:

  • PBF: spherocytes — small, round, deeply staining cells with loss of the central pallor (cardinal feature); reticulocytes 5–20% and occasional normoblasts; MCV/MCH normal but MCHC often raised.
  • Osmotic fragility test: increased — lysis starts in 0.5% NaCl, 50% lysis (mean corpuscular fragility) at 0.4–0.45% NaCl, complete lysis at 0.3% NaCl; the incubated test is more sensitive.
  • Biochemistry: raised unconjugated bilirubin and LDH, absent haptoglobin.
  • Direct antiglobulin (Coombs') test negative — this excludes acquired spherocytosis due to AIHA, HDN or septicaemia.
  • Family study — autosomal dominant inheritance with affected members; the modern confirmatory test is eosin-5-maleimide (EMA) dye-binding by flow cytometry.

Ref: V1-ch16

4 How anaemia occurs in G6PD deficiency? What are the agents causing hemolysis in G6PD patients?

How anaemia occurs in G6PD deficiency: G6PD is the rate-limiting enzyme of the hexose-monophosphate shunt and the only source of NADPH in the red cell. NADPH keeps glutathione in its reduced (GSH) form, which normally protects haemoglobin and the membrane from oxidation. In deficiency, NADPH and GSH synthesis is impaired, so oxidant stress denatures haemoglobin into Heinz bodies that bind the membrane; the rigid cells lyse within vessels or are trapped and destroyed in the spleen. The result is an acute, episodic haemolytic anaemia with shortened red cell survival, appearing after infection, drugs or fava beans. Other manifestations: neonatal jaundice and rarely a congenital non-spherocytic haemolytic anaemia.

Agents causing haemolysis:

  • Infection and other acute illness (e.g. diabetic ketoacidosis).
  • Antimalarials: primaquine, pamaquine, chloroquine, Fansidar, Maloprim.
  • Sulphonamides and sulphones: co-trimoxazole, sulfanilamide, dapsone, Salazopyrin.
  • Other antibacterial agents: nitrofurans, chloramphenicol.
  • Analgesics (aspirin in large doses); antihelminths (β-naphthol, stibophen).
  • Miscellaneous: vitamin K analogues, naphthalene, probenecid.
  • Fava beans (favism) and possibly other vegetables.

Ref: V1-ch16

5 Mention the causes of reticulocytosis. What is polychromatic cell? What is its significance?

Causes of reticulocytosis: any condition that increases red cell loss or destruction with an intact, responsive marrow. Remember the 3 H — Haemorrhage (acute and chronic), Haemolysis (haemolytic anaemia) and Haematinics therapy (response to iron, B12 or folate). Also after recovery from marrow suppression, at high altitude, with erythropoietin therapy and after splenectomy.

Polychromatic cell: red cells that take a diffuse light-blue tint in a Romanowsky-stained blood film because they still contain residual ribosomal RNA. They are, in fact, reticulocytes — young red cells appearing as large polychromatic cells without central pallor.

Significance: polychromasia indicates accelerated/exaggerated erythropoiesis. It is therefore a feature of haemolysis, acute haemorrhage, and of response to treatment. Its presence in an anaemic film shows the marrow is responding (regenerative anaemia); its absence suggests a hypoproliferative anaemia such as iron deficiency, aplastic anaemia or megaloblastic anaemia.

Ref: V1-ch14, V1-ch12

6 How hemolytic anaemia can be diagnosed in laboratory? What is schistocyte?

Laboratory diagnosis of haemolytic anaemia:

  • Blood: Hb markedly reduced; MCV, MCH and MCHC normal or reduced; ESR high; reticulocytes 5–20%; WBC often raised; platelets normal.
  • PBF: reticulocytosis, spherocytosis, polychromatic macrocytes, nucleated red cells, elliptocytes, fragmentation and Heinz-body formation.
  • Bone marrow: hypercellular, reduced M/E ratio with normoblastic or macronormoblastic erythroid hyperplasia; granulopoiesis and megakaryopoiesis normal.
  • Biochemical: raised unconjugated bilirubin, raised urine and faecal urobilinogen/stercobilinogen, absent haptoglobin, raised LDH, increased osmotic fragility.
  • Other: shortened red cell lifespan by isotope (chromium-51) study; direct Coombs' test to exclude immune causes.

Schistocyte: fragmented red cells that are products of premature red cell destruction. They are smaller than normal, of varying shape, with sharp angles or spurs, staining deeply, and take the forms of crescents, triangles, helmets and microspherocytes. Seen in thalassaemia, microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia (HUS, TTP, DIC), cardiac haemolytic anaemia, disseminated carcinoma and thermal injury in severe burns.

High yield: schistocyte = helmet/crescent/triangle cell → think MAHA — HUS, TTP, DIC, mechanical heart valve, disseminated carcinoma, burns.

Ref: V1-ch16

7 How increased red cell breakdown in hemolytic anaemia can be detected in laboratory?

Detection of increased red cell breakdown (evidence of haemolysis):

  • ↑ Unconjugated (indirect) bilirubin, with jaundice; ↑ LDH.
  • ↓ Plasma haptoglobin and haemopexin — they bind the released haemoglobin.
  • ↑ Urinary urobilinogen and ↑ faecal stercobilinogen.
  • Plasma free haemoglobin, methaemalbuminaemia, haemoglobinuria and urine haemosiderin — only when the breakdown is intravascular.
  • Shortened red cell survival demonstrated by isotope (Cr-51) labelling studies.
FeatureIntravascular haemolysisExtravascular haemolysis
Site of destructionDirectly within the circulationExcessive removal by RE cells, mainly spleen and liver
Haemoglobinaemia / haemoglobinuriaPresentAbsent
MethaemalbuminaemiaPresentAbsent
Urine haemosiderinPresentAbsent
ExamplesMismatched transfusion, PNH, some autoimmune haemolytic anaemiaHaemolytic disease of the newborn, hereditary spherocytosis, warm AIHA

Ref: V1-ch16

8 Why jaundice occur in hemolytic anaemia? Describe the mechanism of physiological jaundice in newborn.

Why jaundice occurs in haemolytic anaemia: Increased red cell destruction releases haemoglobin, whose haem group is converted to biliverdin and then to unconjugated (indirect) bilirubin. The rate of bilirubin production exceeds the liver's conjugating capacity, so unconjugated bilirubin accumulates in the blood. This gives jaundice with a raised indirect fraction, increased urinary and faecal urobilinogen, but no bilirubin in the urine (acholuric jaundice).

Mechanism of physiological jaundice in the newborn: most infants develop visible jaundice from raised unconjugated bilirubin in the first week — it appears at 2–4 days of age and disappears by 1–2 weeks. It is harmless and results from:

  1. Immaturity of the baby's liver, so bilirubin is conjugated and processed slowly.
  2. Greater red cell turnover in newborns — more bilirubin is produced than in adults.
  3. Reabsorption of too much bilirubin from the intestines (enterohepatic circulation) before it is excreted in stool.

Treatment is usually not needed; phototherapy is used if levels are very high, since severe unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia can cause kernicterus, cerebral palsy and deafness.

Ref: V1-ch16, V2-ch19

9 How increased red cell production in hemolytic anaemia can be detected in laboratory?

Evidence of increased red cell production (compensatory erythroid hyperplasia):

  • Reticulocytosis — 5–20%; the corrected reticulocyte count (reticulocyte production index) confirms an appropriate marrow response.
  • Polychromasia and polychromatic macrocytes on the PBF (young RNA-rich cells) — a macrocytosis with normal B12 and folate.
  • Nucleated red cells (normoblasts) circulating in the blood.
  • Bone marrow: hypercellular, normoblastic or macronormoblastic erythroid hyperplasia with reduced M/E ratio; granulopoiesis and megakaryopoiesis normal.
  • Radiological changes in the skull and tubular bones (congenital haemolytic anaemias only) due to marrow expansion.
  • Raised serum erythropoietin; splenomegaly from extramedullary haemopoiesis in long-standing cases.

Ref: V1-ch16

10 What is compensated hemolytic disease?

Definition: Compensated haemolytic disease is a state in which increased red cell destruction is balanced by an equally increased marrow output, so the haemoglobin remains normal or near normal despite a markedly shortened red cell survival. There is no overt anaemia, but the increased red cell turnover is detectable.

Why it happens: the normal marrow can increase erythropoiesis several-fold; while this keeps pace with destruction, Hb is maintained. If the response fails — folate deficiency, infection, marrow disease, or overwhelming destruction — the patient decompensates into overt anaemia.

Recognition in laboratory: raised reticulocyte count (often the only clue), mild unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia, raised LDH and urinary urobilinogen, low haptoglobin, mild jaundice, splenomegaly and a normal or slightly reduced Hb. Examples: mild hereditary spherocytosis, thalassaemia trait, G6PD deficiency between crises. Reticulocytosis with hyperbilirubinaemia are the main criteria suggesting overt haemolytic anaemia.

Ref: V1-ch16

11 What are the forms of damaged red cells found in hemolytic anaemia? Whay neucleated/ immature RBCs are found in hemolytic anaemia.

Forms of damaged red cells (poikilocytes) in haemolytic anaemia:

  • Spherocytes — dense, round cells without central pallor; hereditary spherocytosis, AIHA, HDN, septicaemia.
  • Schistocytes/fragmented cells — crescents, triangles, helmets and microspherocytes; MAHA, cardiac haemolytic anaemia, burns.
  • Elliptocytes/ovalocytes, target cells, sickle cells, pencil and tear-drop cells.
  • Bite/blister cells and Heinz bodies — denatured haemoglobin aggregates, seen in oxidant damage (G6PD deficiency), unstable haemoglobins, thalassaemia and after splenectomy.
  • Fragmentation of red cells and increased osmotic fragility of spherocytes.

Why nucleated/immature RBCs are found: severe or chronic haemolysis causes intense stress erythropoiesis; the hyperplastic marrow releases erythroid precursors (normoblasts) and reticulocytes prematurely, before complete nuclear extrusion and maturation. In long-standing anaemia, extramedullary haemopoiesis in the liver and spleen also discharges immature cells into the blood. Their presence therefore indicates marked marrow stimulation and severe haemolysis (e.g. thalassaemia major or a haemolytic crisis).

Ref: V1-ch16, V1-ch14

12 What is reliculocytosis? Why it occurs in hemolytic anaemia? How reticulocyte count is done?

Reticulocytosis: an increased reticulocyte count in the peripheral blood. A reticulocyte is the last stage of the erythroid series before the mature red cell — non-nucleated but retaining ribosomal RNA that precipitates as a reticular network with supravital stains. Normal count: 0.2–2% of red cells.

Why it occurs in haemolytic anaemia: shortened red cell survival stimulates erythropoietin release and marked erythroid hyperplasia, so reticulocytes are released prematurely and in large numbers (5–20%) to replace the destroyed cells. It is one of the hallmarks of haemolysis and shows the marrow is intact and responding.

How reticulocyte count is done: supravital staining with new methylene blue or brilliant cresyl blue is used, because the dye precipitates the residual RNA as blue reticulum. Blood and stain are incubated, a film is made, and 1000 red cells are counted under oil immersion, the result being expressed as a percentage (rapid method of Schilling; automated/flow cytometric methods are also used). The count is then corrected for the degree of anaemia to give the reticulocyte production index.

Ref: V1-ch16, V1-ch12

13 List the causes of hemolysis due to extracorpuscular defect. Name the non-immune hemolytic anaemias.

Extracorpuscular defect: the red cell itself is normal but is damaged by an abnormal plasma or environment.

FeatureIntracorpuscularExtracorpuscular
Site of defectWithin the red cellIn the plasma/surroundings of the cell
Usual onsetHereditary/congenital (except acquired PNH)Acquired
MechanismMembrane, haemoglobin or enzyme defectImmune, mechanical, infective, chemical or thermal injury
ExamplesSpherocytosis, elliptocytosis, sickle cell disease, thalassaemia, G6PD/PK deficiency, PNHAIHA, HDN, incompatible transfusion, drugs, cardiac and microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia, malaria, burns

Non-immune haemolytic anaemias:

  • Mechanical: cardiac/prosthetic valve haemolytic anaemia, microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia (HUS, TTP, DIC, malignant hypertension, eclampsia, disseminated carcinoma, SLE, haemangiomas), march haemoglobinuria.
  • Chemicals and drugs: lead poisoning, sulphonamides, snake and spider venoms.
  • Infection: malaria, Clostridium welchii.
  • Thermal: severe burns.
  • Acquired membrane defect: paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria.

Ref: V1-ch16

14 What are the alloimmune hemolytic anaemias? Discuss on hemolytic disease of newborn.

Alloimmune haemolytic anaemias: haemolysis caused by antibodies produced by one individual that react against red cell antigens of another individual (isoantibodies), in contrast to autoantibodies reacting with the subject's own cells. The main forms are haemolytic transfusion reactions, haemolytic disease of the newborn and isoimmunisation after stem cell grafting.

Haemolytic disease of the newborn (HDN / erythroblastosis fetalis):

  1. An Rh-negative mother carries an Rh-positive fetus; fetal red cells enter the maternal circulation at delivery or abortion.
  2. The mother is sensitised and forms anti-D (IgG) antibodies.
  3. In the next Rh-positive pregnancy these IgG antibodies cross the placenta and coat the fetal red cells.
  4. Coated red cells are destroyed mainly by macrophages of the fetal spleen and liver (extravascular), with some complement-mediated intravascular lysis.
  5. Consequences: fetal anaemia, jaundice, hepatosplenomegaly, hydrops fetalis (oedema, heart failure) and kernicterus; severity increases with each successive pregnancy — the first baby is usually mild. ABO incompatibility causes a milder form, which may affect the firstborn.

Prevention and treatment: anti-D immunoglobulin to the mother within 72 hours of delivery, avoidance of Rh-positive blood for Rh-negative recipients, phototherapy and exchange transfusion with compatible Rh-negative blood for the affected neonate.

Ref: V1-ch16, V1-ch21

15 How hemolysis occurs in hemolytic transfusion reaction? What is drug induced hemolytic anaemia?

Haemolysis in haemolytic transfusion reaction: recipient antibodies react with red cell antigens of the donor (almost always destroying donor cells). Preformed ABO isohaemagglutinins (IgM) fix complement and lyse the cells within the circulation — an immediate intravascular reaction occurring within an hour, with heat in the vein, flushing, chest tightness, lumbar pain, hypotension, rigors and fever, followed by haemoglobinaemia (Schumm's test positive), haemoglobinuria, jaundice, oliguric renal failure and DIC. Antibodies formed after earlier sensitisation by pregnancy or transfusion (usually IgG) cause a delayed extravascular reaction 5–14 days later, presenting with fever, jaundice, anaemia, raised bilirubin and a positive direct antiglobulin test.

Drug-induced haemolytic anaemia: haemolysis caused by a drug, by one of these mechanisms:

  • Drug adsorption (hapten) type: the drug binds the red cell membrane and antibody reacts with the drug-membrane complex (e.g. penicillin).
  • Immune complex type: drug-antibody complexes adsorb complement and cause intravascular haemolysis (e.g. para-aminosalicylic acid, quinidine).
  • Autoantibody induction: warm-type AIHA with a positive DAT (e.g. methyldopa).
  • Oxidant damage type: in G6PD deficiency (primaquine, sulphonamides, dapsone, nitrofurans, chloroquine, co-trimoxazole, chloramphenicol).
