High yield: three immune drug mechanisms — membrane complex (penicillin), immune complex (quinidine), autoantibody (methyldopa); plus oxidant lysis in G6PD deficiency.

Ref: V1-ch16, V1-ch21

16 What is immune hemolytic anaemia (IHA)? Classify immune HA.

Definition: Immune haemolytic anaemia is a group of haemolytic anaemias that result from the development of antibodies directed against antigens on the surface of the patient's own red cells (autoimmune haemolytic anaemias), or from an antibody produced by one individual reacting with the red cells of another (alloimmune haemolytic anaemias).

Classification:

GroupSubtypeExamples / causes
AutoimmuneWarm antibody typeIdiopathic; secondary to CLL, malignant lymphoma, SLE, rheumatoid arthritis, chronic active hepatitis; drugs (e.g. methyldopa)
Cold antibody typeIdiopathic; secondary to infections (Mycoplasma pneumonia, infectious mononucleosis), malignant lymphomas, paroxysmal cold haemoglobinuria
AlloimmuneAntibody to another person's red cell antigensHaemolytic transfusion reactions, haemolytic disease of the newborn, post stem cell grafts
Drug-inducedDrug-red cell membrane complex; immune complexPenicillin; para-aminosalicylic acid, quinidine

Ref: V1-ch16

17 What are the types of autoimmune hemolytic anaemia (AIHA). Describe the pathogenesis of AIHA.

Types of AIHA:

  1. Warm antibody AIHA — the commonest form; IgG reacting at 37°C; idiopathic or secondary (CLL, lymphoma, SLE, rheumatoid arthritis, chronic active hepatitis, drugs such as methyldopa).
  2. Cold antibody AIHA — IgM reacting at low temperature; cold agglutinin disease (idiopathic, Mycoplasma pneumonia, infectious mononucleosis, lymphoma) and paroxysmal cold haemoglobinuria.
  3. Mixed warm/cold type and drug-associated AIHA.

Pathogenesis: loss of immune tolerance to self red cell antigens leads to autoantibody production. The antibody coats (sensitises) circulating red cells. In warm AIHA the coated cells pass through the sple, where macrophages strip off part of the membrane; the cells become spherocytes (loss of central pallor, increased osmotic fragility), are less deformable and are trapped and destroyed in the sple — predominantly extravascular haemolysis. In cold AIHA, IgM fixes complement at cooled sites; lysis may occur within the vessels (intravascular) and complement-coated cells are removed in the liver. Red cell survival shortens → anaemia, unconjugated jaundice, raised LDH and bilirubin, reticulocytosis and marrow erythroid hyperplasia.

FeatureWarm antibodyCold antibody
Antibody and thermal amplitudeIgG, reacting at 37°CIgM, reacting at about 4°C (below 30–37°C)
Site of destructionRE system, predominantly spleen (extravascular)Mainly peripheral circulation where blood is cooled; some in liver
SpherocytosisProminentLess marked
TreatmentCorticosteroids are the usual first lineSteroids are not helpful; keep warm and treat the underlying cause

Ref: V1-ch16

18 What are the types of coomb’s test. How coomb’s test can help in diagnosis of AIHA?

Principle: antihuman globulin (Coombs' reagent) is produced in animals against human globulin, purified complement or specific immunoglobulin (IgG, IgA, IgM). When added to red cells coated with immunoglobulin or complement components, agglutination indicates a positive test.

FeatureDirect Coombs' test (DAT)Indirect Coombs' test (IAT)
DetectsAntibody or complement already bound to the patient's red cells in vivoFree (unbound) antibody in the patient's plasma or serum
MethodWashed red cells of the patient are mixed with AHG → agglutinationPatient serum is incubated with normal donor red cells, washed, then AHG added → agglutination
Positive inAIHA, haemolytic disease of the newborn, drug-induced haemolytic anaemia, haemolytic transfusion reactionAntibody screening before transfusion, cross-matching, antenatal screening for antibodies causing HDN

Help in diagnosis of AIHA: a positive DAT is the hallmark of AIHA — it demonstrates that the patient's red cells are coated in vivo, with IgG in warm AIHA and complement in cold AIHA. About 95% of AIHA cases are DAT positive, so a positive test in a patient with haemolysis confirms the diagnosis, while a negative test makes it unlikely (occasional seronegative AIHA exists). The IAT identifies and types the free antibody in the serum. In haemolytic disease of the newborn the DAT is positive in the baby and the IAT positive in the mother.

Ref: V1-ch16

PROBLEM-BASED QUESTIONS
19 A 5 yrs boy presented with persistent anaemia and jaundice. His PBF shows many microspherocytes, polychromatic cells and few schistocytes. What is the provisional diagnosis? How will you confirm the diagnosis?

Provisional diagnosis: Hereditary spherocytosis — a congenital (intracorpuscular membrane-defect) haemolytic anaemia. Persistent anaemia and jaundice since early childhood with numerous microspherocytes (small, round, dense cells lacking central pallor) and polychromatic cells (reticulocytosis) on PBF is characteristic; the few schistocytes reflect occasional mechanical fragmentation.

Confirmation:

  • Osmotic fragility test — increased: lysis begins at 0.5% NaCl, 50% lysis (MCF) at 0.4–0.45% NaCl and complete lysis at 0.3% NaCl; the incubated test is more sensitive.
  • PBF: spherocytes with loss of central pallor, reticulocytes 5–20%, MCHC normal or raised.
  • Biochemistry: raised unconjugated bilirubin and LDH, absent haptoglobin.
  • Direct Coombs' test negative — excludes acquired spherocytosis (AIHA, HDN, septicaemia).
  • Eosin-5-maleimide (EMA) binding test by flow cytometry — the confirmatory test; family study shows autosomal dominant inheritance with other affected members.

Ref: V1-ch16

20 A young boy suddenly developed hemolytic anaemia when he was given chloroquine for malaria. His family gives history of hemolytic reaction when he was given co-trimoxazol and chloramphenicol. What is your provisional diagnosis? How will you manage the case?

Provisional diagnosis: G6PD deficiency with acute oxidant drug-induced haemolytic anaemia. Chloroquine precipitated the crisis in this boy, and the familial reaction to co-trimoxazole and chloramphenicol — both oxidant drugs — points to X-linked G6PD deficiency; expect sudden jaundice, dark urine, anaemia, reticulocytosis, spherocytes/fragmented cells and Heinz bodies after the drug or infection.

Management:

  1. Stop the offending drug immediately and never re-challenge; document all drugs to avoid.
  2. Supportive care: hydration, rest, treat the underlying infection; monitor Hb, reticulocytes, bilirubin, creatinine and urine output.
  3. Blood transfusion if Hb falls severely or the child is symptomatic; exchange transfusion if neonatal jaundice is marked.
  4. Avoid all oxidant agents — antimalarials (primaquine, chloroquine, Fansidar), sulphonamides/co-trimoxazole, chloramphenicol, nitrofurans, dapsone, high-dose aspirin, vitamin K analogues, naphthalene — and avoid fava beans.
  5. Screen family members and neonates; give folic acid if deficient; give renal support if there is haemoglobinuria or oliguria.

Ref: V1-ch16

21 A new mother presented with anemia and jaundice in her baby after child birth. Baby’s PBF shows features of hemolysis. The direct Coombs test is positive in baby and indirect coombs test is positive in mother. What is the diagnosis? Discuss the pathogenesis.

Provisional diagnosis: Haemolytic disease of the newborn (erythroblastosis fetalis) — alloimmune haemolytic anaemia due to Rh incompatibility (most often anti-D) or, less severely, ABO incompatibility. A positive direct Coombs' test in the baby shows that his red cells are coated with antibody in vivo, while the positive indirect Coombs' test in the mother shows the corresponding free antibody in her serum; anaemia and jaundice after birth with haemolytic features (reticulocytosis, polychromasia, spherocytes) complete the picture.

Pathogenesis:

  1. The mother is Rh-negative (or group O) and the baby Rh-positive (or group A/B); during a previous pregnancy or delivery fetal red cells entered her circulation and sensitised her, so she produced anti-D IgG.
  2. In this Rh-positive pregnancy the maternal IgG antibodies cross the placenta into the fetal circulation.
  3. The antibodies coat the fetal red cells — hence the positive direct antiglobulin (Coombs') test in the baby; the mother's serum contains free antibody, hence the positive indirect test.
  4. Coated red cells are destroyed mainly by macrophages of the fetal spleen and liver (extravascular haemolysis), with some complement-mediated intravascular destruction.
  5. Consequences: fetal anaemia, unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia and jaundice, marrow erythroid hyperplasia with polychromasia and nucleated red cells in the film, hepatosplenomegaly, hydrops fetalis and, if severe, kernicterus or death in utero. Severity rises with each subsequent pregnancy.
  6. Confirmation and management: ABO/Rh typing of mother and baby, antibody titre and bilirubin trend, phototherapy and exchange transfusion with compatible blood; anti-D immunoglobulin to the mother within 72 hours of delivery prevents sensitisation in the next pregnancy.

Ref: V1-ch16, V1-ch21

ITEM 25 — Thalassaemia & Aplastic Anaemia
APLASTIC ANAEMIA AND PANCYTOPENIA
1 What is pancytopenia? What is the clinical presentations of pancytopenia? How it is diagnosed in laboratotry?

Definition: Pancytopenia is the state in which all three formed elements of blood are reduced together — anaemia, leukopenia and thrombocytopenia. It is a picture, not a disease, so the underlying cause must be found.

Clinical presentation: features reflect deficiency of each cell line:

  • Anaemia (↓ RBC): pallor, weakness, easy fatiguability, lassitude, dyspnoea on exertion.
  • Leukopenia / neutropenia (↓ WBC): malaise, fever with chills, sore throat, oral ulceration, recurrent chest and skin infections.
  • Thrombocytopenia (↓ platelets): petechiae, ecchymoses, epistaxis, menorrhagia, bleeding from gums and alimentary tract.
  • Features of the cause: bone pain (marrow infiltration), fever and night sweats (infection, lymphoma, TB), jaundice with splenomegaly (hypersplenism).

Laboratory diagnosis:

  1. History: age, diet, drug/chemical/radiation exposure, bone pain, fever, weight loss.
  2. Examination: lymph nodes, spleen, liver, bone tenderness, gum hypertrophy, signs of portal hypertension.
  3. Essential tests: peripheral blood examination with CBC, PBF and ESR (note anisocytosis, poikilocytosis, immature red/white precursors, rouleaux) plus bone marrow aspiration and trephine biopsy.
  4. Further tests: bone and chest X-ray, serum protein electrophoresis, Bence-Jones protein, LE antibody, liver biopsy where indicated.

Ref: V1-ch16

2 Mention the causes of pancytopenia. How hyperspleenism may cause pancytopenia?

Causes of pancytopenia:

  • Decreased marrow production: aplastic (hypoplastic) anaemia; megaloblastic anaemia; subleukaemic acute leukaemia; myelodysplastic syndrome; marrow infiltration by lymphoma, metastatic carcinoma, multiple myeloma or myelofibrosis.
  • Iatrogenic: cytotoxic drugs (chemotherapy) and radiotherapy.
  • Increased peripheral destruction: hypersplenism with splenomegaly; paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria.
  • Others: overwhelming infection, systemic lupus erythematosus. (Myeloproliferative disorders are not quoted as a cause of pancytopenia.)

How hypersplenism causes pancytopenia: the enlarged, over-active spleen pools blood in its red pulp and sequestrates and destroys all circulating cell lines prematurely, so the life span of red cells, neutrophils and platelets is markedly shortened. Production in the marrow is normal or even hyperplastic, but peripheral removal outstrips production, giving anaemia, leukopenia and thrombocytopenia — the classic triad of hypersplenism with splenomegaly, cytopenias (with hyperplasia of the marrow precursors of the deficient lines) and correction of the cytopenias by splenectomy.

Ref: V1-ch16

3 What is aplastic anaemia? List the causes of aplastic anaemia. What is Fanconi anaemia?

Definition: Aplastic (hypoplastic) anaemia is pancytopenia resulting from aplasia or hypoplasia of the bone marrow — peripheral pancytopenia with a hypoplastic marrow in which normal haemopoietic tissue is replaced by fat. It is usually hypoplasia rather than complete aplasia.

Causes (classification by presumptive aetiology):

  • Primary — congenital: e.g. Fanconi anaemia.
  • Primary — idiopathic acquired: the most common type, accounting for at least two-thirds of acquired cases.
  • Secondary: ionizing radiation (radiotherapy, isotopes); chemicals (benzene, organophosphates, organic solvents, DDT and other pesticides); drugs (chloramphenicol, sulphonamides, cytotoxic/alkylating agents, NSAIDs, anticonvulsants, antithyroid drugs, gold salts, chlorpromazine); viral infections (parvovirus B19, hepatitis C, EBV/mononucleosis); autoimmune cytotoxic T-cell dysfunction; paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria; pregnancy.

Fanconi anaemia: a rare autosomal recessive syndrome of chromosomal instability (defective DNA repair) presenting in childhood with progressive marrow failure (aplastic anaemia, pancytopenia) plus congenital anomalies — short stature, microcephaly, radial-ray defects (thumb anomalies, hypoplastic radius), patchy skin pigmentation and renal/genitourinary malformations — and a raised risk of leukaemia and myelodysplastic syndrome.

Ref: V1-ch16

4 Describe the clinical features and lab diagnosis of aplastic anaemia.

Clinical features:

  • Anaemia (reduced RBC): pallor, weakness, easy fatiguability and dyspnoea on exertion are most prominent.
  • Neutropenia (reduced WBC): malaise, sore throat, oral and pharyngeal ulceration, fever with chills, recurrent chest and skin infections.
  • Thrombocytopenia (reduced platelets): ecchymoses, petechiae, epistaxis, menorrhagia, bleeding from gums and alimentary tract.
  • Cardinal signs: NO lymphadenopathy, NO hepatosplenomegaly, NO bony tenderness — if any is present, suspect another cause of pancytopenia.

Laboratory diagnosis:

  • Blood picture: Hb reduced (may be below 7 g/dl); MCV, MCH and MCHC normal — normocytic, normochromic with anisocytosis and poikilocytosis; ESR high (50–100 mm first hour); reticulocytes sub-normal; neutropenia with relative lymphocytosis; thrombocytopenia.
  • Bone marrow (confirmatory — trephine biopsy usually required): hypocellular (hypoplasia); M/E ratio normal; erythropoiesis reduced and normoblastic; granulopoiesis reduced; megakaryopoiesis severely reduced or absent; plasma cells and reticular cells increased; stored iron normal or increased.
FeatureAplastic anaemiaThalassaemia major
Red cellsNormocytic, normochromicMicrocytic, hypochromic with target cells
Bone marrowHypocellular, fat replacedHypercellular, erythroid hyperplasia
SpleenNo organomegalyMarked splenomegaly
High yield: aplastic anaemia = pancytopenia + hypoplastic marrow + absent organomegaly/lymphadenopathy/bony tenderness. Causes of death: pneumonia, septicaemia and cerebral haemorrhage.

Ref: V1-ch16

THALASSAEMIA
5 What are normal globin chains found in hemoglobin? What are the globin abnormalities? What is the type of globin abnormality occurring in thalassaemia?

Normal globin chains: haemoglobin is a tetramer of four globin (polypeptide) chains, each carrying a haem group whose ferrous iron binds oxygen. Normal human haemoglobins are:

HaemoglobinStructureOccurrence
Hb-Aα2β2~97% of adult haemoglobin
Hb-A2α2δ2~2–3% of adult haemoglobin
Hb-F (fetal)α2γ2dominant in fetal life; replaced by Hb-A by about 6 months
Embryonic (Gower-1, Gower-2, Portland)ζ2ε2, α2ε2, ζ2γ2early embryonic life

α chains contain 141 amino acids (genes on chromosome 16); β, γ and δ chains contain 146 amino acids (genes on chromosome 11).

Globin abnormalities: two broad groups — quantitative defects (reduced amount of an otherwise normal chain, i.e. thalassaemia) and qualitative defects (structurally abnormal chains, i.e. haemoglobinopathies such as Hb-S with β6 Glu replaced by Val, Hb-C β6 Glu by Lys, Hb-E β26 Glu by Lys).

Type of abnormality in thalassaemia: quantitative — the globin chain is structurally normal but its rate of synthesis is reduced or absent, giving unbalanced chain production, unlike the qualitative defect of haemoglobinopathies.

Ref: V1-ch12

6 Describe the pathogenesis of thalassemia. What are the pathological types of thalassemia?

Pathogenesis: thalassaemia is an autosomal recessive inherited disorder with partial or complete failure to synthesize one type of globin chain (heterozygotes are usually healthy). Inadequate β-chain synthesis in β-thalassaemia lowers Hb-A (α2β2) and causes microcytic hypochromic anaemia; the unmatched excess chains (γ in the fetus, β in the adult) precipitate as inclusions that damage the red-cell membrane. Result — ineffective erythropoiesis with intramedullary destruction of red-cell precursors (main cause of anaemia), shortened red-cell life span, splenic sequestration, haemodilution and folate deficiency → anaemia, jaundice and gallstones. Chronic hypoxia drives marrow expansion (thalassaemic facies, hair-on-end skull) and compensatory extramedullary haemopoiesis (hepatosplenomegaly); repeated transfusion later causes iron overload.

Pathological types (by degree of chain suppression):

  • β-thalassaemia: β+ — incomplete suppression of β-chain synthesis; β0 — complete absence of β-chain synthesis.
  • α-thalassaemia: graded by the number of the four α-genes deleted (1 to 4) — silent carrier, α-thalassaemia trait, Hb-H disease, Hb-Bart's hydrops fetalis.

Ref: V1-ch16

7 What are the clinical types of thalassemia? What are the Hb H disease, Hb barts and Hydrops fetalis?

Clinical types: β-thalassaemia major (homozygous, Cooley's anaemia), β-thalassaemia minor (heterozygous, mild or asymptomatic) and thalassaemia intermedia (severity between the two). α-thalassaemia: silent carrier (1 gene deleted), α-thalassaemia trait (2 genes), Hb-H disease (3 genes) and Hb-Bart's hydrops fetalis (4 genes).

FeatureHb-H diseaseHb-Bart's (trait)Hb-Bart's hydrops fetalis
α-genes deleted3 of 42 of 4All 4
Abnormal tetramerHb-H (β4)Hb-Bart's (γ4) in the neonateHb-Bart's (γ4) 80–90%, with Hb-H and Hb-Portland
Effect on HbModerate haemolytic microcytic anaemiaMild microcytosis, asymptomatic carrierNo Hb-A, Hb-A2 or Hb-F; Hb about 6 g/dl
Clinical courseChronic anaemia with splenomegalyNormal haemoglobinStillborn or dies within hours (jaundiced, oedematous, hepatosplenomegaly, ascites)

Explanations: Hb-H disease — excess β-chains form Hb-H (β4) that precipitates and damages red cells, causing chronic haemolytic anaemia. Hb-Bart's — excess γ-chains form non-functional γ4 tetramers. Hydrops fetalis — the most severe α-thalassaemia with near-total suppression of α-chain synthesis; infants are born dead or die shortly after birth.

High yield: α-gene deletions — 1 = silent carrier, 2 = α-thal trait, 3 = Hb-H disease, 4 = Hb-Bart's hydrops fetalis (fatal).

Ref: V1-ch16

8 What are the thalassemia intermedia and thalassaemia minor? How thalassaemia can be diagnosed in laboratory?

Thalassaemia intermedia: severity lies between major and minor — moderate anaemia (roughly 7–9 g/dl), mild jaundice, splenomegaly and some bone changes, but no or only occasional transfusion is needed.

Thalassaemia minor (trait): the heterozygous state in which suppression of β-chain synthesis is much less severe; usually asymptomatic or mildly anaemic, with microcytic hypochromic indices and a raised Hb-A2 (>3.5%) on electrophoresis.

FeatureMinor (trait)IntermediaMajor
GenotypeHeterozygousHomozygous/compound heterozygous, milderHomozygous (β0/β0 or β+/β+)
HaemoglobinMildly reduced7–9 g/dlSeverely reduced (3–9 g/dl)
TransfusionNot requiredOccasionalRegular and lifelong
Usual featuresAsymptomatic microcytosisMild jaundice, splenomegaly, bone changesSevere anaemia, facies, hepatosplenomegaly, iron overload

Laboratory diagnosis: CBC shows low Hb with reduced MCV, MCH and MCHC; PBF shows microcytic hypochromic cells with marked anisocytosis and poikilocytosis, target cells, basophilic stippling, nucleated red cells and reticulocytes 10% or more; serum bilirubin is slightly raised and the iron profile normal (raised after repeated transfusion); haemoglobin electrophoresis is confirmatory — absence or near absence of Hb-A with Hb-F 10–98% in major, raised Hb-A2 in trait; supportive: marrow erythroid hyperplasia, hair-on-end skull X-ray.

Ref: V1-ch16

9 Give the bone marrow findings of a β-thalassaemia major patient. Why extramedullary hematopoiesis occurs in thalassaemia patients?

Bone marrow findings of β-thalassaemia major (marrow examination is not usually needed to diagnose thalassaemia):

  • Cellularity: increased — hypercellular marrow.
  • M/E ratio: decreased — the normal 3–4:1 myeloid:erythroid ratio is reversed by erythroid hyperplasia.
  • Erythropoiesis: normoblastic hyperplasia with small micro-normoblasts; much of it ineffective, with intramedullary destruction of precursors.
  • Granulopoiesis: normal. Megakaryopoiesis: normal.

Why extramedullary haemopoiesis occurs: the marrow cannot meet the enormous red-cell demand produced by severe anaemia and ineffective erythropoiesis; driven by erythropoietin, haemopoiesis reappears in the sites used during fetal life — mainly spleen and liver, occasionally lymph nodes. This is a compensatory (pathological) response to marrow erythropoietic failure, and together with extramedullary blood destruction it produces the hepatosplenomegaly of thalassaemia; expanding marrow in the skull, ribs, vertebrae and facial bones causes the bony changes.

Ref: V1-ch16

10 Mention the common clinical features of thalassemia major patients. Why reticulocytosis occur in thalassaemia?

Common clinical features of thalassaemia major:

  • Symptoms: progressive pallor, fatigue, weakness and lassitude; mild to moderate jaundice; bleeding manifestations and infections from hypersplenism; failure to thrive, anorexia and diarrhoea; bone pain. Symptoms appear at about 6 months of age, when Hb-F is replaced by Hb-A.
  • Clinical triad: anaemia + jaundice + hepatosplenomegaly (abdominal swelling and discomfort).
  • Bony changes: marrow expansion causing thalassaemic (mongoloid) facies, frontal bossing, 'hair-on-end' skull on X-ray, thinning of cortices and pathological fractures; growth retardation.
  • Complications: infections (pneumococcal, Haemophilus, meningococcal, Yersinia, hepatitis B/C, HIV); iron overload from transfusion → cardiomyopathy, cirrhosis, diabetes, hypothyroidism and hypoparathyroidism; osteoporosis, epistaxis, skin pigmentation, leg ulcers and gallstones.

Why reticulocytosis occurs: chronic peripheral haemolysis shortens red-cell survival, and the resulting hypoxia stimulates erythropoietin-driven marrow erythropoiesis; immature red cells are therefore released prematurely, so the reticulocyte count rises (10% or more) with nucleated red cells in the film — an appropriate marrow response to haemolysis.

Ref: V1-ch16

11 Give the pathogenesis of splenomegaly in thalassaemia patient. How it is treated?

Pathogenesis of splenomegaly:

  • Compensatory extramedullary haemopoiesis — in response to severe anaemia the spleen (and liver) resumes red-cell production and enlarges (major mechanism).
  • Extramedullary blood destruction and hypersplenism — the enlarged spleen sequesters and destroys the rigid, abnormal thalassaemic red cells, which deepens the anaemia and further enlarges the spleen.
  • Haemosiderosis / haemochromatosis — repeated transfusion and increased intestinal iron absorption deposit iron in the spleen and liver, adding to organomegaly.

Treatment:

  1. Regular long-term blood transfusion — the mainstay, given to maintain Hb and suppress ineffective erythropoiesis.
  2. General management: avoid iron-containing foods; folic acid 5 mg daily to prevent folate deficiency; zinc supplementation to prevent growth failure; good nutrition and infection prophylaxis.
  3. Iron chelation (e.g. desferrioxamine) to remove excess transfusion iron and prevent cardiac and hepatic damage.
  4. Splenectomy — for hypersplenism, rising transfusion requirement or massive splenomegaly.
  5. Bone marrow transplantation (curative in eligible patients), induction of γ-chain synthesis (e.g. hydroxyurea) and gene therapy.

Ref: V1-ch16

ITEM 26 — Hemorrhagic Disorders
1 Define hemostasis. What are the steps in hemostasis? What may be the causes of hemorrhagic disorder?

Definition: Haemostasis is the physiological process that arrests bleeding at the site of vascular injury while keeping the blood fluid elsewhere. A haemorrhagic (bleeding) disorder is any condition in which an abnormal tendency to bleed results from a defect in the mechanism of haemostasis; clinically it presents as spontaneous bleeding from skin, mucous membranes and internal tissues, excessive bleeding after trauma/surgery, and bleeding from more than one site.

Steps:

  1. Vasoconstriction — immediate neural and chemical response; damaged endothelium releases endothelin while NO and prostacyclin (vasodilators) fall.
  2. Primary haemostasis — platelet adhesion, activation and aggregation to form a platelet plug.
  3. Secondary haemostasis — coagulation cascade generates thrombin, which converts fibrinogen to fibrin; the mesh is cross-linked to stabilise the plug.
  4. Fibrinolysis — plasmin lyses the clot as repair proceeds, restoring vessel patency.

Causes of haemorrhagic disorder: a defect in any of the three components — vascular (hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia, Ehlers-Danlos, senile/simple purpura, scurvy, uraemia, vasculitis, drugs), platelet (thrombocytopenia — ITP, leukaemia, aplastic anaemia, hypersplenism, DIC; qualitative defects — thrombasthenia, vWD, aspirin), and coagulation (haemophilia A/B, vWD, vitamin K deficiency, liver disease, DIC, anticoagulants, massive transfusion).

FeaturePrimary haemostatic defectSecondary haemostatic (coagulation) defect
Site of defectPlatelets, vWF or vessel wallClotting factors / coagulation cascade
Bleeding patternSuperficial — petechiae, purpura, ecchymoses, epistaxis, gum and menorrhagiaDeep — haemarthrosis, muscle haematoma, delayed post-operative re-bleeding
OnsetImmediate after injuryDelayed, hours after injury
Typical disordersITP, thrombosthenia, vWD, vascular purpuraHaemophilia A/B, vitamin K deficiency, liver disease, DIC
Screening testsProlonged BT, normal CTNormal BT, prolonged CT/APTT

Ref: V1-ch20

2 How platelet plug is formed in primary hemostasis? What is bleeding time? How bleeding time reprensents the efficiency of primary hemostasis?

Platelet plug formation:

  1. Vasoconstriction — endothelin narrows the injured vessel (NO and prostacyclin fall).
  2. Adhesion — vWF acts as glue, binding exposed subendothelial collagen and the platelet receptor GPIb.
  3. Activation — adherent platelets change shape and release thromboxane A2 (COX product), alpha granules (fibrinogen, vWF) and dense granules (ADP, serotonin, Ca2+); ADP drives expression of GPIIb/IIIa and further degranulation.
  4. Aggregation — fibrinogen bridges GPIIb/IIIa receptors of adjacent platelets, forming a weak plug that needs secondary haemostasis to stabilise.

Bleeding time (BT): the time required for bleeding to cease after a standardised skin puncture. Normal value: less than 6 minutes (Ivy's or template method; Duke's capillary method 1-3 min). BT depends on platelet count, platelet function and integrity of the vessel wall.

How BT represents primary haemostasis: BT rises when platelets are too few (thrombocytopenia), dysfunctional (thrombasthenia, Bernard-Soulier, aspirin, vWD) or the vessel wall is abnormal (scurvy, Ehlers-Danlos); it stays normal in isolated factor defects such as haemophilia. Thus BT is the screening test of primary haemostasis — platelet function is assessed by bleeding time.

Ref: V1-ch20

3 How fibrin is formed in secondary hemostasis? What is clotting time? How clotting time represents the efficiency of secondary hemostasis?

Fibrin formation (secondary haemostasis): the intrinsic pathway starts with factor XII activated by collagen (measured by APTT) and the extrinsic pathway with factor VII activated by tissue thromboplastin/tissue factor (measured by PT); both converge on the common pathway. Factor Xa, with factor Va, platelet phospholipid and Ca2+, converts prothrombin (II) to thrombin (IIa). Thrombin converts fibrinogen (I) to fibrin monomers, which polymerise and are covalently cross-linked by factor XIIIa into a stable mesh that traps platelets and red cells — a clot that stabilises the primary plug.

Clotting time (CT): the time required for coagulation of freshly shed blood. Normal value: 6-12 minutes. Methods: Lee & White test-tube method and capillary tube method.

How CT represents secondary haemostasis: CT prolongs only when clotting factors are deficient or consumed — prolonged in haemophilia A (factor VIII), Christmas disease (factor IX), vWD (low functional factor VIII), DIC, vitamin K deficiency and heparin therapy; it is normal in thrombocytopenia/ITP and vessel-wall disorders (these prolong BT instead). Hence CT screens the coagulation cascade.

Ref: V1-ch20

4 What are the diseases effecting primary hemostasis? What type of abnormal bleeding occur in these cases?

Diseases affecting primary haemostasis:

  • Quantitative (thrombocytopenia): ITP, acute leukaemia, aplastic anaemia, marrow infiltration, hypersplenism, drugs, DIC, dengue, massive transfusion.
  • Qualitative (thrombosthenia): Glanzmann thrombasthenia, Bernard-Soulier syndrome, storage-pool disease, aspirin/NSAID therapy, uraemia, myeloproliferative disorders.
  • von Willebrand disease — defective platelet adhesion plus low factor VIII.
  • Vascular defects: senile/simple purpura, scurvy, Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia, Henoch-Schonlein purpura, polyarteritis nodosa, drug purpura.

Type of abnormal bleeding: immediate, superficial mucocutaneous bleeding — pinpoint petechiae (non-palpable), purpura (over 3 mm), ecchymoses and easy bruising, epistaxis, gum bleeding, menorrhagia, haematuria, GI bleeding and haemoptysis; BT is prolonged while CT is normal. Deep haemarthrosis and large haematomas are not typical. Intracranial haemorrhage is the lethal complication of severe thrombocytopenia.

Ref: V1-ch20

5 What is thrombocytopenia? List the causes of thrombocytopenia.

Definition: Thrombocytopenia is a reduction of the peripheral blood platelet count below the lower normal limit of 1.5 lakh/mm3 (150 x 10^9/L). Normal count is 1.5-4 lakh/mm3; below 50,000/mm3 bleeding time prolongs and below 20,000/mm3 spontaneous petechiae appear.

Causes:

  1. Decreased production: aplastic anaemia, megaloblastic macrocytic anaemia, leukaemias, bone-marrow infiltration (secondary carcinoma, multiple myeloma, malignant lymphoma, myelofibrosis), drugs/chemicals (cytotoxic drugs, chloramphenicol, quinidine, quinine), alcohol, infections including HIV and dengue.
  2. Increased destruction/consumption: ITP (acute and chronic), DIC, disseminated SLE, drug-induced immune thrombocytopenia, post-transfusion purpura, thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura.
  3. Sequestration/dilution: hypersplenism, liver disease, massive blood transfusion.
  4. Congenital/neonatal: autoimmune and iso-immune (neonatal), congenital infections, maternal drug administration, congenital megakaryocytic hypoplasia, hereditary sex-linked disorders, congenital leukaemia, giant haemangioma (Kasabach-Merritt).

Ref: V1-ch20

6 What is ITP? What are the types? Give the pathogenesis.

Definition: Idiopathic (immune) thrombocytopenic purpura is an autoimmune disease characterised by isolated thrombocytopenia, in almost all cases due to anti-platelet antibody formation. Synonyms: purpura haemorrhagica, Werlhof's disease, autoimmune thrombocytopenia, primary/essential thrombocytopenic purpura.

Types: two clinical types are recognised.

FeatureAcute self-limiting typeChronic type
AgeChildren 2-6 yearsYoung to middle-aged adults (20-30 years)
SexEitherPredominant in females
OnsetAcute, often after viral infectionInsidious, months to years
Platelet countBelow 20,000/mm3Above 20,000/mm3
CourseSpontaneous remission over 2-4 weeks in >80%Persistent; remission in <20%

Pathogenesis: IgG autoantibodies (platelet-associated immunoglobulin, PLA-IgG) are formed against platelet membrane glycoprotein antigens, usually GPIIb/IIIa or GPIb-IX. Antibody-coated (opsonised) platelets are phagocytosed by mononuclear phagocytic cells, mainly in the spleen, so destruction exceeds production and thrombocytopenia results. It is more common in females of reproductive age and aggravated in pregnancy, because oestrogen up-regulates Fc receptors on phagocytes; PLA-IgG crosses the placenta and causes neonatal thrombocytopenia.

Ref: V1-ch20

7 Give the lab diagnosis of ITP including bone marrow findings.

Routine blood test: Hb usually reduced (with menorrhagia), ESR normal, WBC normal or moderately increased.

Peripheral blood film: RBCs normocytic normochromic; WBC normal; platelets reduced with large, small or atypical (giant) forms; no blasts — argues against leukaemia.

Screening tests: BT prolonged (up to 30 minutes or more), CT normal, platelet count reduced (below 1 lakh/mm3), tourniquet (Hess) test positive in most cases, PT/APTT/thrombin time normal.

Bone marrow examination: hypercellular marrow; increased M/E ratio; erythropoiesis normal; granulopoiesis active and maturing to segmented forms; megakaryopoiesis increased with immature, dysplastic (abnormally sized and shaped) megakaryocytes. A hypocellular marrow suggests aplastic anaemia and blasts suggest leukaemia — the main differential diagnoses, with marrow infiltration by secondary carcinoma or lymphoma.

Confirmatory tests: bone marrow examination showing increased megakaryopoiesis, and anti-platelet antibody detection (positive in about 70% of cases).

Ref: V1-ch20

8 What is thrombosthenia? What are the casuses? How platelet disorders are evaluated in laboratory?

Definition: Thrombosthenia (qualitative platelet disorder) means defective platelet function — "bad platelets" of normal number — so adhesion, activation or aggregation fails and the primary plug is unstable. (Quantitative disorders, by contrast, are normal platelets in too few numbers and produce petechiae.)

Causes:

  • Hereditary: Glanzmann thrombasthenia (GPIIb/IIIa defect), Bernard-Soulier syndrome (GPIb defect, giant platelets), storage-pool/secretion defects.
  • Acquired: aspirin and NSAIDs (block COX/thromboxane A2), uraemia, viraemia, hypergammaglobulinaemia, myeloproliferative and myelodysplastic disorders, cardiopulmonary bypass, alcohol.

Laboratory evaluation: (1) bleeding time — prolonged despite normal platelet count; (2) platelet count and morphology on the blood film (giant platelets, granule defects); (3) tourniquet/capillary fragility test; (4) platelet function tests — in-vitro platelet aggregation with ADP, collagen and adrenaline, clot retraction, platelet factor 3 availability, thromboelastography; (5) second-line tests — vWF assay (vWD), specific factor assays to exclude mild factor deficiency.

Ref: V1-ch20

9 What may be the causes of prolonged bleeding time (BT)? What should be done with a patient with prolonged BT but normal CT?

Causes of prolonged BT:

  1. Thrombocytopenia — ITP, leukaemia, aplastic anaemia, hypersplenism, DIC, drugs, massive transfusion.
  2. Qualitative platelet defects — thrombasthenia, Bernard-Soulier syndrome, aspirin/NSAIDs, storage-pool disease.
  3. von Willebrand disease (defective platelet adhesion).
  4. Vascular defects — scurvy (vitamin C deficiency), Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, senile/simple purpura.
  5. DIC (consumes platelets and factors).

What to do with prolonged BT but normal CT: the coagulation cascade is intact, so the defect is primary haemostasis. Take a bleeding/drug history and examine for petechiae; repeat BT and do a platelet count with film (low count = thrombocytopenia — send for bone marrow, which is confirmatory: increased megakaryopoiesis in ITP, blasts in leukaemia, hypocellularity in aplastic anaemia); if the count is normal, do platelet function/aggregation studies and a vWF assay (vWD is the commonest cause of prolonged BT with a normal count), plus tourniquet test and vitamin C assessment for vascular causes.

FeatureProlonged BT, normal CTProlonged CT, normal BT
DefectPrimary haemostasis (platelet/vessel wall)Secondary haemostasis (clotting factors)
CausesThrombocytopenia, thrombosthenia, vWD, scurvy, Ehlers-DanlosHaemophilia A/B, vitamin K deficiency, liver disease, DIC, heparin
First testsPlatelet count + film, tourniquet test, platelet aggregation, vWF assayAPTT, PT, TT, mixing study, factor VIII/IX assay
BleedingMucocutaneous (petechiae, epistaxis, menorrhagia)Deep (haemarthrosis, haematoma), delayed re-bleeding
High yield: BT = platelets + vessel wall; CT/APTT/PT = clotting factors. BT and CT both prolonged points to von Willebrand disease or DIC — and a normal platelet count with a prolonged BT is the classic vWD picture.

Ref: V1-ch20

10 What may be the causes of prolonged Clotting time (CT)? What should be done with a patient with prolonged CT but normal BT?

Causes of prolonged CT (clotting-factor deficiency or inhibition):

  1. Haemophilia A — factor VIII deficiency.
  2. Christmas disease (haemophilia B) — factor IX deficiency.
  3. von Willebrand disease — low functional factor VIII.
  4. DIC — consumption of clotting factors.
  5. Vitamin K deficiency and liver disease (factors II, VII, IX, X).
  6. Heparin/anticoagulant therapy, circulating inhibitors, massive blood transfusion.

What to do with prolonged CT but normal BT: platelets and vessel wall are normal, so investigate the intrinsic and common pathways:

  1. APTT — prolonged in intrinsic-pathway defects (factors I, II, V, VIII, IX, X, XI, XII); PT — prolonged in extrinsic defects (factors I, II, V, VII, X — liver disease, vitamin K deficiency, warfarin); TT — prolonged in DIC, heparin or fibrinogen deficiency.
  2. Peripheral smear and platelet count — normal, excluding thrombocytopenia; BT and tourniquet test normal.
  3. Mixing study with normal plasma — correction indicates factor deficiency; failure to correct indicates an inhibitor.
  4. Confirm by specific factor assay — factor VIII (haemophilia A), factor IX (haemophilia B), vWF assay (vWD); in DIC add FDP/D-dimer and fibrinogen.

Ref: V1-ch20

11 Name the coagulation disorders. What is Von Willibrand disease?

Coagulation disorders (deficiency of clotting factors, inhibition of coagulation, or excessive fibrinolytic activity):

  • Congenital: haemophilia A (classical), haemophilia B (Christmas disease), von Willebrand disease, and deficiencies of factors I, II, V, VII, X, XI, XII, XIII, prekallikrein and high-molecular-weight kininogen.
  • Acquired: DIC, vitamin K deficiency, liver disease, anticoagulant drugs, acute primary fibrinolysis, massive blood transfusion, circulating inhibitors of coagulation.

Von Willebrand disease (vWD): the most common hereditary bleeding disorder — an autosomal disorder caused by deficiency or dysfunction of von Willebrand factor, a large multimeric plasma protein synthesised by endothelial cells and megakaryocytes. vWF has two roles: it promotes platelet adhesion to subendothelium, and it is the carrier protein that protects factor VIII from rapid removal and degradation.

Types: Type I — mild quantitative defect; Type II — qualitative defect of intermediate severity; Type III — virtually no vWF (severe, autosomal recessive). Most cases are autosomal dominant.

Defect and tests: dual haemostatic defect — failed platelet adhesion causes mucocutaneous bleeding with prolonged BT, and functional factor VIII deficiency causes prolonged CT/APTT; platelet count and PT are normal, TT normal. Confirm by vWF assay.

Ref: V1-ch20

12 Give the Pathogenesis and lab diagnosis of hemophilia.

Definition: Haemophilia is an inherited X-linked recessive coagulation disorder in which bleeding time is normal but clotting time is greatly prolonged. The defective gene lies on the X chromosome: males suffer, females are carriers; there is no male-to-male transmission, an affected male passes the gene to all his daughters (carriers), and a carrier female transmits to half her sons.

Pathogenesis: deficiency of factor VIII or IX cripples the intrinsic pathway, so factor Xa and the thrombin burst are inadequate; fibrin mesh forms poorly and the platelet plug remains unstable — hence bleeding after trauma or surgery (tooth extraction, circumcision) and spontaneous deep bleeding, classically haemarthrosis (knee, elbow, ankle), muscle haematoma, prolonged wound bleeding and excessive bruising. Repeated haemarthroses lead to chronic haemophilic arthritis.

Types:

FeatureHaemophilia AHaemophilia B
Also calledClassical/true haemophiliaChristmas disease
Deficient factorFactor VIII (antihemophilic factor)Factor IX (plasma thromboplastin component)
Frequency7 times more common, ~80% of casesRare
InheritanceX-linked recessive (both)

Lab diagnosis: CT greatly prolonged and APTT prolonged; BT, PT, thrombin time, platelet count and platelet morphology all normal; tourniquet test normal. Confirm by factor VIII assay (reduced in A) or factor IX assay (reduced in B).

Ref: V1-ch20

13 What is DIC? Give its pathogenesis and lab diagnosis.

Definition: Disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC) is a haemorrhagic disorder in which diffuse intravascular clotting causes a haemostatic defect from utilisation (consumption) of coagulation factors and platelets in the clotting process. Synonyms: consumption coagulopathy, defibrination syndrome.

Pathogenesis: the key event is increased circulating thrombin activity that overwhelms natural anticoagulants. Triggers: entry of procoagulant material (obstetric accidents — abruptio placentae, amniotic fluid embolism, abortion; trauma; mucin-secreting adenocarcinoma; acute promyelocytic leukaemia; snake bite; haemolytic transfusion reaction) or widespread endothelial damage (Gram-negative and meningococcal septicaemia, severe burns, virus infections). Widespread activation produces microthrombi, consumes platelets and factors I, II, V, VII, X, and stimulates fibrinolysis — FDPs (D-dimer) interfere with fibrin polymerisation. The result is a vicious circle: haemorrhage, organ dysfunction (lung, kidney, liver, brain), and microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia from fibrin strands fragmenting red cells.

Lab diagnosis: whole-blood CT greatly prolonged; BT increased; PT, APTT and TT prolonged; platelet count reduced; factor assay reduced; fibrinogen decreased; FDP/D-dimer increased in serum and urine; blood film shows fragmented red cells (microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia).

High yield: in DIC everything is abnormal in one direction — BT, CT, PT, APTT and TT all prolonged, FDP up — EXCEPT platelet count, fibrinogen and coagulation factors, which are all DOWN (consumed). D-dimer is the confirmatory test.

Ref: V1-ch20

ITEM 27 — WBC Disorders and Leukaemia
THEORY
1 List the causes of leukocytosis. What is leukaemoid reaction?

Leukocytosis: Increase in the total number of WBC above 11,000/mm³ (neutrophilia = neutrophils above 7,500/mm³).

Causes of leukocytosis:

  • Physiological: exercise, newborn, pregnancy and parturition, ingestion of food, emotional stress, slight afternoon (diurnal) rise.
  • Pathological: acute pyogenic bacterial infection (most common — abscess, acute appendicitis, tonsillitis, bacterial pneumonia, pyelonephritis); non-infectious inflammation (rheumatic fever, collagen disorders, gout, acute glomerulonephritis); tissue injury (myocardial infarction, burns, surgery); haemorrhage; stress (labour, severe colic, convulsions); diabetic ketoacidosis; malignant neoplasm; high-dose corticosteroids; chronic myeloid leukaemia; polycythaemia.
  • Line-specific rises: lymphocytosis (whooping cough, typhoid, TB, viral hepatitis, infectious mononucleosis, CLL), eosinophilia (parasites, allergy) and monocytosis (chronic bacterial infections) also raise the total count.

Leukaemoid reaction: A reactive peripheral blood picture resembling leukaemia — marked leucocytosis with immature white cells, or both — in a subject who does not have leukaemia. It may be myeloid (pneumonia, meningitis, septicaemia, miliary tuberculosis, secondary non-haematological malignancy, acute haemolysis) or lymphatic (infectious mononucleosis, cytomegalovirus infection, pertussis, measles, chickenpox, carcinoma, miliary TB).

Ref: V1-ch17

2 What are the causes of neutrophilic leukocytosis? What are difference between leukaemia and leukaemoid reaction?

Causes of neutrophilic leukocytosis (neutrophilia): neutrophil count above 7,500/mm³.

  • Physiological: exercise, newborn, pregnancy and parturition, ingestion of food, emotional stress, slight afternoon diurnal variation.
  • Pathological: acute pyogenic bacterial infection (most common cause, e.g. S. aureus, S. pyogenes, S. pneumoniae, E. coli, Proteus, Pseudomonas); non-infectious inflammatory disorders (rheumatic fever, collagen disorders, gout, glomerulonephritis); tissue injury (infarction, burns, surgery); haemorrhage; stress (labour, colic, convulsions); diabetic ketoacidosis; malignant neoplasm; high-dose corticosteroids; chronic myeloid leukaemia; polycythaemia.
FeatureLeukaemoid reactionLeukaemia
Clinical featuresFeatures of the causative (reactive) disorderSplenomegaly, lymph node enlargement and haemorrhage more common
Total WBC countMostly below 1,00,000/mm³Can exceed 1,00,000/mm³
Immature cellsLess than 10% in most casesUsually numerous
Cell morphologyToxic changes (granulation, vacuoles) in infective cases; no Auer rodsAtypical, immature cells; toxic changes uncommon
AnaemiaSlight or absentPresent and progressive
PlateletsNormal or increasedDecreased (except increased/normal in chronic phase of CGL)
Bone marrowWhite cell hyperplasia, seldom of the degree seen in leukaemiaHyperplastic with a large number of immature cells
Organ infiltrationAbsent at autopsyLeukaemic infiltration of organs and tissues

Ref: V1-ch17

3 What is eosinophilia? What are the common causes? Draw a diagram of eosinophil.

Eosinophilia: Increase in the number of eosinophils above the upper limit of the absolute count — more than 700 cells/mm³ (or more than 500 cells/mm³ by another convention). Normal relative count is 1–6% (40–400/mm³).

Causes of eosinophilia:

  • Parasitic infections: hookworm, ascariasis, tapeworm, hydatid, filariasis, schistosomiasis, trichinosis, amoebiasis, scabies.
  • Allergic reactions: hay fever, asthma, urticaria, atopic dermatitis, angioneurotic oedema; allergy to drugs, blood products, other extrinsic agents.
  • Skin diseases: psoriasis, pemphigus, dermatitis herpetiformis.
  • Others: Loeffler's syndrome and other pulmonary infiltrates, neoplasia (AML, CML, Hodgkin disease, carcinoma), polyarteritis nodosa, recovery from acute infection, post-splenectomy, hypereosinophilic syndrome, familial, graft-versus-host disease.

Diagram of the eosinophil (labelled description):

  • Size: 12–15 µm, larger than a neutrophil; round/oval cell.
  • Nucleus: bilobed, the two lobes joined by a thin chromatin thread ("spectacle" or pince-nez nucleus), dark purple.
  • Cytoplasm: pale, colourless background.
  • Granules: numerous large, uniform, spherical, bright orange-red (eosinophilic) granules that take the acidic eosin stain; contain major basic protein, eosinophil peroxidase and histaminase.
  • Functions: killing of parasites and modulation of allergic reactions.

Ref: V1-ch17

4 What are causes of leukopenia? What are risks of leukopenia?

Leukopenia: Reduction of the total count of WBC below 4,000/mm³; neutropenia = absolute neutrophil count below 1,500/mm³. Agranulocytosis means complete or almost complete absence of neutrophils in the peripheral blood.

Causes of leukopenia / neutropenia:

  • Infections: viral (viral hepatitis, dengue, influenza, measles, atypical pneumonia), bacterial (typhoid and paratyphoid), protozoal (chronic malaria, kala-azar).
  • Bone marrow failure: aplastic anaemia, megaloblastic anaemia, leukaemic/malignant marrow infiltration.
  • Hypersplenism (splenic sequestration).
  • Immune: systemic lupus erythematosus, autoimmune neutropenia.
  • Drugs: analgesics, anti-inflammatory agents, antibiotics, antimalarials, cytotoxic drugs (drug idiosyncrasy is the commonest cause of agranulocytosis — aminopyrine, phenothiazines, carbimazole, sulphonamides, dapsone).
  • Excessive irradiation and miscellaneous causes.

Risks of leukopenia:

  • Markedly increased susceptibility to infection, especially bacterial, with the risk rising sharply when the absolute neutrophil count falls below 500/mm³ — septicaemia, pneumonia and urinary infection are the leading causes of death.
  • Necrotic/ulcerating lesions of the mouth, pharynx and gut mucosa with sore throat, high-grade fever and prostration (agranulocytosis).
  • Opportunistic fungal, viral and Pneumocystis infections, poor wound healing and post-operative sepsis; bleeding occurs only if platelets are also reduced.

Ref: V1-ch17

5 What is leukaemia? Classify it. Why infection is common in leukaemia patient despite of high leukocyte count?

Leukaemia: A neoplastic disorder of haemopoietic stem/precursor cells in which the marrow is progressively replaced by neoplastic cells that appear in the blood and infiltrate the liver, spleen, lymph nodes and other lymphatic tissues; the cells are usually myeloid or lymphoid in type.

Classification:

  1. By clinical course: acute leukaemia (blast cells more than 30% of marrow) and chronic leukaemia (slowly progressive, well-differentiated cells).
  2. By cell line involved: myeloid leukaemia and lymphoid leukaemia.
  3. FAB classification of acute leukaemia: AML — M0 undifferentiated, M1 without maturation, M2 with maturation, M3 hypergranular promyelocytic, M4 myelomonocytic, M5 monocytic, M6 erythroleukaemia, M7 megakaryoblastic; ALL — L1 small homogeneous lymphoblast, L2 heterogeneous lymphoblast, L3 large homogeneous lymphoblast.
  4. Chronic/other: CML, CLL, prolymphocytic and hairy cell leukaemia; related groups are myelodysplastic syndromes, myeloproliferative disorders and plasma cell disorders.

Why infection is common despite a high WBC count: The raised cells are leukaemic — immature and functionally incompetent; they cannot perform chemotaxis, phagocytosis and intracellular killing. At the same time the marrow is packed with blasts, so normal mature neutrophils, lymphocytes and antibody production all fall. The patient is therefore functionally neutropenic and immunodeficient, so bacterial and fungal infection develops despite the high count.

Ref: V1-ch17

6 Give the lab diagnosis of AML.

Blood picture: Hb reduced (3–9 g/dl) and PCV reduced; reticulocytes up to 5%; thrombocytopenia.

Peripheral blood film:

  • WBC: count rises (20,000–1,00,000/mm³); immature blast cells 80–90%; Auer bodies (red, spindle-shaped inclusions, fused azurophil granules, "faggots" when multiple) present in myeloblasts; smear cells absent; cytoplasm more abundant; nucleoli 2–6; loose nuclear chromatin.
  • RBC: anaemia, normocytic normochromic with anisocytosis, poikilocytosis, mild polychromasia, occasional nucleated red cells.
  • Platelets: thrombocytopenia.

Bone marrow: hypercellular; myeloid:erythroid ratio increased; erythropoiesis and megakaryopoiesis depressed; leukopoiesis increased with immature blast cells 30–95% (diagnostic threshold more than 30%).

Cytochemical stains: myeloperoxidase (MPO) positive, Sudan Black B positive, non-specific esterase positive (Auer rods strongly MPO positive).

Immunophenotyping (flow cytometry): CD13, CD33 (and MPO, CD117); CD10 absent — distinguishes AML from ALL.

Cytogenetics: chromosomal abnormality may be found — e.g. t(15;17) in M3, t(8;21) in M2, inv(16) in M4eo; retinoic acid receptor abnormality in M3 (basis of ATRA therapy).

Ref: V1-ch17

7 Define acute leukaemia. Give the clinical features and lab diagnosis of acute lymphoblastic leukaemia.

Acute leukaemia: A malignant disorder in which marrow precursors are blocked at an early step of differentiation, so immature blasts (myeloblasts or lymphoblasts) accumulate in the marrow, spill into the blood and infiltrate tissues. It is defined as more than 30% blasts in the marrow; rapidly fatal if untreated but potentially curable.

Clinical features:

  • Common: anaemia; fever and malaise (infection from lack of mature WBC); haemorrhage, bruising, petechiae (thrombocytopenia).
  • Less common: mouth and pharyngeal infection, bone and joint pain, upper respiratory infection, superficial lymph node enlargement (children).
  • Occasional: abdominal pain, mediastinal obstruction, nervous system involvement, skin rash, gum hypertrophy.

Lab diagnosis of ALL: Hb and PCV reduced; thrombocytopenia; leucocytosis with lymphoblasts larger than mature lymphocytes, no Auer bodies, smear cells present, scanty cytoplasm, 0–2 nucleoli, dense chromatin. Marrow hypercellular, M:E increased, erythropoiesis and megakaryopoiesis depressed, blasts 30–95%. Cytochemistry: PAS and focal acid phosphatase (T-cell) positive, MPO and Sudan Black B negative. Immunophenotyping: B-cell CD10, CD19/CD20; T-cell CD2–CD7.

FeatureAMLALL
Usual ageAdults (median 65 years)Children under 15 years
Predominant cellMyeloblasts / promyelocytesLymphoblasts
Auer rod / smear cellsAuer rods present; smear cells absentAuer rods absent; smear cells present
Cytochemistry, CD10, prognosisMPO and Sudan Black B positive; CD10 absent; poorPAS and acid phosphatase positive; CD10 present; good

Ref: V1-ch17

8 Give the lab diagnosis of CML. What is Philadelphia chromosome?

Lab diagnosis of CML (CGL):

  • Blood picture: Hb moderately reduced (8–10 g/dl); thrombocytopenia later, but platelets normal or increased in the chronic phase (only leukaemia with normal/high platelets in chronic phase); reticulocytes moderate.
  • PBF: leucocytosis, may exceed 6 lakhs/mm³; every stage of myeloid cell present but segmented neutrophils and myelocytes predominate — myelocytes 10–50%, myeloblasts up to 10%; neutrophils vary in size (giant and dwarf forms); eosinophils and basophils increased; RBC normocytic normochromic with polychromasia and basophilic stippling; normoblasts occasionally seen.
  • Bone marrow: hypercellular, M:E ratio increased, erythropoiesis depressed, leukopoiesis increased with myelocytes (~50%) predominating and myeloblasts below 10% in the chronic phase; megakaryopoiesis initially increased, later dismegakaryopoiesis.
  • Biochemistry: increased serum vitamin B12, uric acid and LDH; decreased LAP (leukocyte alkaline phosphatase) score.
  • Cytogenetics: Philadelphia chromosome — present in about 90%, diagnostic. Course: 80% die of blast crisis.

Philadelphia chromosome: An abnormal chromosome 22 produced by reciprocal translocation between chromosomes 9 and 22 — t(9;22). The C-abl (Abelson proto-oncogene) of chromosome 9 is transferred to the BCR (breakpoint cluster region) of chromosome 22, forming a BCR–ABL fusion oncogene that codes an abnormal tyrosine kinase. It is seen in about 90% of CML and is important for prognosis.

Ref: V1-ch17

9 What are the myeloprolifeative diseases? Write shortly on myelofibrosis.

Myeloproliferative diseases: Proliferative neoplastic disorders arising from marrow stem cells, due to uncontrolled clonal proliferation of one or more haemopoietic components — erythroid, myeloid and megakaryocytic — and often involving the liver and spleen. The four classic disorders are:

  1. Polycythaemia rubra vera (predominantly erythropoiesis).
  2. Essential thrombocythaemia (predominantly megakaryopoiesis).
  3. Myelofibrosis with myeloid metaplasia (idiopathic/primary myelofibrosis).
  4. Chronic myeloid leukaemia (now regarded as a separate entity).

Myelofibrosis (short note): Progressive generalised reactive fibrosis and collagen formation in the marrow with extramedullary haemopoiesis (myeloid metaplasia) in the spleen and liver, and a leuco-erythroblastic blood picture. Synonyms: idiopathic myelofibrosis, myelosclerosis, agnogenic myeloid metaplasia. Types: primary (developing in polycythaemia vera) and secondary (TB, secondary carcinoma, Hodgkin disease, leukaemia). It affects adults aged 40–70 years and presents with anaemia symptoms (weakness, fatigue, dyspnoea, palpitation), massive splenomegaly and hepatomegaly, weight loss, fever, night sweats, bleeding, bone pain, gout; radiological bone changes in about 30%.

Laboratory: Hb reduced; PBF shows normocytic normochromic RBCs with marked anisopoikilocytosis, characteristic tear-drop cells, nucleated RBCs and a variable WBC/platelet count; marrow is fibrotic and hypercellular (dry tap); spleen enlarged with myeloid metaplasia; serum urate and LDH high.

Ref: V1-ch18

PROBLEM / SCENARIO-BASED QUESTIONS
10 A 5yrs boy presented with marked anaemia, thrombocytopenia and high WBC count. PBF shows many blast cells. What is/are the diagnosis? How will you confirm?

Diagnosis: Acute lymphoblastic leukaemia (ALL) — the commonest acute leukaemia in young children (usually under 15 years), presenting with marrow failure: anaemia (replacement of normal erythropoiesis), thrombocytopenia (bleeding/bruising) and leucocytosis with many lymphoblasts in the film.

How to confirm:

  • PBF: lymphoblasts predominate — larger than mature lymphocytes, scanty cytoplasm, dense chromatin, 0–2 nucleoli, no Auer rods, smear cells present; thrombocytopenia.
  • Bone marrow: hypercellular with more than 30% blasts, M:E ratio increased, erythropoiesis depressed, megakaryopoiesis reduced; this is diagnostic of acute leukaemia.
  • Cytochemistry: PAS positive, focal acid phosphatase positive (T-cell), myeloperoxidase and Sudan Black B negative (excludes AML).
  • Immunophenotyping (flow cytometry): B-cell markers CD19, CD20, CD22 and CD10; T-cell markers CD2, CD3, CD5, CD7 — confirms lymphoid lineage.
  • Cytogenetics: chromosomal abnormalities (e.g. hyperdiploidy, t(12;21)) for prognosis; also blood count, coagulation and CNS assessment.

Ref: V1-ch17

11 A 55 yrs male presented with marked anaemia, thrombocytopenia and high WBC count. PBF shows many blast cells. What is/are the diagnosis? How will you confirm?

Diagnosis: Acute myeloid leukaemia (AML) — in an adult (median onset about 65 years) the commonest acute leukaemia is myeloid. The triad of anaemia, thrombocytopenia and leucocytosis with many blasts reflects marrow replaced by myeloblasts, with failure of normal erythroid and megakaryocytic series.

How to confirm:

  • PBF: myeloblasts 80–90% of WBC, with abundant cytoplasm, loose chromatin, 2–6 nucleoli and Auer rods (red spindle-shaped inclusions, "faggots" when multiple) — confirm myeloid lineage; smear cells absent.
  • Bone marrow: hypercellular, blasts more than 30%, M:E ratio increased, erythropoiesis and megakaryopoiesis depressed.
  • Cytochemistry: myeloperoxidase and Sudan Black B positive, non-specific esterase positive (Auer rods strongly MPO positive).
  • Immunophenotyping: CD13, CD33, CD117 and MPO; CD10 absent (distinguishes from ALL).
  • Cytogenetics: karyotype/FISH — t(15;17) (M3, treats with ATRA), t(8;21), inv(16); molecular studies for prognosis.
High yield: Child + many blasts = ALL; Adult + many blasts = AML until proved otherwise. Auer rod + MPO/Sudan Black B positive = myeloid; PAS + focal acid phosphatase + CD10 = lymphoid.

Ref: V1-ch17

12 A 46 yrs male presented with high WBC count. PBF shows immature WBCs of various stages mostly myelocytes and metamyelocytes along with mature neutrophils. What is/are the diagnosis? How will you confirm?

Diagnosis: Chronic myeloid leukaemia (CML / chronic granulocytic leukaemia) — a middle-aged adult with leucocytosis showing a full spectrum of myeloid maturation (myelocytes and metamyelocytes predominating with mature neutrophils) is the classic picture of CML in the chronic phase; basophilia and eosinophilia are typical associates.

How to confirm:

  • PBF: leucocytosis (may exceed 6 lakhs/mm³), myelocytes 10–50%, myeloblasts up to 10%, increased eosinophils and basophils, giant and dwarf neutrophils; platelets normal or increased in chronic phase; RBC normocytic normochromic with basophilic stippling.
  • Bone marrow: hypercellular, M:E ratio increased, myeloid hyperplasia with myelocytes predominating; megakaryopoiesis increased.
  • Cytogenetics (gold standard): Philadelphia chromosome, t(9;22) / BCR-ABL fusion, present in about 90% of cases.
  • Biochemistry: low LAP (leukocyte alkaline phosphatase) score (low in CML, high in leukaemoid reaction and leucocytosis), raised serum vitamin B12, uric acid and LDH.

Ref: V1-ch17

13 A 46 yrs male presented with high WBC count. PBF shows all mature WBCs with high neutrophil count. What is/are the diagnosis? What may be the causes?

Diagnosis: Neutrophilic leukocytosis (neutrophilia) — a high total WBC with only mature neutrophils and no blast excess means a reactive leucocytosis rather than leukaemia. The leading possibilities are acute pyogenic bacterial infection, a physiological/stress rise, a leukaemoid reaction or, less commonly, CML with a low blast count (to be excluded).

How to confirm: Repeat CBC with absolute count and manual differential; look for toxic granulation, cytoplasmic vacuoles and left shift without blasts (reactive), and check platelets, CRP and culture. If the count keeps rising, or basophils/platelets are high, do marrow examination, LAP score (raised in infection/leukaemoid, low in CML) and Philadelphia chromosome study to exclude CML.

Causes:

  • Physiological: exercise, newborn, pregnancy and parturition, ingestion of food, emotional stress, slight afternoon diurnal variation.
  • Inflammatory/infectious: acute pyogenic bacterial infection (abscess, appendicitis, tonsillitis, pneumonia, pyelonephritis) — the most common cause; non-infectious inflammation (rheumatic fever, collagen disorders, gout, glomerulonephritis).
  • Other tissue/stress causes: infarction (e.g. myocardial), burns, surgery, haemorrhage, labour, severe colic, convulsions, diabetic ketoacidosis.
  • Iatrogenic/neoplastic: high-dose corticosteroids, malignant neoplasm, CML, polycythaemia.

Ref: V1-ch17

14 A young patient of 12yrs done CBC and it shows: TC of WBC: 10,000/cmm and DC: Neutrophil 60%, lymphocyte:25%, Monocytes: 05%, and eosinophil 10%. What is the diagnosis from CBC findings? What may be the causes?

Diagnosis: The total count and differential are essentially within normal limits for a 12-year-old (TC 4,500–13,500/mm³; neutrophil 40–75%, lymphocyte 20–50%, monocyte 2–10%) — the only abnormality is the eosinophil of 10% (normal 1–6%). Absolute eosinophil count = 10% × 10,000 = 1,000 cells/mm³, i.e. eosinophilia (relative eosinophilia with a normal total count). So the CBC shows isolated eosinophilia with normocytosis and no leukaemia.

How to confirm: Calculate the absolute eosinophil count from a repeat CBC (percentage × TC) — above 500–700/mm³ confirms eosinophilia. The film shows normal eosinophils with no blasts, and the normal TC, Hb and platelets exclude leukaemia; then search for the underlying cause.

Causes to look for:

  • Parasitic/helminthic infection (commonest in children): ascariasis, hookworm, tapeworm, filariasis, schistosomiasis, trichinosis, amoebiasis, scabies.
  • Allergic/atopic disease: asthma, hay fever, urticaria, atopic dermatitis, angioneurotic oedema; drug allergy (sulphonamides, antibiotics).
  • Pulmonary: Loeffler's syndrome and other pulmonary eosinophilic infiltrates.
  • Skin diseases: psoriasis, pemphigus, dermatitis herpetiformis.
  • Others: recovery from acute infection, collagen/vasculitic disorders (polyarteritis nodosa), neoplasia (Hodgkin disease, AML, CML), hypereosinophilic syndrome, familial, post-splenectomy, graft-versus-host disease.
High yield: 10% × 10,000 = 1,000/mm³ — absolute eosinophilia despite a normal total count. Next steps: stool for ova and parasites, allergy/drug history, chest X-ray.

Ref: V1-ch17

ITEM 30 — Blood Grouping and Cross Matching
1 What are the common indications of blood transfusion? Name are components of blood used for transfusion?

Indications of blood transfusion:

  • Haemorrhage: acute blood loss; peri-operative bleeding during a major operation; post-operative support in severely debilitated and anaemic patients; pre-operative preparation in severe anaemia.
  • Chronic anaemia: symptomatic anaemia without haemorrhage; deficiency anaemia (iron, vitamin B12, folic acid); thalassaemia; aplastic anaemia.
  • Exchange transfusion: haemolytic disease of the newborn, acute renal failure, acute transfusion reaction.
  • Other: severe burns; ITP and acute leukaemia (platelets); coagulation disorders such as haemophilia; DIC; dengue.

Blood components used for transfusion:

ComponentIndication
Red cell concentrate (also washed, leucocyte-poor, frozen deglycerolized red cells)Acute blood loss, severe anaemia
Platelet concentrate (random donor or single donor)Aplastic anaemia, acute leukaemia, ITP, dengue, DIC
White cells (single-donor granulocytes)Overwhelming bacterial or fungal infection unresponsive to antibiotics
Fresh frozen plasmaDIC, haemophilia (factor VIII / factor IX deficiency), TTP, reversal of warfarin effect
Cryoprecipitate (fibrinogen, clotting factors)DIC, von Willebrand disease, haemophilia A
Factor VIII concentrateHaemophilia A
Albumin / immunoglobulinBlood volume replacement, liver cirrhosis, nephrotic syndrome

Ref: V1-ch21

2 What are the qualities of a good blood donor? Name the conditions when a donor is refused to donate blood.

Qualities of a good blood donor:

  • Healthy adult, age 18–60 years (repeat voluntary donors up to 65 years), body weight at least 45–50 kg, haemoglobin 12 g/dL or above.
  • Afebrile and clinically well, with normal pulse (50–100/min, regular) and normal blood pressure.
  • Voluntary, non-remunerated and repeat donor who gives a truthful health history and donates no more often than once in 3 months (12 weeks).
  • Low-risk life style with no known exposure to transfusion-transmitted infections.

Conditions when a donor is refused (deferred):

Donor eligibleDonor refused / deferred
Age 18–60 years, weight at least 45–50 kgAge below 18 or above 60 years, low body weight
Haemoglobin 12 g/dL or above, no anaemiaAnaemia, recent blood loss, recent donation
Afebrile, well, normal vital signsFever or acute illness, current medication, recent surgery
Healthy history, interval of 3 months since last donationBlood transfusion within 12 months, tattoo / piercing / surgery in the previous 6–12 months, pregnancy or delivery within the last year
All screening tests negativeHBsAg, anti-HCV, HIV or VDRL positive; malaria attack or treatment within 3 years; recent travel to a malaria-endemic area
No high-risk behaviourIntravenous drug use, high-risk sexual behaviour, past jaundice / hepatitis, chronic diseases (insulin-dependent diabetes, epilepsy, heart or kidney disease, cancer)

Ref: V1-ch21

3 What is screening before transfusion? What are the diseases that can transmit during blood transfusion? Name the tests commonly done before blood transfusion.

Screening before transfusion: Testing of every unit of donated blood with a standard panel of serological tests — ABO and Rh typing, antibody screen and tests for transfusion-transmitted infections — so that the transfused blood is both immunologically compatible and free of infective agents. Safe blood transfusion means (a) proper matching of donor and recipient blood with no mismatched reaction and (b) no disease transferred from donor to recipient.

Diseases that can be transmitted during blood transfusion:

  • Bacterial: syphilis (Treponema pallidum); contaminated blood with gram-negative organisms causing endotoxic shock.
  • Viral: hepatitis B, C and D, HIV (AIDS), cytomegalovirus, Epstein-Barr virus.
  • Protozoal: malaria (Plasmodium species), kala-azar (leishmaniasis).

Tests commonly done before blood transfusion:

  1. ABO blood grouping of donor and recipient.
  2. Rh (D) typing of donor and recipient.
  3. HBsAg (hepatitis B surface antigen).
  4. Anti-HCV (hepatitis C antibody).
  5. HIV antigen / antibody.
  6. VDRL (syphilis serology).
  7. MP (malarial parasite) test.
  8. Screening of recipient serum for irregular antibodies and cross matching against donor red cells.
High yield: mandatory screening panel = HBsAg + anti-HCV + HIV + VDRL (syphilis) + MP, plus ABO / Rh typing and cross matching. Even group O (universal donor) blood must be cross matched — non-cross-matched O or Rh-negative blood is not risk-free.

Ref: V1-ch21

4 What are the complications of blood transfusion? How the complications can be prevented?

Complications of blood transfusion and their prevention:

ComplicationFeaturesPrevention
Febrile (pyrogenic) reactionChills, rigor and fever during or shortly after transfusion; common in repeatedly transfused patients (e.g. aplastic anaemia)Leucodepletion with a white-cell reduction filter, use of fresh blood, pyrogen-free disposable equipment, antipyretic pre-medication
Allergic reaction (about 1% of transfusions)Skin rash, hypotension, tachycardia, respiratory distress, rarely anaphylactic shockThorough donor screening, antihistamine pre-medication, washed red cells in susceptible recipients
Haemolytic (mismatched) reactionWithin 1 hour: chest tightness, back / lumbar pain, rigor and pyrexia, hypotension, haemoglobinuria, shock, acute kidney shutdownStrict ABO and Rh typing of donor and recipient, antibody screening and cross matching, accurate labelling, bedside identity check
Circulatory overloadPulmonary congestion and acute heart failure — the most common cause of death following transfusionSlow infusion, small volumes in cardiac, elderly and severely anaemic patients, prior diuresis
Bacterial contamination of stored bloodGram-negative endotoxic shock, septic reactionAseptic collection, storage at 4°C within shelf life (ACD 21 days, CPD 28 days), inspect the bag before use
Transmission of diseaseHepatitis B / C / D, HIV, syphilis, malaria, CMV, EBV, kala-azarMandatory screening of every unit (HBsAg, anti-HCV, HIV, VDRL, MP)
Massive transfusion complicationsHypothermia, hyperkalaemia, citrate toxicity / hypocalcaemia, coagulopathy, metabolic acidosis, cardiac arrhythmiaWarm and slowly infused blood, monitor electrolytes and blood gases, give calcium gluconate, avoid over-transfusion
Delayed complicationsTransfusion haemosiderosis (iron overload), post-transfusional purpura, delayed haemolytic reaction, sensitization, graft-versus-host reaction, thrombophlebitis, air embolismComponent therapy instead of whole blood, iron chelation, irradiated blood for at-risk recipients, single-use giving sets
High yield: most common reaction = febrile (non-haemolytic); most common cause of death after transfusion = circulatory overload; most dangerous = acute haemolytic reaction from ABO mismatch.

Ref: V1-ch21

5 A thalassemia patient, who receive regular blood transfusion every month from various sources developed hepatitis recently. Investigation revealed he is hepatitis C positive. What may be the causes? How it could be prevented?

Cause: This is transfusion-transmitted hepatitis C infection. A chronically transfused thalassaemia patient receives blood from many different donors over the years; if any unit came from an unscreened source, or from a donor in the antibody-negative "window period" of HCV infection, the virus is passed parenterally to the recipient. Repeated exposures from various sources compound the cumulative risk, which is why multiply transfused thalassaemia is a classic cause of post-transfusion HCV positivity. Non-transfusion sources (intravenous drug use, tattooing / piercing, dialysis, vertical transmission) should be excluded by history.

Prevention:

  • Test every donated unit for anti-HCV (along with HBsAg, HIV, VDRL and MP) before release; nucleic acid testing (PCR) where available further shortens the window period.
  • Strict donor selection and deferral of at-risk donors; use of low-risk, repeat voluntary donors.
  • Use only single-use sterile disposable blood bags, needles and giving sets — never re-use equipment.
  • Reduce total exposure: give packed cells or specific components rather than repeated whole blood, minimise the number of different donors, and use iron chelation so transfusion frequency can be lowered.
  • Maintain the cold chain and aseptic technique throughout storage and administration.

Ref: V1-ch21

6 A young female while recieving blood transfusion developed chest chills, rigor and chest discomfort. What is the cause/causes? How it could be prevented?

Cause: Rigor, chills and chest discomfort arising during transfusion indicate an acute transfusion reaction. The most likely cause is a febrile (pyrogenic) non-haemolytic transfusion reaction — caused by recipient antibodies against donor leucocyte / HLA antigens or by pyrogens and cytokines in stored blood — which produces chills, rigor and fever during or shortly after transfusion and is typical in patients receiving repeated transfusions. Acute haemolytic (ABO-incompatible) reaction must be excluded urgently, because it also causes chest tightness, rigor and pyrexia within the first hour, with back pain, hypotension, haemoglobinuria and shock. Allergic reaction (rash, hypotension, respiratory distress) and bacterial contamination of the unit are other possibilities.

Prevention:

  • Stop the transfusion immediately if a reaction is suspected, keep the line open with normal saline, and re-check patient identity, grouping and cross match.
  • Careful ABO and Rh typing of donor and recipient, antibody screen and cross matching before transfusion, with accurate labelling and a bedside identity check.
  • Leucodepletion (white-cell reduction filter) or use of fresh blood to prevent febrile reactions; pre-medication with an antipyretic / antihistamine in multiply transfused patients.
  • Pyrogen-free single-use equipment, aseptic technique, blood warmed to 37°C when transfused rapidly, and a slow initial rate of infusion.

Ref: V1-ch21
